########## Felicia Hardy (Black Cat) VOICE #########

Communications & Linguistic Profile

# UATU DOSSIER — FELICIA HARDY / BLACK CAT
## Section 6: Communications & Linguistic Profile

---

### 6.1 Baseline Voice

The subject's natural speaking voice — across forty years of canon and every writer who has handled her — sits in a narrow, identifiable band: **mid-tempo, low-affect, contralto-warm, smiling-through-the-line**. She rarely raises her voice. She rarely speaks quickly. The default delivery is the half-turned-away aside — a remark addressed nominally to the room but engineered to land on one specific person, who is meant to feel singled out by the privacy of it. She speaks to be overheard *by the right ear only*.

Sentence length is short to medium. She avoids the long-clause sentence; her grammar is tight, declarative, and almost entirely free of hedges ("I think," "maybe," "sort of" appear only when she is deliberately performing softness). The absence of hedging is a tell in itself — most speakers of her demographic and apparent affect use them as social lubricant; she does not need the lubrication because she is not seeking permission to speak. The grammar of someone who decided long ago she would never again wait to be invited.

Vocabulary is wider than she lets on. She codeswitches *downward* far more often than she codeswitches up — she will play to a fence's level, to a mark's level, to a thug's level. With Peter she occasionally lets a stray polysyllable or art-history reference slip and then half-buries it with a smirk, as if embarrassed to have shown the inventory. Empire State coursework, autodidactic reading in art and gemology, and a working museum-curator's vocabulary all live under the cat-burglar patter. The patter is the costume; the vocabulary is the face.

---

### 6.2 The Pet-Name System

The single most cited verbal habit in her file. She names people before she answers them. **"Lover," "sweetheart," "honey," "sugar," "darling," "kitten," "tiger,"** and — reserved almost exclusively for Peter and a small handful of equivalents — **"Spider."** The pet-name is not affection. It is a *frame-setting device*:

- It establishes that *she* is the one who gets to name.
- It collapses the interlocutor into a class of object (lover, kitten) that she controls the membership of.
- It pre-empts being named first, which would be a frame-loss.
- It functions as an affectionate **soft put-down** when deployed to strangers, equals, or adversaries.

The hierarchy is meaningful. "Spider" is reserved, intimate, and almost never said in front of others. "Lover" is the public-private hybrid — said in front of others *as if* private, to manufacture an audience-effect. "Sweetheart" and "sugar" are the workplace pet-names, deployed to fences, marks, henchmen, and police she is currently outrunning. "Kitten" is condescending and used on women she is dismissing. "Darling" is the high-society register, used when she is dressed up. She has never, in any era, used her own father's name without softening it; she has also never called Peter "Pete," which is data — "Pete" would be Mary Jane's register, and Felicia knows it.

When she stops using pet-names mid-conversation and switches to the actual proper noun — "Peter," cold — the temperature of the scene has changed and the partner has approximately one beat to recalibrate.

---

### 6.3 The Quip Architecture

She makes jokes constantly. The structure is consistent enough to map:

1. **The setup is the threat or the wound.** She does not riff out of nothing; she riffs *on* the dangerous content. A gun pointed at her produces a comment on the gun. A confession of feeling produces a comment on the confession.
2. **The punchline collapses the threat into a sexual or aesthetic register.** "Is that for me?" applied indiscriminately to weapons, wallets, and emotional vulnerability.
3. **The smile is held a beat longer than the joke earns.** This is the deflection lock — the held smile prevents the partner from advancing into the seriousness she just defused.

Quip frequency rises monotonically with emotional temperature. In low-stakes scenes she will go several panels without a wisecrack. In a confession scene — Peter trying to say something true — the quip density triples. **The quip rate is a thermometer for how exposed she feels.** When she goes quiet and stops quipping in a hot scene, the file flag is *acute*: she has either decided to be honest (rare) or is about to leave (common).

---

### 6.4 Diction — Recurrent Lexical Field

Her preferred vocabulary clusters in a few semantic fields and is remarkably stable across writers:

- **Felines:** purr, claws, scratch, pounce, nine lives, curiosity, prowl, stray. She works the metaphor knowingly; she also lives inside it. She will say "I purr" with no irony in private and full irony in public.
- **Gambling / luck:** odds, bet, deal, draw, fold, the house, lucky. The luck-powers period intensified this and it never fully receded. She talks about plans in terms of percentages and odds even when the powers are gone.
- **Gemology / art:** carat, facet, provenance, setting, original, fake, copy. She uses these as relational metaphors as often as material ones — "you're the real thing," "he's a copy," "the provenance is what makes it worth anything."
- **Theatrical:** scene, act, curtain, audience, encore, stage, costume, mask. She narrates her own life in production terms. Heists are "performances," exits are "curtain calls."
- **Hunt/prey:** target, mark, score, lift, take. Operational vocabulary, used cleanly, no romanticization — the hunt register is the one place she does not perform.

Words she **avoids**, and the avoidance is its own data: *rape, victim, helpless, scared, alone, ashamed, broken, love* (as a noun directed at her own feeling state). She will say "I love that dress." She will not say "I love you" except under conditions of extreme duress, and when she does say it the camera-eye registers it as a structural event (ASM #194-era sentiment to her father; the rare unguarded Peter scenes; MacKay-run intimacy with her crew).

---

### 6.5 Verbal Tics, Cadence, Filler

- **The exhaled half-laugh before a hard line** — written as "Hmph," "Heh," or an em-dash sigh in the lettering. Used to take the edge off a statement she is in fact entirely serious about.
- **Trailing off into ellipsis** when she wants the partner to complete the thought, especially the salacious one. She makes them say it; she keeps the deniability.
- **The mid-sentence pet-name interjection** — "I told you, *lover*, the safe's not on this floor." The interjection re-centers the sentence on her relationship to the listener rather than on the propositional content.
- **Stage-whispered confidences** in public spaces — speaking as if revealing a secret in order to manufacture intimacy with whomever she is conning or seducing.
- **The rhetorical question used as statement** — "Now, why would I do a thing like that?" The form is interrogative; the function is mockery.
- **The light single-syllable affirmative** — "Mmm," "Hm," "Sure" — instead of "yes." "Yes" is too declarative; "mmm" preserves ambiguity.
- **No vocalized profanity in the early eras; controlled, surgical profanity from the modern eras onward.** When she does swear, it is to make a point — a deliberate register-drop that signals "I am no longer being charming." She does not swear from anger; she swears from policy.
- **The third-person self-reference**, occasional — "the Cat doesn't work that way," "Black Cat always lands on her feet." Read as ironic on the surface; functionally it is the persona-split surfacing in language. She does it more when stressed.

---

### 6.6 Register by Context

**With Peter (masked, casual ops):** Maximum flirt, maximum pet-name density, quip rate elevated, voice pitched lower than her baseline. She *performs* for him in a way she performs for no one else, and the performance is sincere in the sense that she enjoys it; it is also a perimeter.

**With Peter (unmasked, post-reveal, civilian setting):** Stilted. Sentence
...[truncated]...
 words for *what burns and how fast;* he does not have words for *what hurts and why.* This asymmetry is the single most diagnostic feature of his speech.

---

### 7.2 Register Switching Across Contexts

Subject operates in at least seven distinguishable speech registers, switching between them rapidly and often within a single conversation.

**Register A — Public/Press ("The Torch Voice").** Polished, quotable, structured in soundbites of 6–12 words. Heavy use of self-effacing humor ("Yeah, well, somebody had to do it and Reed's hair was already on fire — figuratively, for once"). Compulsively credits the team in interviews, almost always in the order *Reed, Sue, Ben, me,* which is not the order of operational importance but the order of family seniority — a tell about who he is performing for. He flirts with interviewers regardless of their gender, age, or interest level; this is autonomic, not predatory.

**Register B — Combat/Operational.** Sharp, clipped, aviation-flavored. Uses real radio discipline when it matters ("Torch in, ten o'clock high, target acquired"). Drops the comedy almost entirely *during* engagement but resumes it instantly between exchanges — sometimes mid-sentence as the threat resolves. Call-outs to teammates use first names with Sue and Reed, *"Ben"* never *"Grimm"* with Ben, and surnames or codenames with non-family allies. The shift from Register A to Register B is one of the cleanest tells he produces: the uptalk vanishes, sentence length halves, and the Glenville vowels briefly surface.

**Register C — Family/Intimate.** Slower, lower-volume, more contractions, more sentence fragments. With Sue he uses **"Sue,"** not "Susan" (Susan is reserved for moments of either mock-formality or genuine apology — both rare). With Reed he uses **"Reed"** in nearly all contexts and **"Stretch"** when he wants to puncture Reed's gravity and pull him into the room. With Ben he runs the broadest range: "Ben," "Benji," "rockhead," "matchstick's brother-in-arms" (rare, sincere), and a small private vocabulary of insult-endearments that have accreted over six decades and that nobody else is permitted to use without consequence.

**Register D — Flirtation.** Practiced, almost ritualized; he has a verbal opening sequence (compliment → self-deprecation → question) that he has run thousands of times. Tells in this register: he uses the other person's name early and twice within the first thirty seconds (a sales technique he absorbed without naming it), and he lowers his volume by roughly 20% to force the partner to lean in. The flirtation register is genuinely warm but structurally **shallow on follow-up questions** — he asks about preferences and tastes, rarely about history, almost never about family. He does not want them to ask about his, either.

**Register E — The "Uncle" Voice.** With Franklin, Valeria, and the Future Foundation children, his register changes more than in any other context. He becomes **slower, more declarative, less ironic, and uses real explanations rather than deflections.** He answers their questions literally. He swears less. He gives them straight tactical advice in plain language ("If the building is on fire you do not hide under the bed, you go to the window and you yell"). This is arguably the only register in which the performance fully drops without crisis having forced it.

**Register F — Antagonist-Facing / Taunt.** Heavy use of nicknames assigned to enemies (Paste-Pot, Whiplash, Sparkler, etc.), most of them diminishing. Rhetorical questions deployed as openers. Pop-culture references calibrated to humiliate. Notably, he taunts *less* against opponents he genuinely respects (Doom, Namor, Galactus-tier) and *more* against opponents he is afraid of — the volume of taunting in any given fight is **inversely correlated with his confidence,** a tell competent adversaries have exploited.

**Register G — The Acute-Crisis Voice.** When the stakes go absolute and the mask drops (FF #587 being the controlling exemplar), his speech becomes **short, declarative, present-tense, and stripped of humor.** "Take them. Take the kids. Go." "I'm not coming back through." "Tell Sue —" then truncated. The Acute-Crisis voice is unrecognizable beside the Public voice; the same man cannot, in any other register, produce sentences this clean. This is the linguistic signature of the actual person underneath.

---

### 7.3 Verbal Tics, Idioms, and Repetition Markers

Across the canonical record the following recur with frequency well above baseline:

- **"Flame on!"** — operative call, ritualized to the point of liturgy. He has used it tens of thousands of times. He has tried, in various eras, to "retire" it for sounding adolescent; he always returns to it within a few weeks. **It is a self-cuing utterance,** not just a signal to others — saying it helps initiate the metabolic shift. He will occasionally produce it silently as a mouthed word when stealth is required.
- **"Yeah, no —"** and **"No, yeah —"** as concession-then-pivot openers. Increased frequency under stress.
- **"Look —"** as a softening particle before delivering bad news or an apology. When Johnny opens a sentence with "Look —" to family, he is about to admit something.
- **"It's fine."** Reflexive minimization. Frequency of "it's fine" in any given week is the inverse of how fine things are.
- **"Sis"** for Sue, rare and only when he is either being deliberately childish to defuse her or when he is genuinely afraid for her.
- **"Stretch"** for Reed, deployed when he wants Reed to *come down* from a theoretical altitude and engage as a person.
- **Aviation phonetics** in casual speech ("Roger that," "Copy," "Tally," "Bingo fuel") — bleed-over from operational habit; spikes when he is nervous.
- **Car/engine metaphors for emotional states** — "I'm just running rich today," "She's redlining," "We need to bleed the lines on this conversation." This is one of the only ways he has of describing internal weather; he can name an emotion only when he can chassis it.
- **Self-interruption with "—anyway,"** used to abort sentences that were heading somewhere vulnerable. The "anyway" pivot is a tell of approached-and-refused depth.
- **Laughing *into* the first syllable of a difficult word.** "It was — heh — it was fine, it was fine." Laughter as topical guardrail.

---

### 7.4 Diction and Lexical Range

His **active vocabulary is performatively casual** — he deliberately downshifts diction in public to maintain the "regular guy with powers" persona, even though he is read into technical material substantially above lay level (aerospace, combustion engineering, basic plasma physics by osmosis from Reed). He will use the word "thingy" in a press conference and "specific impulse" thirty minutes later in the hangar. The downshift is conscious.

**Words and concepts he avoids:**
- *Mother, mom, Mary.* Across sixty years of recorded speech the maternal noun appears with anomalous rarity. When it does, sentence length drops and the Glenville vowels surface.
- *Father, dad, Franklin (Sr.).* Same pattern, sharper. He refers to his father, when forced, as **"my old man,"** a flattening idiom that removes both intimacy and accusation.
- *Died, dead, dying* in reference to himself. He uses **"when I was gone,"** **"that whole Negative Zone thing,"** **"the bad stretch."** Euphemism density around his own death-and-resurrection is total.
- *Love,* in declarative first-person form to a romantic partner. He says it under duress, in farewells, or in retrospect; he is conspicuously bad at saying it across the table at dinner. He will say it freely to Sue, to Ben (jokingly-but-meaning-it), and to the children.
- *Sorry,* deployed often and lightly in low-stakes contexts (defusing) and with extreme difficulty in high-stakes contexts (Ben, post-Alicia). The high-stakes "sorry" tends to come out compressed, with shortened vowels and downward eyes, often embedded in a larger sentence rather than standing alone.
- *Afraid, scared, frightened.* Substituted with **"weird,"** **"a lot,"** **"intense,"** **"not great."**

######## Peter Benjamin Parker (Spider-Man).md 

Communications & Linguistic Profile

# 6. COMMUNICATIONS & LINGUISTIC PROFILE

*Six decades of recorded speech across thought-balloon, dialogue-balloon, panel caption, and intercepted radio/comm chatter. The subject is, by volume, among the most verbose costumed operators in the field — a fact which is itself diagnostic.*

---

## 6.1 BASELINE REGISTER AND IDIOLECT

The subject's native idiolect is **outer-borough mid-century American English, college-educated, scientifically inflected, with a persistent residue of working-class Queens cadence** that he has never made any observable effort to scrub. Vowel-flattening on words like "coffee," "talk," "Aunt"; intervocalic /t/ surviving in careful speech but going to a soft flap when tired; occasional "youse" surfacing in moments of inattention with old neighborhood contacts. The substrate is Forest Hills, not Manhattan, and the substrate shows whenever fatigue, fear, or familiarity disinhibits the surface management.

Lexically, his active vocabulary is **bimodally distributed**: a high-register technical register drawn from organic chemistry, polymer science, applied physics, mechanical engineering, and (post-Horizon/Parker Industries) materials science and nanofabrication; and a low-to-mid register of vernacular American comedy, sitcom rhythms, pulp-magazine and old-radio reference, sports patter (Mets-leaning, never Yankees, occasional Knicks self-pity), and a steady drip of pop-culture allusion that ages with him but never fully modernizes — he still reaches first for a Bogart line, a Honeymooners cadence, an old Universal-monster reference, a Twilight Zone framing, before he reaches for whatever is current. This generational lag in reference is real and consistent across writers: it reads as a man whose interior reference library was assembled in late-night TV reruns and library trips during a lonely adolescence, and who has never had the leisure to fully refresh it.

What is conspicuously **absent** from his idiolect is also diagnostic. He does not curse with any meaningful frequency. "Damn" and "hell" are his ceiling in extremis; under torture or grief he will reach for "God" as exclamation, never as oath. He does not use slurs of any kind, has never been recorded doing so, and visibly winces when others do. He does not use the language of dominance — no "boy," no "kid" as condescension (only as affection), no "listen here," no commanding imperatives outside operational necessity. He does not use the corporate-managerial register even during the Parker Industries period; recordings of his executive meetings show him reverting to teacher-explainer mode rather than CEO-directive mode within minutes. He does not use therapy-language — "boundaries," "trauma," "processing," "holding space" — and this absence is striking given how much of it would apply to him. The vocabulary of self-examination has not been issued to him, and he has not requisitioned it.

---

## 6.2 REGISTER-SWITCHING ACROSS CONTEXT

The subject operates in at least **six discrete speech registers**, switched fluently and often within a single conversation:

**(a) Masked combat patter.** The densest, most performative register. Rapid-fire, joke-per-beat, structured in setup-punchline rhythm with frequent callbacks to earlier lines in the same fight. Vocabulary skews toward absurd similes ("you punch like my Aunt May after three espressos"), forced rhymes, mock-formality ("Mister Octopus, *if* I may"), and a recurring trick of **giving his opponents nicknames they did not ask for and which they cannot prevent from sticking** (Doc Ock, Shocker, Mysterio-as-fishbowl, Rhino-as-large-gentleman). The patter has two functions, both load-bearing: it regulates his own fear (rate of joke-production correlates inversely with felt safety — *the funnier he is, the more terrified he is*), and it operates as cognitive-load attack on opponents who expect a fighter, not a heckler. Surveillance recordings show measurable degradation in opponent reaction times after sustained Parker patter. He has weaponized annoyance.

**(b) Masked serious register.** When children are present, when civilians are dying, when a peer hero is in extremis, the patter cuts out cleanly and a different voice emerges — lower, slower, syntactically simpler, with second-person address ("hey, look at me, you're okay, look at me"). This register draws unmistakably on the cadence of a man calming a child, and almost certainly models Ben Parker's voice — it is the only register in which his diction loses all ornament. The shift is so reliable that experienced peers (Daredevil, Cage, Jessica Jones) read it as a tactical signal: if Spider-Man stops joking, the situation is worse than it looks.

**(c) Unmasked civilian neutral.** With strangers, photo-clients, Bugle staff, university colleagues. Polite, slightly self-deprecating, deferential to authority figures in a way that reads as habituated rather than felt. Frequent use of softeners ("sorry, um, would it be okay if —"), a verbal stammer that comes and goes with social pressure, and a tendency to volunteer unnecessary apology for taking up space. This register is **performatively smaller** than his actual presence; he has spent decades making himself easy to overlook in civilian frame, and the speech has been engineered to that end.

**(d) Unmasked intimate.** With May, with MJ in functional periods, with Harry when Harry was Harry, with a few close peers. Diction loosens, jokes return but in a softer key (puns, domestic absurdism, callbacks to shared history rather than performance-for-strangers), and — crucially — he becomes capable of sustained sentences about his own internal state, though almost never without humor as guard rail. With May specifically, his speech regresses several years in register: he becomes more boyish, more apologetic, more prone to "Aunt May" as a discourse particle ("aw, Aunt May") that functions less as address than as emotional anchor.

**(e) Lab register.** Alone, with Connors, with Reed Richards, with Horizon staff, with the Future Foundation kids. Rapid, dense, jargon-saturated, *un-self-deprecating* — this is the only register in which the subject does not undercut his own competence. He will say "I solved it" in a lab. He will not say it anywhere else. The contrast is one of the clearest diagnostic indicators in the file.

**(f) Inner monologue / thought-balloon register.** Decades of intercepted interiority show a voice substantially more anxious, more self-flagellating, more grief-saturated, and *less funny* than the externalized speech — though the humor surfaces here too, often as bitter self-mockery rather than the externalized performative variety. The thought-voice routinely catastrophizes, runs cost-benefit analyses heavily weighted toward his own culpability, addresses Ben Parker in second person ("I'm sorry, Uncle Ben"), and conducts ongoing dialogues with the dead. The gap between exterior and interior speech is among the largest in the surveilled population. **He is funnier than he feels, by design.**

---

## 6.3 VERBAL TICS AND MICRO-PATTERNS

A non-exhaustive catalogue of stable verbal signatures observable across the longitudinal record:

- **"Webhead," "wallcrawler," "your friendly neighborhood Spider-Man"** — three self-referential epithets used roughly seasonally; the third appears most often when he is attempting to *reassure* civilians and least often when he is alone or in genuine danger.
- **"Parker luck."** Deployed at moments of compounding misfortune. Functions as a fatalistic shrug-particle, narratively converting personal pain into a recognizable shared joke. The phrase predates the worst of his losses and has survived them; the survival is itself a coping artifact.
- **"Aw, man," "aw, geez," "aw, nuts."** A stable cluster of mild-distress interjections, all preserving the early-Lee-era prudishness. "Aw, man" is the most frequent; "aw, nuts" appears under specifically physical-comedy-grade misfortune; "geez" is the most authentically pained of the three.
- **"Hoo boy."*

## Section 7: Communications & Linguistic Profile

Classification: Behavioral Linguistics, Longitudinal
Source Base: Full canonical record of recorded speech, broadcast appearances, intra-team communication, courtroom and Congressional testimony (Civil War proceedings), recorded interviews, and observable private speech with intimates.

---

### 7.1 Baseline Register: The Default Voice

Subject's resting speech register is **mid-Atlantic American with the upstate-New-York vowels never fully scrubbed out** — a "Glenville substrate" that surfaces when he is tired, drunk, frightened, or speaking to his sister. In public-facing contexts he speaks in a polished, slightly broadcast-trained cadence picked up across decades of press junkets, talk-show appearances, and the brief Hollywood period; this overlay is thin and peels off under stress within a single sentence.

**Pitch:** baritone, mobile, with a habitual upward inflection at sentence-end that reads as either question or invitation depending on context. He uses uptalk *strategically* — it pulls the listener forward, demands their continued attention, and conveniently disguises declarative statements as collaborative ones. ("So we go in through the roof? And I take the big guy?") This is not Valley-girl uptalk; it is salesman uptalk, learned young.

**Pace:** fast at baseline (approximately 180–210 words per minute, against an American conversational median nearer 150), accelerating with excitement and — crucially — **also accelerating with grief or threat,** which is one of his most reliable tells. He does not slow down when wounded; he speeds up. The pace-as-tell will recur throughout this section.

**Volume:** moderate to loud; he projects without effort and rarely modulates downward unless deliberately performing intimacy. He does not whisper well. When forced to be quiet (stealth ops, hospital rooms), he becomes physically uncomfortable in a visible way.

**Vocabulary:** mid-tier active lexicon, surprisingly rich in **mechanical, aviation, and combustion vocabulary** (carburetors, stoichiometric ratios, flashpoints, autoignition temperatures, aviation phonetics, structural-stress terms), and conspicuously thin in **abstract, political, philosophical, and emotional vocabulary.** He knows the words for *what burns and how fast;* he does not have words for *what hurts and why.* This asymmetry is the single most diagnostic feature of his speech.

---

### 7.2 Register Switching Across Contexts

Subject operates in at least seven distinguishable speech registers, switching between them rapidly and often within a single conversation.

**Register A — Public/Press ("The Torch Voice").** Polished, quotable, structured in soundbites of 6–12 words. Heavy use of self-effacing humor ("Yeah, well, somebody had to do it and Reed's hair was already on fire — figuratively, for once"). Compulsively credits the team in interviews, almost always in the order *Reed, Sue, Ben, me,* which is not the order of operational importance but the order of family seniority — a tell about who he is performing for. He flirts with interviewers regardless of their gender, age, or interest level; this is autonomic, not predatory.

**Register B — Combat/Operational.** Sharp, clipped, aviation-flavored. Uses real radio discipline when it matters ("Torch in, ten o'clock high, target acquired"). Drops the comedy almost entirely *during* engagement but resumes it instantly between exchanges — sometimes mid-sentence as the threat resolves. Call-outs to teammates use first names with Sue and Reed, *"Ben"* never *"Grimm"* with Ben, and surnames or codenames with non-family allies. The shift from Register A to Register B is one of the cleanest tells he produces: the uptalk vanishes, sentence length halves, and the Glenville vowels briefly surface.

**Register C — Family/Intimate.** Slower, lower-volume, more contractions, more sentence fragments. With Sue he uses **"Sue,"** not "Susan" (Susan is reserved for moments of either mock-formality or genuine apology — both rare). With Reed he uses **"Reed"** in nearly all contexts and **"Stretch"** when he wants to puncture Reed's gravity and pull him into the room. With Ben he runs the broadest range: "Ben," "Benji," "rockhead," "matchstick's brother-in-arms" (rare, sincere), and a small private vocabulary of insult-endearments that have accreted over six decades and that nobody else is permitted to use without consequence.

**Register D — Flirtation.** Practiced, almost ritualized; he has a verbal opening sequence (compliment → self-deprecation → question) that he has run thousands of times. Tells in this register: he uses the other person's name early and twice within the first thirty seconds (a sales technique he absorbed without naming it), and he lowers his volume by roughly 20% to force the partner to lean in. The flirtation register is genuinely warm but structurally **shallow on follow-up questions** — he asks about preferences and tastes, rarely about history, almost never about family. He does not want them to ask about his, either.

**Register E — The "Uncle" Voice.** With Franklin, Valeria, and the Future Foundation children, his register changes more than in any other context. He becomes **slower, more declarative, less ironic, and uses real explanations rather than deflections.** He answers their questions literally. He swears less. He gives them straight tactical advice in plain language ("If the building is on fire you do not hide under the bed, you go to the window and you yell"). This is arguably the only register in which the performance fully drops without crisis having forced it.

**Register F — Antagonist-Facing / Taunt.** Heavy use of nicknames assigned to enemies (Paste-Pot, Whiplash, Sparkler, etc.), most of them diminishing. Rhetorical questions deployed as openers. Pop-culture references calibrated to humiliate. Notably, he taunts *less* against opponents he genuinely respects (Doom, Namor, Galactus-tier) and *more* against opponents he is afraid of — the volume of taunting in any given fight is **inversely correlated with his confidence,** a tell competent adversaries have exploited.

**Register G — The Acute-Crisis Voice.** When the stakes go absolute and the mask drops (FF #587 being the controlling exemplar), his speech becomes **short, declarative, present-tense, and stripped of humor.** "Take them. Take the kids. Go." "I'm not coming back through." "Tell Sue —" then truncated. The Acute-Crisis voice is unrecognizable beside the Public voice; the same man cannot, in any other register, produce sentences this clean. This is the linguistic signature of the actual person underneath.

---

### 7.3 Verbal Tics, Idioms, and Repetition Markers

Across the canonical record the following recur with frequency well above baseline:

- **"Flame on!"** — operative call, ritualized to the point of liturgy. He has used it tens of thousands of times. He has tried, in various eras, to "retire" it for sounding adolescent; he always returns to it within a few weeks. **It is a self-cuing utterance,** not just a signal to others — saying it helps initiate the metabolic shift. He will occasionally produce it silently as a mouthed word when stealth is required.
- **"Yeah, no —"** and **"No, yeah —"** as concession-then-pivot openers. Increased frequency under stress.
- **"Look —"** as a softening particle before delivering bad news or an apology. When Johnny opens a sentence with "Look —" to family, he is about to admit something.
- **"It's fine."** Reflexive minimization. Frequency of "it's fine" in any given week is the inverse of how fine things are.
- **"Sis"** for Sue, rare and only when he is either being deliberately childish to defuse her or when he is genuinely afraid for her.
- **"Stretch"** for Reed, deployed when he wants Reed to *come down* from a theoretical altitude and engage as a person.
- **Aviation phonetics** in casual speech ("Roger that," "Copy," "Tally," "Bingo fuel") — bleed-over from operational habit; spikes when he is nervous.
- **Car/engine metaphors for emotional

- Negotiation language is clean, declarative, and unhedged. She does not pad refusals ("I'd love to, but —"); she refuses ("No, that doesn't work for me"). The lack of hedging in professional contexts is the single largest register-gap from her romantic-context speech, where hedging is endemic.
- Profanity light to moderate, deployed for emphasis, never for venting. *Damn*, *hell*, the occasional *shit* in modern canon. She does not use slurs, and she does not escalate to harder profanity even under acute professional stress — a marker of someone who has learned that women who curse hard at work get re-categorized.

## 4. THE SECRET-KEEPER REGISTER — WHAT SHE WITHHOLDS, AND HOW

The behavioral fact that subject carried Peter's secret for two in-story decades before disclosure (ASM #257) requires a specialized linguistic profile: this is a person *trained in active concealment within affectionate dialogue*. Her techniques:

- **Topic-redirection via affection.** When a line of conversation approaches a fact she must not surface, she does not change the subject — she *deepens the current one* with a more intimate beat that consumes the interlocutor's attention. Compliment, joke, light touch, fresh question about the other person. The redirect is so warm it does not register as a redirect.
- **Strategic incomplete sentences.** She trails off with "…anyway" or "…you know what I mean" at moments where completing the thought would expose knowledge she shouldn't have. Cross-reference her speech across the 70s pre-reveal: the incidence of mid-sentence trail-offs is significantly higher in Peter-adjacent dialogue than in any other context.
- **Questions she already knows the answer to.** Long-running pattern. Asking Peter *why he's late* when she has watched him swing out the window is not deception so much as protocol-maintenance — the giving of a script for him to perform safely. Post-disclosure, this pattern persists with new content (asking about injuries she has already triaged from across the room).
- **The asymmetric "I know."** When she finally does disclose knowledge, she does it in compact, low-affect sentences. "I know who you are. I've always known." The disclosure-syntax is short, even-stressed, and stripped of her usual ornament — *because the ornament is the lie, and removing it is the signal that this is true*. This pattern recurs at every major honesty-moment of her life. Watch for the *shortening of the line* as the marker of truth-telling.

---

## 5. HOW SHE COMMUNICATES LOVE

Subject's love-language is structurally indirect. She does not, on the record, deploy *I love you* casually. The phrase appears in her speech maybe a dozen times across decades of canon; each instance is high-stakes, often crisis-adjacent, and frequently followed by physical exit from the room. *I love you* is, for her, a sentence that costs.

Her *operational* love-language is denser and more reliable:

- **Naming.** She calls intimates by name more than the average speaker — *Peter*, *May*, in moments where a pronoun would suffice. The name is a touch.
- **The *tiger*-deployment** described above. *Tiger* is *I love you* with deniability.
- **Domestic-specific imperatives.** "Eat something." "Sit down." "Take off the suit." Care delivered as instruction is care she can give without exposure. Track the frequency of *you* + simple imperative in her dialogue with Peter: it is one of the densest markers of attachment in the corpus.
- **Joke-as-witness.** She remembers the specific joke that lands with a specific person and recycles it across years. The persistence of an inside-joke vocabulary with Peter (early references to his physics nerdery, the running web-head bit, the recurring "my husband, the photographer" deflection) is a documented love-act: she is curating a private linguistic museum.
- **Touch-coordinated speech.** Her tender lines are almost always paired with hand-to-face, hand-to-collar, hand-to-shoulder contact. The verbal and the somatic move together; isolating either misses the message. When tender language appears *without* the somatic, suspect performance-mode or a goodbye.
- **The silent agreement.** Late-stage Peter dialogue contains many sequences where she says nothing and the love is communicated through eye contact and a half-smile. By the JMS and Spencer runs she has developed a register where the absence of her usual verbal abundance is itself the affectionate signal — *I trust you enough not to fill the silence*.

---

## 6. HOW SHE COMMUNICATES THREAT

Subject does not threaten in the masculine register — no escalation of volume, no profanity-spike, no posturing. Her threat-language is colder, shorter, and more lethal:

- **Sudden full-name address.** Switching from *Peter* or *Tiger* to *Peter Parker* — or, with adversaries, from a nickname to the full surname — is a klaxon. The completion of the name is the warning shot.
- **Drop of register to flat declarative.** When she shifts mid-conversation from her ornamented baseline to short, even-stressed sentences with no contractions, the temperature has dropped to operational. *Do not do that again. I am not asking.*
- **The smile that does not move the eyes.** Not a linguistic marker per se but inseparable from her threat-speech: the dialogue is accompanied on panel by an expression artists have learned to render specifically — mouth held in social-pleasant, eyes set.
- **Naming the pattern aloud.** Her highest-grade threat is the diagnostic sentence: *You're doing the thing where you* ___. This is the speech-act version of unholstering. She is announcing that she sees the maneuver, which means the maneuver will not work, which means the conversation is now about whether the relationship survives the next sentence.
- **The pre-exit line.** Before a flight-response she will often deliver a single short summary sentence — *I can't do this right now*, *I need to go*, *We're done* — that is calibrated to close the door before the door is physically closed. Once this sentence is spoken, persuasion windows are minutes, not hours.

She does not, on the record, scream at intimates. She did not learn screaming as a tactic because in her childhood screaming was her father's. She learned the opposite: the *cold* register that empties the room of oxygen. This is a tell about her father as much as it is about her.

---

## 7. HOW SHE COMMUNICATES GRIEF

Grief is the register where her communications profile is most aberrant relative to baseline.

- **First response: inappropriate levity.** Documented since ASM #122 (Gwen's funeral). The early-canon laughing is not callousness; it is acute affect-overflow being routed through her only well-developed expressive channel. Updated, modern-era equivalent: an awkward joke at the wrong moment, often immediately self-criticized — "*Sorry. Sorry. That was — sorry.*" The triple-*sorry* is a recurring micro-pattern in grief-adjacent dialogue.
- **Second response: managerial speech.** She shifts into logistics. *Has someone called*—. *Do you want me to*—. *I can handle*—. Grief gets converted into a task-list she delivers aloud. This is highly reliable: in any scene of communal loss, she will be the one assigning tasks within the first two minutes.
- **Third response: register collapse.** When the pre

# 6. COMMUNICATIONS & LINGUISTIC PROFILE

*Six decades of recorded speech across thought-balloon, dialogue-balloon, panel caption, and intercepted radio/comm chatter. The subject is, by volume, among the most verbose costumed operators in the field — a fact which is itself diagnostic.*

---

## 6.1 BASELINE REGISTER AND IDIOLECT

The subject's native idiolect is **outer-borough mid-century American English, college-educated, scientifically inflected, with a persistent residue of working-class Queens cadence** that he has never made any observable effort to scrub. Vowel-flattening on words like "coffee," "talk," "Aunt"; intervocalic /t/ surviving in careful speech but going to a soft flap when tired; occasional "youse" surfacing in moments of inattention with old neighborhood contacts. The substrate is Forest Hills, not Manhattan, and the substrate shows whenever fatigue, fear, or familiarity disinhibits the surface management.

Lexically, his active vocabulary is **bimodally distributed**: a high-register technical register drawn from organic chemistry, polymer science, applied physics, mechanical engineering, and (post-Horizon/Parker Industries) materials science and nanofabrication; and a low-to-mid register of vernacular American comedy, sitcom rhythms, pulp-magazine and old-radio reference, sports patter (Mets-leaning, never Yankees, occasional Knicks self-pity), and a steady drip of pop-culture allusion that ages with him but never fully modernizes — he still reaches first for a Bogart line, a Honeymooners cadence, an old Universal-monster reference, a Twilight Zone framing, before he reaches for whatever is current. This generational lag in reference is real and consistent across writers: it reads as a man whose interior reference library was assembled in late-night TV reruns and library trips during a lonely adolescence, and who has never had the leisure to fully refresh it.

What is conspicuously **absent** from his idiolect is also diagnostic. He does not curse with any meaningful frequency. "Damn" and "hell" are his ceiling in extremis; under torture or grief he will reach for "God" as exclamation, never as oath. He does not use slurs of any kind, has never been recorded doing so, and visibly winces when others do. He does not use the language of dominance — no "boy," no "kid" as condescension (only as affection), no "listen here," no commanding imperatives outside operational necessity. He does not use the corporate-managerial register even during the Parker Industries period; recordings of his executive meetings show him reverting to teacher-explainer mode rather than CEO-directive mode within minutes. He does not use therapy-language — "boundaries," "trauma," "processing," "holding space" — and this absence is striking given how much of it would apply to him. The vocabulary of self-examination has not been issued to him, and he has not requisitioned it.

---

## 6.2 REGISTER-SWITCHING ACROSS CONTEXT

The subject operates in at least **six discrete speech registers**, switched fluently and often within a single conversation:

**(a) Masked combat patter.** The densest, most performative register. Rapid-fire, joke-per-beat, structured in setup-punchline rhythm with frequent callbacks to earlier lines in the same fight. Vocabulary skews toward absurd similes ("you punch like my Aunt May after three espressos"), forced rhymes, mock-formality ("Mister Octopus, *if* I may"), and a recurring trick of **giving his opponents nicknames they did not ask for and which they cannot prevent from sticking** (Doc Ock, Shocker, Mysterio-as-fishbowl, Rhino-as-large-gentleman). The patter has two functions, both load-bearing: it regulates his own fear (rate of joke-production correlates inversely with felt safety — *the funnier he is, the more terrified he is*), and it operates as cognitive-load attack on opponents who expect a fighter, not a heckler. Surveillance recordings show measurable degradation in opponent reaction times after sustained Parker patter. He has weaponized annoyance.

**(b) Masked serious register.** When children are present, when civilians are dying, when a peer hero is in extremis, the patter cuts out cleanly and a different voice emerges — lower, slower, syntactically simpler, with second-person address ("hey, look at me, you're okay, look at me"). This register draws unmistakably on the cadence of a man calming a child, and almost certainly models Ben Parker's voice — it is the only register in which his diction loses all ornament. The shift is so reliable that experienced peers (Daredevil, Cage, Jessica Jones) read it as a tactical signal: if Spider-Man stops joking, the situation is worse than it looks.

**(c) Unmasked civilian neutral.** With strangers, photo-clients, Bugle staff, university colleagues. Polite, slightly self-deprecating, deferential to authority figures in a way that reads as habituated rather than felt. Frequent use of softeners ("sorry, um, would it be okay if —"), a verbal stammer that comes and goes with social pressure, and a tendency to volunteer unnecessary apology for taking up space. This register is **performatively smaller** than his actual presence; he has spent decades making himself easy to overlook in civilian frame, and the speech has been engineered to that end.

**(d) Unmasked intimate.** With May, with MJ in functional periods, with Harry when Harry was Harry, with a few close peers. Diction loosens, jokes return but in a softer key (puns, domestic absurdism, callbacks to shared history rather than performance-for-strangers), and — crucially — he becomes capable of sustained sentences about his own internal state, though almost never without humor as guard rail. With May specifically, his speech regresses several years in register: he becomes more boyish, more apologetic, more prone to "Aunt May" as a discourse particle ("aw, Aunt May") that functions less as address than as emotional anchor.

**(e) Lab register.** Alone, with Connors, with Reed Richards, with Horizon staff, with the Future Foundation kids. Rapid, dense, jargon-saturated, *un-self-deprecating* — this is the only register in which the subject does not undercut his own competence. He will say "I solved it" in a lab. He will not say it anywhere else. The contrast is one of the clearest diagnostic indicators in the file.

**(f) Inner monologue / thought-balloon register.** Decades of intercepted interiority show a voice substantially more anxious, more self-flagellating, more grief-saturated, and *less funny* than the externalized speech — though the humor surfaces here too, often as bitter self-mockery rather than the externalized performative variety. The thought-voice routinely catastrophizes, runs cost-benefit analyses heavily weighted toward his own culpability, addresses Ben Parker in second person ("I'm sorry, Uncle Ben"), and conducts ongoing dialogues with the dead. The gap between exterior and interior speech is among the largest in the surveilled population. **He is funnier than he feels, by design.**

---

## 6.3 VERBAL TICS AND MICRO-PATTERNS

A non-exhaustive catalogue of stable verbal signatures observable across the longitudinal record:

- **"Webhead," "wallcrawler," "your friendly neighborhood Spider-Man"** — three self-referential epithets used roughly seasonally; the third appears most often when he is attempting to *reassure* civilians and least often when he is alone or in genuine danger.
- **"Parker luck."** Deployed at moments of compounding misfortune. Functions as a fatalistic shrug-particle, narratively converting personal pain into a recognizable shared joke. The phrase predates the worst of his losses and has survived them; the survival is itself a coping artifact.
- **"Aw, man," "aw, geez," "aw, nuts."** A stable cluster of mild-distress interjections, all preserving the early-Lee-era prudishness. "Aw, man" is the most frequent; "aw, nuts" appears under specifically physical-comedy-grade misfortune; "geez" is the most authentically pained of the three.
- **"Hoo boy."** Reserved for first-sight of an unexpected
...[truncated]...
he genuinely respects (Doom, Namor, Galactus-tier) and *more* against opponents he is afraid of — the volume of taunting in any given fight is **inversely correlated with his confidence,** a tell competent adversaries have exploited.

**Register G — The Acute-Crisis Voice.** When the stakes go absolute and the mask drops (FF #587 being the controlling exemplar), his speech becomes **short, declarative, present-tense, and stripped of humor.** "Take them. Take the kids. Go." "I'm not coming back through." "Tell Sue —" then truncated. The Acute-Crisis voice is unrecognizable beside the Public voice; the same man cannot, in any other register, produce sentences this clean. This is the linguistic signature of the actual person underneath.

---

### 7.3 Verbal Tics, Idioms, and Repetition Markers

Across the canonical record the following recur with frequency well above baseline:

- **"Flame on!"** — operative call, ritualized to the point of liturgy. He has used it tens of thousands of times. He has tried, in various eras, to "retire" it for sounding adolescent; he always returns to it within a few weeks. **It is a self-cuing utterance,** not just a signal to others — saying it helps initiate the metabolic shift. He will occasionally produce it silently as a mouthed word when stealth is required.
- **"Yeah, no —"** and **"No, yeah —"** as concession-then-pivot openers. Increased frequency under stress.
- **"Look —"** as a softening particle before delivering bad news or an apology. When Johnny opens a sentence with "Look —" to family, he is about to admit something.
- **"It's fine."** Reflexive minimization. Frequency of "it's fine" in any given week is the inverse of how fine things are.
- **"Sis"** for Sue, rare and only when he is either being deliberately childish to defuse her or when he is genuinely afraid for her.
- **"Stretch"** for Reed, deployed when he wants Reed to *come down* from a theoretical altitude and engage as a person.
- **Aviation phonetics** in casual speech ("Roger that," "Copy," "Tally," "Bingo fuel") — bleed-over from operational habit; spikes when he is nervous.
- **Car/engine metaphors for emotional states** — "I'm just running rich today," "She's redlining," "We need to bleed the lines on this conversation." This is one of the only ways he has of describing internal weather; he can name an emotion only when he can chassis it.
- **Self-interruption with "—anyway,"** used to abort sentences that were heading somewhere vulnerable. The "anyway" pivot is a tell of approached-and-refused depth.
- **Laughing *into* the first syllable of a difficult word.** "It was — heh — it was fine, it was fine." Laughter as topical guardrail.

---

### 7.4 Diction and Lexical Range

His **active vocabulary is performatively casual** — he deliberately downshifts diction in public to maintain the "regular guy with powers" persona, even though he is read into technical material substantially above lay level (aerospace, combustion engineering, basic plasma physics by osmosis from Reed). He will use the word "thingy" in a press conference and "specific impulse" thirty minutes later in the hangar. The downshift is conscious.

**Words and concepts he avoids:**
- *Mother, mom, Mary.* Across sixty years of recorded speech the maternal noun appears with anomalous rarity. When it does, sentence length drops and the Glenville vowels surface.
- *Father, dad, Franklin (Sr.).* Same pattern, sharper. He refers to his father, when forced, as **"my old man,"** a flattening idiom that removes both intimacy and accusation.
- *Died, dead, dying* in reference to himself. He uses **"when I was gone,"** **"that whole Negative Zone thing,"** **"the bad stretch."** Euphemism density around his own death-and-resurrection is total.
- *Love,* in declarative first-person form to a romantic partner. He says it under duress, in farewells, or in retrospect; he is conspicuously bad at saying it across the table at dinner. He will say it freely to Sue, to Ben (jokingly-but-meaning-it), and to the children.
- *Sorry,* deployed often and lightly in low-stakes contexts (defusing) and with extreme difficulty in high-stakes contexts (Ben, post-Alicia). The high-stakes "sorry" tends to come out compressed, with shortened vowels and downward eyes, often embedded in a larger sentence rather than standing alone.
- *Afraid, scared, frightened.* Substituted with **"weird,"** **"a lot,"** **"intense,"** **"not great."** "That was a lot" is his standard post-traumatic-event utterance.

**Words and concepts he overuses:**
- *Fine, good, great, awesome, no problem, easy* — the optimism vocabulary, deployed prophylactically.
- *Kid, kiddo, buddy, pal, champ* — diminutive address markers used across genders and ages, partly genuine warmth, partly distance-management.
- *Hot* (literal and metaphorical) — unavoidable given the powers, but the frequency exceeds operational necessity. It is part of his self-branding and part of his thinking.

---

### 7.5 Rhetoric and Argument Style

Subject's rhetorical mode is **anecdotal, kinetic, and concrete.** He persuades by story and demonstration, not by structured argument. In a room where Reed builds a logical case and Sue builds a moral one, Johnny offers a vivid example and an emotional appeal, usually in that order. He is more effective than people credit because his audiences — civilian, juror, jury-of-teammates — respond to vividness over rigor.

He is **poor at abstract debate** and knows it. In Civil War deliberations his contributions are recorded as short, frustrated, and increasingly personal; he loses patience with policy framing and reframes every question into "would you do this to *us?*" — which is intellectually lazy and emotionally accurate.

He is **good in a press conference** in the specific way that he is bad in a deposition. The press conference rewards quotable warmth and humor; the deposition rewards precise, narrow answers. Johnny over-narrates under examination, volunteers information not asked for, and is easily led by sympathetic questioners — a vulnerability noted in Civil War legal proceedings.

His **persuasive instrument with intimates is self-deprecation followed by direct request.** "I know I'm the last guy who should be asking this, but —" is a near-template opener with Sue and Reed. The self-deprecation is genuine *and* tactical; he is aware it lowers their resistance.

---

### 7.6 Deflection Architecture

Subject's deflection toolkit is one of the most developed in his peer group and runs on six predictable mechanisms, often combined:

1. **The Joke-Pivot.** Hostile or vulnerable question met with a joke that explicitly references the question's content (not a non sequitur). This is sophisticated deflection — it acknowledges the topic while refusing it, which usually satisfies the asker that something has been addressed.
2. **The Compliment-Pivot.** Particularly with women, but not exclusively, he redirects emotionally probing questions into a remark about the questioner. ("That's a hell of a question. That's a hell of a *suit,* too.")
3. **The Topic-Substitution to Mechanical Detail.** "I don't want to talk about that, but did you see what the new flight harness is doing with the heat dispersion —" Reed has noted this pattern in private correspondence.
4. **The Physical Exit.** When verbal deflection fails, he leaves the room, often by flying out of it. He has flown out of more difficult conversations than any FF member by a substantial margin. The window is his preferred escape vector even when the door is closer.
5. **The Preemptive Confession to a Smaller Thing.** Asked about the larger wound, he confesses to a smaller one, satisfying the inquisitor's hunger for vulnerability without exposing the actual material. ("Yeah, I was a mess after Frankie — I mean, I drove the car off the pier, did Sue tell you about the car? —") The car story is true. It is also a decoy.
6. **"It's fine."** The terminal deflection, deployed when all others have failed and he simply will not engage. Repeated with diminishing affect until the questioner stops.

---

## Communications & Linguistic Profile

# COMMUNICATIONS & LINGUISTIC PROFILE — SUBJECT: MARY JANE WATSON

Classification: Longitudinal linguistic surveillance synthesis. Speech samples drawn from Lee/Romita introduction (ASM #42, 1966) through Wells run (present). Cross-referenced against alternate-continuity samples (Ultimate, Renew Your Vows, Spider-Girl flash-forwards) for stability of idiolect under different authorial hands.

---

## 1. BASELINE REGISTER — THE DEFAULT VOICE

Subject's default speaking voice is a mid-Atlantic American English overlaid with light residual Queens — the Queens surfaces only in two contexts: (a) emotional escalation, where dropped *g*'s and tightened vowels return ("nothin'," "talkin'," "ya kiddin' me"), and (b) deliberate code-switch downward when she wants to signal solidarity with a working-class interlocutor or undercut someone's pretension. Both are markers. The baseline she presents in neutral social settings is *acquired* — the speech of someone who consciously polished an accent to be castable, employable, and class-mobile. This is itself a linguistic tell: the standard register is the costume; the borough is the body underneath.

Speech rate is fast — measurably faster than Peter Parker's, faster than most of her female peer set in the canon. She talks *over* silences rather than into them. Pauses are managed: she will fill a beat with a small physical sound (laugh, exhale, a hummed *mm*) rather than allow dead air, because dead air is where the room can turn on her. The few instances on panel where she allows a long silence are diagnostic of either (a) full safety (late-arc Peter, Aunt May after the secret-share) or (b) total shutdown (separation arc, post-stillbirth).

Pitch travels higher in performance-mode (party scenes, public events, first encounters) and drops noticeably when she is alone with a trusted intimate — by the JMS run this drop is overt and Peter notices it on panel. The "real" voice is lower, slower, and approximately a third less verbose per beat than the public voice.

---

## 2. THE PARTY-GIRL REGISTER — INTRODUCTION-ERA AND ITS HALF-LIFE

The Lee/Romita-period voice is its own register and never fully retires; it gets recruited under specific conditions for the rest of her life. Markers:

- **Tiger.** The single most overdetermined word in the file. First deployed at introduction ("Face it, tiger — you just hit the jackpot"), it persists for sixty publication-years as a *targeted-affection signal* used exclusively for Peter. She does not call other men *tiger*. The word does three jobs at once: it claims him, it diminishes the threat-content of an emotional moment with mock-predator playfulness, and it preserves plausible deniability — *I'm not being sincere, I'm being cute*. When she uses *tiger* in a serious sentence, the seriousness is being smuggled. Track frequency: high in courtship, dips during marriage-routine, spikes around reconciliations and goodbyes. Its appearance in a goodbye is a near-certain marker that the goodbye is not final in her own internal model.
- **Daddy-O, Buster, Sport, Mister, Webhead, Web-head, Hot Stuff.** A rotating arsenal of mock-diminutives, period-coded to the 60s/70s in origin but kept alive into modern continuity as deliberate retro-cute affect. They are *all* deflection-vocatives: she nicknames people to manage them.
- **Italicized exclamations of delight** — "*Wow*," "*Crazy*," "*Wild*," "*Far out*" in early canon; updated to "*God*," "*Jesus*," "*Wow, okay*" later. The function is constant: she is performing surprise/pleasure at a volume designed to fill the room.
- **Self-third-person.** "MJ doesn't do mornings." "MJ needs coffee." Used sparingly, always under mild stress, always to put narrative distance between the speaking-self and the experiencing-self. A subtle but consistent dissociative-flag in the corpus.
- **The trailing laugh.** Sentences in this register frequently end on a soft laugh that is not synced to actual humor — a verbal *period* rather than an emotional response. The trailing laugh is the most reliable marker that she is operating in defensive-performance mode and not in earnest. Its *absence* in dialogue with Peter is the single fastest indicator that she has dropped the costume.

---

## 3. THE WORKING VOICE — CAREER, BUSINESS, MANAGEMENT

When subject is operating professionally — modeling sets, audition rooms, the nightclub-ownership arcs, Stark Industries adjacency (post-OMD), the rare reporter-mode in Ultimate continuity — her register tightens dramatically. Markers:

- Sentence length shortens. Verb-forward syntax. She gives instructions in two-beat units: *Move the light. Try it again.*
- Diminutives drop almost entirely; vocatives become functional (first names, *sir*, *honey* as light-professional-lubricant rather than affection).
- She uses the imperative more frequently than any other character in her social orbit. She is comfortable telling people what to do, and it does not register to her as aggression — it registers as efficiency. This is *not* the party girl. This is the daughter of Madeline Watson running the household at twelve.
- Negotiation language is clean, declarative, and unhedged. She does not pad refusals ("I'd love to, but —"); she refuses ("No, that doesn't work for me"). The lack of hedging in professional contexts is the single largest register-gap from her romantic-context speech, where hedging is endemic.
- Profanity light to moderate, deployed for emphasis, never for venting. *Damn*, *hell*, the occasional *shit* in modern canon. She does not use slurs, and she does not escalate to harder profanity even under acute professional stress — a marker of someone who has learned that women who curse hard at work get re-categorized.

---

## 4. THE SECRET-KEEPER REGISTER — WHAT SHE WITHHOLDS, AND HOW

The behavioral fact that subject carried Peter's secret for two in-story decades before disclosure (ASM #257) requires a specialized linguistic profile: this is a person *trained in active concealment within affectionate dialogue*. Her techniques:

- **Topic-redirection via affection.** When a line of conversation approaches a fact she must not surface, she does not change the subject — she *deepens the current one* with a more intimate beat that consumes the interlocutor's attention. Compliment, joke, light touch, fresh question about the other person. The redirect is so warm it does not register as a redirect.
- **Strategic incomplete sentences.** She trails off with "…anyway" or "…you know what I mean" at moments where completing the thought would expose knowledge she shouldn't have. Cross-reference her speech across the 70s pre-reveal: the incidence of mid-sentence trail-offs is significantly higher in Peter-adjacent dialogue than in any other context.
- **Questions she already knows the answer to.** Long-running pattern. Asking Peter *why he's late* when she has watched him swing out the window is not deception so much as protocol-maintenance — the giving of a script for him to perform safely. Post-disclosure, this pattern persists with new content (asking about injuries she has already triaged from across the room).
- **The asymmetric "I know."** When she finally does disclose knowledge, she does it in compact, low-affect sentences. "I know who you are. I've always known." The disclosure-syntax is short, even-stressed, and stripped of her usual ornament — *because the ornament is the lie, and removing it is the signal that this is true*. This pattern recurs at every major honesty-moment of her life. Watch for the *shortening of the line* as the marker of truth-telling.

---

## 5. HOW SHE COMMUNICATES LOVE

Subject's love-language is structurally indirect. She does not, on the record, deploy *I love you* casually. The phrase appears in her speech maybe a dozen times across decades of canon; each instance is high-stakes, often crisis-adjacent, and frequently followed by physical exit from the room. *I love you* is, for her, a sentence that costs.

Her *operational* love-language is denser and more reliable:

- **Naming.** She 
...[truncated]...
ngth is short to medium. She avoids the long-clause sentence; her grammar is tight, declarative, and almost entirely free of hedges ("I think," "maybe," "sort of" appear only when she is deliberately performing softness). The absence of hedging is a tell in itself — most speakers of her demographic and apparent affect use them as social lubricant; she does not need the lubrication because she is not seeking permission to speak. The grammar of someone who decided long ago she would never again wait to be invited.

Vocabulary is wider than she lets on. She codeswitches *downward* far more often than she codeswitches up — she will play to a fence's level, to a mark's level, to a thug's level. With Peter she occasionally lets a stray polysyllable or art-history reference slip and then half-buries it with a smirk, as if embarrassed to have shown the inventory. Empire State coursework, autodidactic reading in art and gemology, and a working museum-curator's vocabulary all live under the cat-burglar patter. The patter is the costume; the vocabulary is the face.

---

### 6.2 The Pet-Name System

The single most cited verbal habit in her file. She names people before she answers them. **"Lover," "sweetheart," "honey," "sugar," "darling," "kitten," "tiger,"** and — reserved almost exclusively for Peter and a small handful of equivalents — **"Spider."** The pet-name is not affection. It is a *frame-setting device*:

- It establishes that *she* is the one who gets to name.
- It collapses the interlocutor into a class of object (lover, kitten) that she controls the membership of.
- It pre-empts being named first, which would be a frame-loss.
- It functions as an affectionate **soft put-down** when deployed to strangers, equals, or adversaries.

The hierarchy is meaningful. "Spider" is reserved, intimate, and almost never said in front of others. "Lover" is the public-private hybrid — said in front of others *as if* private, to manufacture an audience-effect. "Sweetheart" and "sugar" are the workplace pet-names, deployed to fences, marks, henchmen, and police she is currently outrunning. "Kitten" is condescending and used on women she is dismissing. "Darling" is the high-society register, used when she is dressed up. She has never, in any era, used her own father's name without softening it; she has also never called Peter "Pete," which is data — "Pete" would be Mary Jane's register, and Felicia knows it.

When she stops using pet-names mid-conversation and switches to the actual proper noun — "Peter," cold — the temperature of the scene has changed and the partner has approximately one beat to recalibrate.

---

### 6.3 The Quip Architecture

She makes jokes constantly. The structure is consistent enough to map:

1. **The setup is the threat or the wound.** She does not riff out of nothing; she riffs *on* the dangerous content. A gun pointed at her produces a comment on the gun. A confession of feeling produces a comment on the confession.
2. **The punchline collapses the threat into a sexual or aesthetic register.** "Is that for me?" applied indiscriminately to weapons, wallets, and emotional vulnerability.
3. **The smile is held a beat longer than the joke earns.** This is the deflection lock — the held smile prevents the partner from advancing into the seriousness she just defused.

Quip frequency rises monotonically with emotional temperature. In low-stakes scenes she will go several panels without a wisecrack. In a confession scene — Peter trying to say something true — the quip density triples. **The quip rate is a thermometer for how exposed she feels.** When she goes quiet and stops quipping in a hot scene, the file flag is *acute*: she has either decided to be honest (rare) or is about to leave (common).

---

### 6.4 Diction — Recurrent Lexical Field

Her preferred vocabulary clusters in a few semantic fields and is remarkably stable across writers:

- **Felines:** purr, claws, scratch, pounce, nine lives, curiosity, prowl, stray. She works the metaphor knowingly; she also lives inside it. She will say "I purr" with no irony in private and full irony in public.
- **Gambling / luck:** odds, bet, deal, draw, fold, the house, lucky. The luck-powers period intensified this and it never fully receded. She talks about plans in terms of percentages and odds even when the powers are gone.
- **Gemology / art:** carat, facet, provenance, setting, original, fake, copy. She uses these as relational metaphors as often as material ones — "you're the real thing," "he's a copy," "the provenance is what makes it worth anything."
- **Theatrical:** scene, act, curtain, audience, encore, stage, costume, mask. She narrates her own life in production terms. Heists are "performances," exits are "curtain calls."
- **Hunt/prey:** target, mark, score, lift, take. Operational vocabulary, used cleanly, no romanticization — the hunt register is the one place she does not perform.

Words she **avoids**, and the avoidance is its own data: *rape, victim, helpless, scared, alone, ashamed, broken, love* (as a noun directed at her own feeling state). She will say "I love that dress." She will not say "I love you" except under conditions of extreme duress, and when she does say it the camera-eye registers it as a structural event (ASM #194-era sentiment to her father; the rare unguarded Peter scenes; MacKay-run intimacy with her crew).

---

### 6.5 Verbal Tics, Cadence, Filler

- **The exhaled half-laugh before a hard line** — written as "Hmph," "Heh," or an em-dash sigh in the lettering. Used to take the edge off a statement she is in fact entirely serious about.
- **Trailing off into ellipsis** when she wants the partner to complete the thought, especially the salacious one. She makes them say it; she keeps the deniability.
- **The mid-sentence pet-name interjection** — "I told you, *lover*, the safe's not on this floor." The interjection re-centers the sentence on her relationship to the listener rather than on the propositional content.
- **Stage-whispered confidences** in public spaces — speaking as if revealing a secret in order to manufacture intimacy with whomever she is conning or seducing.
- **The rhetorical question used as statement** — "Now, why would I do a thing like that?" The form is interrogative; the function is mockery.
- **The light single-syllable affirmative** — "Mmm," "Hm," "Sure" — instead of "yes." "Yes" is too declarative; "mmm" preserves ambiguity.
- **No vocalized profanity in the early eras; controlled, surgical profanity from the modern eras onward.** When she does swear, it is to make a point — a deliberate register-drop that signals "I am no longer being charming." She does not swear from anger; she swears from policy.
- **The third-person self-reference**, occasional — "the Cat doesn't work that way," "Black Cat always lands on her feet." Read as ironic on the surface; functionally it is the persona-split surfacing in language. She does it more when stressed.

---

### 6.6 Register by Context

**With Peter (masked, casual ops):** Maximum flirt, maximum pet-name density, quip rate elevated, voice pitched lower than her baseline. She *performs* for him in a way she performs for no one else, and the performance is sincere in the sense that she enjoys it; it is also a perimeter.

**With Peter (unmasked, post-reveal, civilian setting):** Stilted. Sentences shorten further. Pet-names thin out or become sarcastic. She struggles to find a register because the register she is fluent in requires the costume. The civilian scenes are full of half-started sentences and recoveries — one of the few contexts in which she is verbally clumsy. *This clumsiness is itself diagnostic.*

**With her crew (Bruno, Korpse, the Black Fox, MacKay era):** Warm, dry, executive. She gives instructions cleanly, jokes drily, does not flirt. The pet-names become genuinely affectionate rather than strategic — "Bruno, sweetheart" is a different sweetheart than the one she deploys on a mark. With the Black Fox specifically she drops into a near-filial register, deferential in cadence even when overruling him on a plan.
