bash -lc mkdir -p /mnt/data/earth_gk_work; cat > /mnt/data/earth_gk_work/manuscript.txt <<'EOF'
###|00|The Watcher Keeps a Count
N|On Earth-GK, the first lie about any disaster is usually the smallest one. A wrong street number. A unit marked clear while it is still wiping blood off its floor. A supervisor saying there are thirty-one people in a building because thirty-one is the number on the sheet.
N|From a distance sufficient to contain a galaxy, Uatu watched the eastern coast of North America turn the color of a healing bruise. A storm was moving over New York. It was a weather event, not an omen. The distinction mattered. Omens excused people from checking the wiring.
N|He had observed stars fall inward. He had observed empires invent astonishingly persuasive reasons not to rescue their own citizens. He had also observed exhausted creatures in cheap uniforms make decisions that deserved monuments, then go home to eat cereal over the sink because the laundromat was closed.
N|The Watcher did not intervene.
N|He did, however, keep the count.
N|At 2:17 in the morning, the official count at the Harbor Street Annex was thirty-one.
N|It was wrong.
###|01|Three Bells and No Coffee
N|At 2:17 a caller reported smoke at a service door beneath the Harbor Street Interchange. At 2:19 someone reported a woman screaming from a stairwell. At 2:21 a third caller said there were children behind a locked door and the lights were going out.
N|At 2:23, Captain Elena Ortiz of city EMS put down the coffee she had just purchased and told her driver to take the corner gently. There was no lid on the cup. There never was when it mattered.
ORTIZ|[dry]|"If this is another transformer smoking in a puddle, I am going to marry a dentist."
N|Paramedic Nate Bell, thirty-two, handsome in the way people were handsome before night work rearranged their faces, turned the wheel.
BELL|[lightly]|"Can I come to the wedding?"
ORTIZ|[dry]|"Not if you drive like that."
N|She had been in emergency medicine long enough to distrust both a quiet radio and an exciting one. What she trusted was a pattern. Three independent callers, one location, three different descriptions: that was a pattern. The number on the dispatch screen was just a number.
N|Under the interchange, Lieutenant Ruth Finch of the NYPD had already put two officers on the public entrance and another on the service lane. She had the blunt posture of someone who did not ask a crowd to cooperate twice. Her first officer was young enough to be worried about doing the right thing in front of the right people. Her second had worked double overtime and was worried about getting home.
FINCH|[commanding]|"Nobody goes through that gate unless fire says it's safe. I mean nobody. Cameras, politicians, and anybody who tells you they know a commissioner."
N|The fire department arrived with engine crews, a truck company, and Battalion Chief Thomas Rourke, who emerged from his command vehicle looking at the building as if it had insulted his mother. The south entrance breathed gray smoke. Somewhere beyond it, an alarm gave two notes, gave up, and began again.
ROURKE|[controlled]|"What do we know?"
FINCH|[matter-of-fact]|"Three calls. Security says thirty-one on site. One employee says the lower doors close automatically at midnight."
ROURKE|[flat]|"Did anyone ask the doors how many people they think are inside?"
N|Finch did not smile. She did write something down.
N|Spider-Man arrived from above, inverted on a streetlight, thirty seconds after the second engine. He had come to the scene because his phone said fire and his instincts said worse. Both were generally expensive opinions to ignore.
PETER|[bright, forced]|"Good morning, New York. Love what you've done with the smoke. Very industrial. Little much for a Tuesday."
N|Rourke looked up without changing expression.
ROURKE|[dry]|"Are you here to help or to do commentary?"
PETER|[chastened]|"Usually both. I'm trying to diversify."
N|Ortiz walked past them with the oxygen bag and a marker board. She had no interest in a stranger's branding, not even a stranger who could stick to walls.
ORTIZ|[working]|"Chief, I need a casualty collection point away from the smoke. I need the count to stay provisional. And when you get eyes on the lower level, I need to hear what you see, not what security says you ought to see."
N|Rourke nodded. It was the kind of exchange that happens between people who have spent years watching other people improvise certainty.
N|A security supervisor in a spotless navy polo approached from the side gate. His laminated badge said VANE COMMUNITY OPERATIONS. His shoes had not encountered the kind of night everyone else's had.
SECURITY|[insistent]|"There are thirty-one employees authorized for the overnight maintenance shift. We have a controlled occupancy schedule."
ORTIZ|[even]|"That's nice. How many people are inside?"
SECURITY|[impatient]|"I just told you."
ORTIZ|[cold]|"You told me what your computer believes. I'm asking about the people."
N|The supervisor looked toward Rourke, assuming he could find a more congenial kind of authority. The chief kept his attention on the smoke.
N|From inside, metal struck metal three times. It was followed by the ragged sound of somebody screaming a name.
N|Peter's joke supply stopped.
PETER|[quiet]|"There are more than thirty-one."
N|Rourke glanced up again. This time he listened.
###|02|The Party Upstairs
N|Forty-three floors above the problem, Mary Jane Watson was directing the disassembly of a charity gala with the calm fury of a woman who had been promised a working freight elevator. The event's sponsor, the Vane Foundation, had spent an obscene amount of money celebrating a new program called Safe Harbor. The press release used the words resilient and community eleven times. Not once did it mention the overnight workers beneath the ballroom.
N|MJ had booked the performers, hired the caterers, negotiated the security plan, and kept a room full of donors from discovering how close they were to the people who actually built the city. Her job was to make chaos look intentional. She was very good at it. Her private opinion of donors who said they were changing the world while complaining about room-temperature champagne was not fit for publication.
N|Her assistant brought her a phone showing live footage from Harbor Street. Smoke rolled under the interchange. A firefighter carried a folded stretcher toward a door.
ASSISTANT|[worried]|"The sponsor's asking whether we should postpone the closing photos."
MJ|[crisp]|"There are no closing photos. Get the staff out. Pay everybody for the full night. And tell the sponsor that if he wants a quote he can go down there and give one standing beside the ambulances."
N|Her phone vibrated. Peter. Not a call: a six-word message, because he knew a call might let her hear the truth in his breathing. She read it twice.
N|Harbor Street. I'm there. Don't worry.
MJ|[under her breath]|"Oh, tiger."
N|She called him. No answer. She called a second time, not because she expected the phone to work differently but because repetition was her least favorite old habit and still the easiest to reach for.
N|Across the street from the gala, Felicia Hardy had not attended the fundraiser. She had attended the building. There was a difference. She had entered a service lift two hours earlier, wearing an expensive dress and a guest badge nobody had thought to verify because the dress was expensive. She had left the dress in a maintenance closet, changed into black, and reached the records room beneath the interchange before the first alarm.
N|A contractor had stored two sets of occupancy records. Felicia was interested in the set they did not show the fire marshal. A friend of a friend had asked her to retrieve an original ledger. His price had been embarrassingly generous. His reason had been less persuasive. Felicia had said yes because the job was interesting, then spent two nights wondering why she had said yes so quickly.
N|At 2:17, she was photographing a paper log when the ventilation changed pitch. At 2:19, the emergency lights blinked. At 2:21, a reinforced corridor door engaged behind her with a sound that was much too final.
FELICIA|[amused, thin]|"Well. That's a poor design choice."
N|Her earpiece carried Bruno's voice from a parked van three streets north.
BRUNO|[tense]|"Cat? That is an actual fire alarm. We are done."
FELICIA|[casual]|"I noticed the alarm part, sweetheart. I'm researching the done part."
N|She looked at the page in her hand. Shift sheet, visitor passes, temporary shelter authorization. The top sheet held thirty-one names. Under it, the carbon paper carried impressions of more. A lot more.
N|In the service corridor a small voice asked, from somewhere near the floor, whether she was a firefighter.
N|Felicia turned. Behind a glass panel, in a space somebody had once designed for storage carts, a girl in a red sweatshirt stood with one hand wrapped around the wrist of a boy younger than she was. A third person, an older custodian, sat against the wall struggling to keep his eyes open. A lock light glowed red above their door.
GIRL|[trying not to cry]|"Are you a firefighter?"
N|Felicia knew three answers. None of them was useful.
FELICIA|[warm, precise]|"No, darling. I'm very good at doors. That's what you need right now. What's your name?"
GIRL|[small]|"Ivy. This is Leo. That's Mr. Cho."
FELICIA|[gentle]|"Ivy, I need you to tell me whether there's a second way out of your room."
GIRL|[scared]|"There was. It closed."
N|Felicia checked the frame. A fire-rated door; no reachable release on their side. Whatever had failed tonight had failed in several directions at once.
FELICIA|[low]|"Okay. I have eyes on you. Keep talking to me."
N|The girl pressed her palm against the glass. Felicia put her own hand opposite it. She did not feel romantic about danger. She could read a clock, and she could smell the smoke.
N|She turned her earpiece back on.
FELICIA|[controlled]|"Bruno, change of plans. Send the fire people to the lower east corridor, storage level two. Three civilians. One's a kid. Actually, two kids."
BRUNO|[alarmed]|"Felicia, you need to leave."
FELICIA|[quiet]|"I heard you. Now tell them."
###|03|No Flames in a Fire
N|Johnny Storm landed in the street wearing a tuxedo jacket over a T-shirt whose price he would have denied. Three cameras pivoted toward him as if the city had built him for this exact purpose. He gave them a grin and an easy thumbs-up. Under the grin he counted the ambulances.
JOHNNY|[showman]|"Okay, everybody, good news. Your extremely handsome backup has arrived. Bad news: apparently I have been booked for a basement."
N|A fire lieutenant wearing a facepiece around his neck pointed at the smoke.
FIRE_LT|[practical]|"If you light up inside that space you can make an ugly situation worse. No freelancing."
JOHNNY|[mildly offended]|"I know how fire works. It's a whole brand."
FIRE_LT|[dry]|"Then this is gonna be a relaxing conversation."
N|Johnny had wanted to help. It was one of the irritating things about him: the impulse was sincere, even when the packaging made everybody want to throw something at his head. He looked at the entry team staging beside the engine. Their breathing apparatus, their tools, their hands checking each other's gear. He had watched the Fantastic Four do versions of that ritual a thousand times. He had not always noticed the part where somebody kept count.
N|The chief brought him into the circle.
ROURKE|[commanding]|"Storm. You're useful on the exterior. You can see the roof and the service courtyard. You are not my ventilation plan. You tell me what you find."
JOHNNY|[subdued]|"Copy that."
N|He rose without igniting. In the air he felt almost naked. His body's normal heat was a small thing compared with what he could unleash, and for once more power was not the answer. The roof had split in a diagonal above the east service run. A metal housing shook with every underground impact. The place looked less like a fire than a machine taking a final breath.
N|He reported exactly what he saw. No spin, no clever words. When Rourke asked whether the roof was moving, he said yes. When asked how fast, he said he didn't know. He hated that last part. It was also the right answer.
N|On the north side, Wade Wilson climbed out of a black SUV carrying a courier bag, two swords, and the peculiar fatigue of a man who had chosen a profession that required entirely too many pockets. He had been hired by someone from Vane's legal office to retrieve an encrypted drive before the scene went under public control. The job description had used the phrase business continuity. Wade had seen a lot of unpleasant work conducted under the protection of boring nouns.
WADE|[cheerfully bleak]|"Ah, a citywide gathering of people who didn't get invited to the fun party. My favorite reunion. Is this a fire, a lawsuit, or the pilot episode of a lawsuit?"
N|Finch intercepted him outside the perimeter.
FINCH|[flat]|"You're not going in."
WADE|[winningly]|"I'm with the worst possible people, and I am prepared to show you a business card."
FINCH|[flat]|"You can show it to a different officer from over there."
N|Wade looked across the lane, toward Ortiz. Two small children were being brought to the casualty collection area by an engine crew. One had vomit on her shoes. One kept asking about a father who was still inside. Ortiz crouched to the child's level, her tone changed, and the rest of the scene stopped mattering for a second.
ORTIZ|[soft]|"You're in the right place now. I'm going to stay right here while we figure out your dad."
N|Wade's grin stayed in place. His eyes did not. He turned to the suited man who had hired him, waiting in the SUV.
WADE|[pleasant]|"So, when you said retrieve the asset, would that be before or after we retrieve the children?"
N|The man looked away from the kids.
CLIENT|[firm]|"Our instructions are specific."
WADE|[soft, dangerous]|"So is my question."
N|For one long beat, Wade was perfectly still. Then he took the encrypted drive case from the client's hand and set it on the hood of the police car. He did not draw a weapon. He did not need to. Finch saw the exchange and picked up the case without touching the handle.
FINCH|[even]|"I will take custody of that."
WADE|[light]|"Excellent. Please put down 'delivered by the world's most regrettable messenger.'"
FINCH|[deadpan]|"I was going to put 'male, unknown, loud.'"
WADE|[delighted]|"The accuracy. It hurts."
N|High above, Johnny heard a child crying through an open ventilation slot. He hovered, listening. He had no problem with flame. He had a problem with being seen without it.
JOHNNY|[into comms, stripped]|"Chief, there's somebody below the south vent. Small voice. Maybe two. I'm staying here."
N|It surprised him to hear how glad he sounded about the last sentence.
###|04|The Geometry of an Exit
N|Peter entered through an opening a fire crew had already cleared. He did not try to outrun them; he was growing reluctantly competent at learning the difference between speed and useful speed. The first level smelled of wet cement and scorched insulation. His spider-sense kept insisting that the building was a problem too large to punch.
N|A firefighter shouted directions. He gave back the location he was heading to, because Ortiz had taught him once, with the savage patience of someone teaching a child not to touch a stove, that being hard to track was not an endearing quality at a rescue scene.
PETER|[radio voice]|"Moving toward the east storage corridor. I'll call back before I change levels."
N|He moved through a hall where emergency lights made every puddle look like spilled blood. He heard a metal tapping sound. Three strikes, a pause, three strikes. He followed it to a sealed door. Behind the glass, Felicia looked at him with a smile that had been engineered to survive most forms of trouble.
FELICIA|[dry]|"Spider. Did you come all the way down here to take me out? The restaurant is terrible."
PETER|[too fast]|"I could complain about the service, but I think the fire department already has. You okay?"
FELICIA|[quiet]|"I'm fine. The children aren't."
N|The smile thinned. Peter saw Ivy and Leo behind the second door and Mr. Cho slumped against the wall. A ribbon of smoke dragged past the ceiling tiles. He tried the nearest frame. It did not yield. Not an ordinary lock. The entire system had warped under heat.
N|Peter could have torn into it. He could have taken the wall apart. He could also have pulled down the ceiling onto the three people he wanted to save. That was the first genuinely difficult thing about the room: strength had become a liability.
PETER|[working, clipped]|"I can reach them if I get the hinge side free. I need a little time."
FELICIA|[crisp]|"You have some. Not much. Mr. Cho's breathing is getting ugly."
N|Peter knelt to the glass. He could see Ivy's face in the reflection of his mask.
PETER|[soft, no jokes]|"Hey, Ivy. I'm Spider-Man. You've probably heard of me. The short version is that I'm pretty good at getting people out of bad rooms. I need you to sit with Leo on the floor, away from the door. Can you do that?"
IVY|[small]|"Are you going to break it?"
PETER|[gentle]|"Maybe a little. Not toward you."
N|Ivy nodded. She was trying not to look afraid because her brother was watching. Peter had seen adults work themselves into the same posture. It never became less terrible when you recognized it.
N|Felicia leaned close to him while he examined the frame.
FELICIA|[almost inaudible]|"Please don't make me ask twice."
N|Peter glanced at her. There were very few people alive who knew how rare that sentence was. He did not answer with a joke.
N|Above them, the building groaned. They both looked at the ceiling. Peter's instincts screamed. A line of dust spilled along the wall, then stopped.
PETER|[into radio]|"East storage level two. Four persons with me, three behind a fire door. Structural movement overhead. I need the entry crew to know we're down here."
N|Rourke's reply came with static and the unwilling authority of a man responsible for other people's parents.
ROURKE|[radio]|"Copy. We have movement on the east roof. Do not compromise the wall. Confirm you have a way back."
PETER|[hesitates]|"I have the corridor."
ROURKE|[sharp]|"That wasn't the question."
N|Felicia put a hand on Peter's wrist. She wasn't stopping him. She was making him look at the corridor he had come through. Smoke had begun to collect there, denser now, down to the height of a man's chest. The way out existed. Its usefulness was changing.
FELICIA|[low]|"Spider. We need a second exit."
N|Peter looked toward the locked partition, then the old service hatch beside it. The hatch wasn't an exit to the street, but it connected to a maintenance shaft. Felicia saw him understand. She also saw the part of him that wanted to do both things at once.
FELICIA|[firm]|"One problem at a time. The children first."
N|He obeyed. There were times when the most intimate thing another person could do was give you an order you could actually follow.

###|05|Everybody Has an Assignment
N|At 3:04, the incident had a command post, a casualty collection area, a perimeter, and more people wearing command vests than Ortiz considered spiritually necessary. It did not have a reliable occupancy count. It also did not have enough time.
N|An engine crew brought out Mr. Cho on a carried stretcher. Peter had opened the storage partition without dropping the ceiling, and Felicia had guided Ivy and Leo through the narrow gap ahead of the firefighters. The kids had come out holding hands so tightly that they had left half-moon marks on each other's skin.
N|Ortiz met them at the stairwell. She did not praise anybody's courage. She checked the children, handed them to a waiting medic, and turned to Mr. Cho.
ORTIZ|[working]|"Mr. Cho, my name's Elena. You're outside. I'm going to ask you some questions, and some of them are going to sound stupid."
CHO|[breathless]|"Is the little girl..."
ORTIZ|[steady]|"I have her. She's right there."
N|He relaxed enough to cough. Ortiz took the improvement for what it was worth, and nothing more.
N|Peter stood behind the engine crew with dust in the seams of his suit. He watched the children disappear into the bright rectangle of an ambulance. It should have been a good moment. It was a good moment. He felt almost nothing. A good moment, to him, was a receipt that there were more people he had not found yet.
N|Captain Ortiz reached back without looking at him and handed him a bottle of water.
ORTIZ|[flat]|"Drink."
PETER|[automatic]|"I'm fine."
ORTIZ|[flat]|"That wasn't a diagnosis."
N|He drank, which proved that miracles still occurred on earth.
N|Mary Jane arrived through the outer perimeter with a contract binder under one arm and rain in her hair. She had spent ten minutes persuading someone in a city office to send her the actual occupancy agreement and another five persuading a deputy police commissioner that the event manager of Vane's new Safe Harbor initiative was more useful in the street than in the ballroom.
N|Finch met her beside the press vans.
FINCH|[skeptical]|"You have ten seconds to explain why I should listen."
MJ|[businesslike]|"I organized the donor event for the company that runs this annex. I have the program contract. Their public count includes licensed workers. Their supplementary agreement allows temporary cooling access for contract staff and families. Those are not the same population."
FINCH|[alert]|"How many?"
MJ|[careful]|"I don't know yet. That's why I'm here."
N|Finch had been lied to very smoothly in her career. She recognized the novelty of someone important declining to guess.
FINCH|[short]|"Come with me. No live broadcast. No interfering with medical."
MJ|[even]|"Agreed."
N|Inside the command post, Rourke listened while MJ laid out the discrepancy. A representative from Vane's public affairs office tried to correct her wording twice. On the third attempt, Ortiz interrupted him.
ORTIZ|[hard]|"You can decide what to call the building in court. I need to know if there are sixty people in it or thirty."
N|The representative began to explain that his office had not approved any overnight shelter function. Ortiz's face went still.
ORTIZ|[low]|"That sounds like a separate conversation."
N|Wade stood at the edge of the tent, now unarmed except for a truly devastating quantity of misplaced confidence. Finch had made him surrender his swords to a supervisor, who seemed offended by their existence. Wade was studying the operations board. On the board, a magnetic strip marked UNACCOUNTED FOR. There were no names underneath it.
WADE|[uncharacteristically gentle]|"You should put a bigger magnet there."
N|Ortiz turned toward him.
ORTIZ|[suspicious]|"Why?"
WADE|[quiet]|"Because that list is going to get heavy."
N|Outside, a firefighter named Leon Arroyo was handing a recovered plush rabbit to an ambulance crew. It was wet and missing an eye. He tucked it in a plastic patient-belongings bag and wrote the location where he had found it on the label.
N|He had two children at home, a daughter and a son. He also had a habit of keeping hard candy in the pocket of his station pants for pediatric calls. Both facts mattered to him in the same ordinary, untheatrical way. He had arrived believing he would see them again after shift. None of the people around him had reason to think otherwise.
N|Rourke gave his crew assignments. Arroyo checked the seal on his mask and stepped into the entrance with his partner. No one played music. No one made a speech. There was simply work, and then more work.
###|06|The Door Does Not Love You Back
N|At 3:28 the east mezzanine lost its lights. At 3:31 a section of old roof decking began to move. At 3:34, Johnny Storm reported from the exterior that the movement was no longer a maybe.
JOHNNY|[urgent, radio]|"Chief, it's shifting toward the service stair. You've got seconds, not minutes."
N|Rourke ordered the interior crews to withdraw to the next safe area and called for an accountability check. On the radio, people answered in clipped bursts: location, partner, status. Arroyo's team did not answer the first time.
N|Peter was two levels down, beside the mouth of a maintenance shaft. He had found another cluster of occupants following a water pipe toward what they hoped was fresh air: a woman with a damaged ankle, two delivery riders, and a man who said he was all right in the firm, insulted voice people sometimes use when they are not remotely all right.
N|Peter had four people moving toward a safe exit and a fifth person somewhere beyond a fallen mesh gate. The fifth was calling to him in Spanish and coughing between sentences. Peter could understand enough to recognize the word sister.
N|His radio crackled.
ROURKE|[radio, hard]|"Spider-Man, withdraw. Now. We have potential structural failure on your level."
PETER|[quick]|"I have one more just behind this gate. I can reach her."
ROURKE|[hard]|"Negative. We are losing the stair. Get the four out."
N|It was a command from a person responsible for more than Peter's conscience. Peter heard it. He also heard the person calling beyond the gate. His mind did what it always did when a choice hurt: it searched for a third choice in which nobody paid.
PETER|[breathless]|"Give me twenty seconds."
N|Twenty seconds was the lie Peter told most often. There were people to whom he had promised a lifetime with equal sincerity and less confidence.
N|Felicia appeared at the shaft entrance, her hair full of dust, the old occupancy ledger tucked inside her jacket. She had been on her way out. She stopped when she saw the way he was looking at the gate.
FELICIA|[flat]|"No."
PETER|[stunned]|"Felicia, I can see her."
FELICIA|[firmer]|"I know. And you have four people behind you who cannot move without you."
N|He turned. The woman with the injured ankle was leaning on one of the delivery riders, both of them staring at him as though he were the only exit that existed. He had not noticed that he was the thing holding them in place.
N|For a terrible instant, Peter hated Felicia. Not because she was wrong. Because she was the person he could trust to be right when being right was unbearable.
PETER|[low, breaking]|"I can't leave her."
FELICIA|[very quiet]|"You can't make her your permission to lose them."
N|A sound like an enormous fist striking the roof moved through the passage. The floor trembled. Peter acted. He secured the path for the four and sent them up the shaft ahead of him. Felicia followed, counting them aloud. Peter stayed at the bottom until the last pair of feet was clear. Then he climbed.
N|On the opposite side of the mezzanine, Arroyo had found a worker collapsed near the stair. He and his partner were bringing her out when the overhead decking failed. The partner cleared the opening. Arroyo didn't. The radio recorded a thump, a curse, and then an open channel that nobody wanted to hear.
N|Johnny descended toward the damaged roof and for a moment almost ignited. Fire was his most familiar answer to fear. The impulse was so deeply learned that it took effort to keep it in his chest. He could have burned a pathway through debris. He did not know what that would do to the people beneath it.
N|He planted both feet against the exterior steel and braced the portion that was still moving. His own muscles did not provide the force. His powers did, in quiet ways the cameras would never be able to photograph. The metal stopped shifting long enough for firefighters to reach the edge of the collapse.
JOHNNY|[radio, stripped]|"I'm holding it. Whatever you've got to do, do it now."
N|Rourke answered without ceremony.
ROURKE|[commanding]|"Copy. Rescue crew moving. Stay on that point."
N|The strain hurt. Johnny had never been entirely sure whether the things he did not feel while burning would be waiting for him when the fire went out. This time he was already out. He had to feel every second.
N|Arroyo came out alive. He was conscious long enough to ask whether his partner had cleared the stair. Ortiz heard the answer. She also heard the change in his voice when he tried to take another breath. She went to work with her team. People brought equipment. People moved. People did the exact things they had trained for. The work did not negotiate with the outcome.
N|Later, there would be an argument about whether calling him a hero was a kindness or a way of not talking about why the roof had failed. For now there was only the effort to keep Leon Arroyo alive.
N|In the stairwell, Peter reached the surface with the fourth civilian and looked back toward the gate where the fifth person had been calling. The building settled with a noise like distant thunder. He stood motionless until Felicia put her hand between his shoulder blades.
FELICIA|[soft]|"Come outside."
N|He did. It did not feel like a victory.
###|07|Sixty-Eight
N|By 4:12 the smoke had thinned in the service courtyard and grown heavier inside. Nobody was calling the event controlled. In the command tent, the old security supervisor was still defending the figure thirty-one, now with the dedicated determination of a man who had decided that being correct and being safe were interchangeable.
SECURITY|[defensive]|"Those other entries are not authorized occupancy. They are subcontractor access notes. Some could be duplicates."
N|Felicia set her photographed records beside MJ's binder. She did it carefully, the way she handled a piece of jewelry she had decided not to keep.
FELICIA|[cool]|"They're handwritten, signed, and time-stamped. There's a carbon copy underneath your official sheet. If you're planning to call them duplicates, darling, you should probably try not to say it in front of the detective."
N|Finch examined the documents without pretending she could tell their whole history from a glance. She asked Felicia where they had come from. Felicia met her eyes.
FELICIA|[matter-of-fact]|"From a locked records room below street level. I entered without permission. You can put that in the report."
N|Finch's expression barely changed. It cost Felicia more than anyone in the tent understood to make that admission in a room where she could not control the terms.
FINCH|[deliberate]|"I am going to need the original, every copy you made, and your statement about how you obtained it. We'll preserve the questions about admissibility. Right now the rescue count comes first."
N|MJ laid out two sheets side by side. One was the official nightly occupancy roster. Thirty-one. The second was an access ledger showing sixty-eight distinct entries, including maintenance workers, temporary cooling-center guests, and dependents. There were names written as initials; there were names in three languages. In one column someone had used little stars to mark children.
MJ|[working]|"This isn't a complete head count. It's a list of people who may have been there. But we can cross-check calls, names from survivors, and the ambulance destinations. We cannot keep reporting thirty-one."
N|Finch gave an officer the first half of the task. Ortiz assigned a medic to the second. Neither of them asked MJ to take command of a function she was not qualified to run. MJ did not ask. She knew the difference between being useful and taking over.
N|On the other side of the perimeter, Wade watched the security supervisor try to quietly delete something on his phone. He could have taken the phone. He could have taken the man apart. Both options were available to him, and one of them was disturbingly tempting.
N|Instead he called Finch over and told her exactly what he had observed. She directed the supervisor to stop handling potential evidence. An officer documented the device and the exchange. Wade kept his hands in his pockets.
WADE|[to himself, almost impressed]|"Look at me, doing the boring responsible thing. Somebody get a camera."
N|The joke made it out of his mouth. The relief did not reach the rest of him.
N|At 4:37 an emergency physician at the receiving hospital called Ortiz about Leon Arroyo. She listened, asked one question, and sat down on an equipment case with the phone still in her hand. Nate Bell stood next to her and did not say anything. He knew the silence.
BELL|[low]|"Captain?"
ORTIZ|[even, exhausted]|"He didn't make it."
N|Bell shut his eyes. Then he picked up the marker board and wrote the fact where someone could later transfer it into a record. He did not use a euphemism. Neither did Ortiz.
N|Arroyo was not on the occupant list. He was on an entirely different list, one that would pass through departments, insurance offices, investigations, and eventually a family who would learn to hate the sound of a doorbell. People would say he died doing what he loved. Ortiz hoped none of them would say it within earshot of his children.
N|Johnny returned from the roof and stood at the command tent's edge, black marks on his hands where he'd braced the steel. A photographer asked for a picture in front of the damaged entrance. Johnny looked at the lens, then at the ambulance bay, then back at the lens.
JOHNNY|[flat]|"Not tonight."
N|It was three words. For once, he did not offer a second sentence to make the first easier to receive.
N|At 5:06 the revised working list contained sixty-eight names. It had names verified through eyewitness accounts, records, calls, and hospital cross-checks. The staff marked each entry with the source of confirmation. Some were still uncertain. Nobody crossed a person off just because the line was inconvenient.
N|One hundred and sixty miles above the city, a weather satellite recorded the first light of dawn. It had no interest in any of this. Below it, six people were confirmed dead inside the annex. Fifty-eight were confirmed alive. Four remained unlocated, their status unresolved. The list was not yet a conclusion. It was the best account anyone could defend.
###|08|The Five Minutes After
N|At 8:40 the next morning, the first television summary used the phrase miraculous rescue. It showed Spider-Man climbing out of a doorway carrying a man, Johnny Storm suspended in the storm-light, and police officers guiding a line of survivors across the street. It showed none of the people who had checked the air, held a stair, started an intravenous line, or gone back into a bad room with ordinary human skin.
N|Peter saw the segment playing above a diner counter and asked the waitress to change the channel. She obliged because the television was hers and because the person asking looked like he had slept in a gravel pit. He was wearing a borrowed sweatshirt, not a mask. The bruises on his jaw had stopped being interesting to his body hours earlier.
N|MJ was waiting in a corner booth. She had a plate of eggs and toast in front of the empty seat opposite her. She hadn't ordered for him out of habit. She'd ordered because she knew the kitchen would close the breakfast line before he admitted he was hungry.
MJ|[quiet]|"Sit down, Peter."
PETER|[weakly joking]|"It's a very demanding seat. Do I need a reservation?"
MJ|[steady]|"Sit."
N|He sat. His hands hovered over the coffee without lifting it. She let the silence go on long enough that it ceased to be a performance.
PETER|[small]|"I left somebody."
N|MJ did not correct the wording, though she could have. He needed to hear his own sentence before she gave him a different one.
MJ|[low]|"Tell me what happened."
N|He told her about the gate, the four people behind him, the voice beyond it, and the roof coming down. It took a long time because he kept explaining things nobody had accused him of. MJ listened without becoming his judge. When he finished, she looked at the untouched toast and pushed it a little closer.
MJ|[soft]|"I believe you. And I believe the four who came out because you moved. Both things can be true."
PETER|[brittle]|"That doesn't make it feel any better."
MJ|[plain]|"I didn't say it would."
N|She reached across the table and rested a hand next to his. Not on top of it. He could choose.
MJ|[serious]|"I am not going to be your personal emergency department, tiger. I can love you. I can show up. I cannot make every terrible call come out clean so you'll finally sleep."
N|He stared at their hands. The gap between them was a small one, but he had crossed whole cities more easily.
PETER|[low]|"Are you leaving?"
MJ|[honest]|"I'm having breakfast with you. Don't turn that into a lifetime contract or a farewell."
N|At the far end of the same diner, Felicia was drinking tea she had no intention of finishing. She had refused an opportunity to vanish with the ledger and the security firm's contact list, and the fact that the refusal had been her own was both comforting and offensive. Her crew had pulled her from the scene, fed her, and told her to sleep. She had accomplished none of those things gracefully.
N|Wade occupied the stool beside her. He had ordered pancakes and dismantled a broken wind-up robot someone had left on the counter. The owner had said it belonged to his kid and didn't work anymore. Wade was trying to make it work, although his hands were not built for very small screws.
WADE|[bright]|"You ever notice how everyone wants a hero until the hero's billing department arrives?"
FELICIA|[dry]|"No, but I notice you're putting the spring in backward."
WADE|[offended]|"I am creating suspense."
N|She corrected the spring without taking the toy away from him. It was an oddly intimate thing to permit.
WADE|[quiet, trying for humor]|"What do you do with the ones you didn't get?"
N|Felicia kept her eyes on the little machine. She knew he was not talking about toys.
FELICIA|[unadorned]|"You remember their names. You don't use them to excuse what you do next."
N|Wade was silent for four seconds. It felt like a medical event.
WADE|[soft]|"Well, that's horribly inconvenient."
N|A few neighborhoods away, Johnny had returned to the Baxter Building. He stood in a kitchen that was too clean and listened to a message from his sister. Sue had asked him to call. He called, then almost hung up before she answered.
SUE|[warm, cautious]|"Johnny?"
JOHNNY|[attempting lightness]|"Hey, Sis. The good news is I didn't set the whole city on fire."
SUE|[quiet]|"I saw."
N|He waited for her to ask about the footage, the roof, the heroics. She didn't.
SUE|[gently]|"Are you home?"
JOHNNY|[barely audible]|"Yeah."
SUE|[soft]|"Okay."
N|He leaned against the counter and, for the first time in hours, stopped moving. Somewhere across the city, Ortiz filled out the beginning of an incident report. She wrote what they had found, what they had done, what they knew, and what they did not. The little space under the heading NARRATIVE seemed offensively small.
N|She wrote anyway.
###|09|The Parts That Make It Into the Record
N|Three weeks later the Vane Foundation suspended its Safe Harbor program pending an investigation. The company said it was cooperating fully. The word fully was doing work no human being had authorized.
N|Investigators obtained conflicting occupancy records, maintenance logs, and access-control histories. The evidence supported an important fact: the overnight shelter function had been broader than the official documents described. The question of who knew what, and when they knew it, remained under investigation. That distinction frustrated the public. It also mattered.
N|Felicia gave a statement with an attorney. She admitted where she had been and what she had taken. She did not apologize for rescuing the children; nor did she ask anyone to pretend her burglary had become legal because the night ended badly. Finch found the honesty inconvenient and preferable to the alternatives.
N|MJ terminated her commercial relationship with the Vane Foundation. Her agent warned her about the money. MJ said she knew the amount exactly. Then she used her event contacts to help build a private, consent-based reunion network for families who were still searching for people. She kept the photographs private. She had learned early that being visible was not the same thing as being accessible.
N|Rourke went to a memorial for Leon Arroyo, stayed for the whole service, and stood near the back so the family would not feel obliged to speak to him. Ortiz went, too. Nate Bell drove her because he knew she would not ask for a ride. Neither of them called the other brave.
N|The official count settled slowly. Fifty-eight survivors were traced and confirmed. Six people had died in the annex. Four names on the reconciled list remained unlocated. They were not entered as fatalities. The files stayed open. Arroyo's death stood beside them as an additional loss, not a rounding error folded into the building's occupancy tally.
N|At a public meeting a reporter asked Peter whether he regretted obeying the evacuation order. He had prepared a joke. He had practiced it in his head, adjusting the timing until he was sure it would make the room laugh and move on. Then he saw Ortiz sitting near the exit in a dark jacket, with her hands folded over a paper cup of coffee she had never opened.
PETER|[steady, slow]|"I regret that people died. I regret that the door didn't open. I regret that we didn't know who was inside. The four people who came out with me are alive. I don't regret getting them out."
N|The reporter asked if Spider-Man had learned a lesson.
PETER|[honest]|"I learned that I am not a substitute for a working building, a trained crew, or a list with the right names on it."
N|He had never before said anything at a microphone that felt quite so small and quite so difficult.
N|When the meeting ended, Johnny slipped out without a camera and joined Peter outside. The rain had washed the banners clean. Johnny had an unopened can of soda in each hand. He offered one.
JOHNNY|[quiet]|"You did good in there. Not the speech. The part where you didn't make it about you."
PETER|[dry]|"Thanks. Is this where you tell me the suit looks fat?"
JOHNNY|[more like himself]|"I was saving that for the ride home."
N|Wade appeared beside the stair rail. No one saw him approach. He held a wind-up robot with a new spring and one mismatched screw.
WADE|[grandly]|"Gentlemen. Proof that one of us can repair a broken thing without punching it, setting it on fire, or developing an emotionally complicated relationship with the manufacturer."
N|He wound the key. The toy took three steps and toppled onto its face. Wade picked it up, set it upright, and wound it again.
WADE|[with quiet satisfaction]|"Close enough for a first shift."
N|Across the avenue, Felicia was leaning against a cab with her arms folded. She watched MJ finish a call with a family liaison. For once Felicia did not announce herself. MJ looked over and held up a second set of keys. The cab driver had agreed to wait for both of them.
MJ|[light]|"You coming?"
FELICIA|[warm]|"Depends. Who's driving?"
MJ|[dry]|"Someone with a license. A novel concept for this crowd."
N|Felicia laughed, a real laugh, short and almost private. She climbed into the cab.
N|Peter watched it leave. The old reflex moved through him: there would always be another call, another stair, another locked room. He could already feel the city's problems reaching for him. He had grown to mistake the reach for a command. He took out his phone, dialed MJ, and left a message instead of an excuse.
PETER|[soft]|"I'm going to eat something. Then I'm going to sleep for a few hours. You don't have to supervise it. I just wanted you to know."
N|He put the phone away before he could improve the sentence into a promise he might not keep.
N|Uatu watched the five figures separate into the city. He watched the crews who had never worn costumes. He watched four unresolved names remain on a list because someone had refused to turn uncertainty into a convenient answer. He watched the morning shift come in, inspect their vehicles, check their equipment, and ask the night crew how it had gone.
N|The night crew gave the answer they always gave when the words were too large.
N|Long night.
N|Then they went home, if they could.
N|Somewhere in the Bronx, a dispatcher keyed a microphone. Another incident began. Nobody yet knew what kind it would be. Nobody yet knew whether anyone would make it back. The city had no anthem for that uncertainty. It had only the sound of a radio opening, and the ordinary human decision to answer.
N|Earth-GK did not need cleaner heroes.
N|It needed people who would keep the count, tell the truth about the cost, and come back to work without pretending the cost was nothing.

The manuscript now has a complete disaster-to-aftermath arc, including a reconciled casualty ledger instead of a magically tidy rescue. from pathlib import Path
from collections import Counter
from html import escape
from PIL import Image, ImageDraw, ImageFont
from lxml import etree
import zipfile, json, re, csv, os, textwrap, hashlib

BASE=Path('/mnt/data/earth_gk_work')
OUT=Path('/mnt/data')
raw=(BASE/'manuscript.txt').read_text(encoding='utf8')
chapters=[]
voices={
 'N':'Narrator / Uatu', 'PETER':'Peter Parker / Spider-Man', 'MJ':'Mary Jane Watson',
 'FELICIA':'Felicia Hardy / Black Cat', 'JOHNNY':'Johnny Storm / Human Torch',
 'WADE':'Wade Wilson', 'ORTIZ':'Captain Elena Ortiz', 'FINCH':'Lieutenant Ruth Finch',
 'ROURKE':'Battalion Chief Thomas Rourke', 'BELL':'Paramedic Nate Bell',
 'SECURITY':'Vane Security Supervisor', 'ASSISTANT':'MJ Assistant',
 'BRUNO':'Bruno', 'GIRL':'Ivy', 'IVY':'Ivy', 'FIRE_LT':'Fire Lieutenant',
 'CLIENT':'Vane Contractor Representative', 'CHO':'Mr. Cho',
 'SUE':'Sue Storm',
}
for i,line in enumerate(raw.splitlines(),1):
 line=line.strip()
 if not line: continue
 if line.startswith('###|'):
  _,num,title=line.split('|',2)
  chapters.append({'num':num,'title':title,'segments':[]})
  continue
 if not chapters: raise ValueError('body before chapter: '+str(i))
 parts=line.split('|',2)
 if len(parts)==2:
  who,body=parts; tag=''
 elif len(parts)==3:
  who,tag,body=parts
 else: raise ValueError('malformed line: '+str(i))
 if who not in voices: raise ValueError('unmapped voice: '+who)
 if not body: raise ValueError('empty paragraph')
 if who!='N' and not (body.startswith('"') and body.endswith('"')):
  raise ValueError(f'dialogue must be quoted: {i}: {body}')
 chapters[-1]['segments'].append({'speaker':who,'direction':tag,'text':body})
assert len(chapters)==10
count=Counter(s['speaker'] for c in chapters for s in c['segments'])
wordcount=sum(len(s['text'].split()) for c in chapters for s in c['segments'])
print('Chapters:',len(chapters),'words',wordcount,'speakers',len(count),'segments',sum(count.values()))

# Cover art: original, non-photo cover artwork generated for EPUB.
W,H=1600,2400
im=Image.new('RGB',(W,H),(13,22,23)); d=ImageDraw.Draw(im)
for y in range(H):
 factor=y/H
 d.line((0,y,W,y),fill=(int(9+10*factor),int(21+6*factor),int(23+3*factor)))
for x in range(0,W,40):
 d.line((x,0,x,H),fill=(20,32,32),width=1)
for y in range(0,H,40):
 d.line((0,y,W,y),fill=(20,32,32),width=1)
# Geometric skyline; rain and rescue-grid aesthetic.
for k,(x,h,bw) in enumerate([(65,610,105),(195,740,125),(355,560,85),(465,820,144),(646,655,135),(815,990,120),(962,730,134),(1104,920,145),(1310,580,190)]):
 d.rectangle((x,H-410-h,x+bw,H-410),fill=(14+k%3,30+k%3*4,32+k%3*3))
 for yy in range(H-410-h+30,H-410,52):
  for xx in range(x+22,x+bw-15,35):
   if (xx*13+yy*7)%9<3:
    d.rectangle((xx,yy,xx+10,yy+19),fill=(121,110,61))
for i in range(0,80):
 x=(i*271)%W; y=(i*733)%H
 d.line((x,y,x-24,y+78),fill=(45,62,57),width=2)
# Emergency radio pulse and metallic keyline.
d.line([(90,1460),(240,1460),(300,1430),(355,1495),(415,1360),(485,1540),(545,1410),(600,1460),(1500,1460)],fill=(162,135,72),width=7)
d.rectangle((70,70,W-70,H-70),outline=(96,121,96),width=4)
d.line((120,170,1480,170),fill=(175,145,83),width=4)
d.line((120,2200,1480,2200),fill=(175,145,83),width=4)
fontdir='/usr/share/fonts/truetype/dejavu'
def font(name,sz):
 path=f'{fontdir}/{name}.ttf'
 return ImageFont.truetype(path,sz)
serif=font('DejaVuSerif',118); serifBig=font('DejaVuSerif-Bold',138)
sans=font('DejaVuSans',38); sanse=font('DejaVuSans-Bold',47)
mini=font('DejaVuSans',34)
def center(text,y,f,fill,spacing=0):
 if spacing:
  widths=[d.textbbox((0,0),ch,font=f)[2] for ch in text]
  total=sum(widths)+spacing*(len(text)-1)
  x=(W-total)/2
  for ch,cw in zip(text,widths):
   d.text((x,y),ch,font=f,fill=fill);x+=cw+spacing
 else:
  b=d.textbbox((0,0),text,font=f);d.text(((W-(b[2]-b[0]))/2,y),text,font=f,fill=fill)
center('GRIZZLY KNIGHTS',300,sanse,(180,159,100),5)
center('EARTH - GK',495,font('DejaVuSans-Bold',102),(234,234,210),4)
center('THE LAST',785,serif,(244,236,206))

def xhtml(title,body,extra=''):
 return f'''<?xml version="1.0" encoding="utf-8"?>
<html xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1999/xhtml" xmlns:epub="http://www.idpf.org/2007/ops" xml:lang="en">

spines=[]
for c in chapters:
 blocks=[]
 blocks.append(f'<p class="kicker">{("Prologue" if c["num"]=="00" else "Chapter " + str(int(c["num"])))}</p>')
 blocks.append('<h1>'+escape(c['title'])+'</h1>')
 for j,s in enumerate(c['segments']):
  kind='spoken' if s['speaker']!='N' else 'prose'
  kind+=' first' if j==0 else ''
  attr=f' data-character="{escape(voices[s["speaker"]],quote=True)}"' if s['speaker']!='N' else ''
  blocks.append(f'<p class="{kind}"{attr}>{escape(s["text"])}</p>')
 c['html']=xhtml(c['title'],'\n'.join(blocks))
 spines.append(f'chapter_{c["num"]}.xhtml')

titlepage=xhtml('Title page','<h1 class="prose-title">EARTH-GK</h1><p class="sub">GRIZZLY KNIGHTS</p><p class="prose-title" style="font-size:1.6em;">THE LAST GOOD SHIFT</p><p class="sub">VOLUME ONE - UNOFFICIAL ALTERNATE-UNIVERSE STORY</p><p class="sub">An audiobook-first fiction manuscript</p>')

It is not affiliated with, endorsed by, or published by Marvel or Disney.</p><p>The events, incident, first responder characters, and Harbor Street Annex are fictional. Disaster-response scenes are written for dramatic credibility, not as operational medical, fire, or police instructions.</p><p class="colophon">The clean manuscript is designed for reading and for voice casting. Separate production materials identify speakers and suggest performance direction. Performance prompts are not part of this reading edition.</p>')
nav_li=''.join(f'<li><a href="chapter_{c["num"]}.xhtml">{escape(c["title"])}</a></li>' for c in chapters)
nav=xhtml('Contents',f'<nav epub:type="toc" id="toc"><h1>Contents</h1><ol>{nav_li}</ol></nav>')
coverx=xhtml('Cover','<div class="cover"><img src="images/cover.jpg" alt="Grizzly Knights Earth-GK: The Last Good Shift cover" /></div>')
manifest=[('cover','cover.xhtml','application/xhtml+xml',''),('title','title.xhtml','application/xhtml+xml',''),('nav','nav.xhtml','application/xhtml+xml','nav'),('colophon','colophon.xhtml','application/xhtml+xml',''),('style','style.css','text/css',''),('image','images/cover.jpg','image/jpeg','cover-image')]
manifest += [(f'chap{c["num"]}',f'chapter_{c["num"]}.xhtml','application/xhtml+xml','') for c in chapters]
manifestxml=''.join(f'<item id="{mid}" href="{href}" media-type="{mtype}"{(" properties="+chr(34)+prop+chr(34)) if prop else ""}/>' for mid,href,mtype,prop in manifest)
spineitems=['cover','title']+[f'chap{c["num"]}' for c in chapters]+['colophon']

uid='urn:uuid:19ab8809-07fb-4ae3-9766-11898cfc78af'
opf=f'''<?xml version="1.0" encoding="utf-8"?>

<manifest>{manifestxml}</manifest><spine>{spine}</spine></package>'''
container='''<?xml version="1.0" encoding="UTF-8"?><container xmlns="urn:oasis:names:tc:opendocument:xmlns:container" version="1.0"><rootfiles><rootfile full-path="OEBPS/content.opf" media-type="application/oebps-package+xml" /></rootfiles></container>'''
epub=OUT/'Earth_GK_The_Last_Good_Shift.epub'
with zipfile.ZipFile(epub,'w') as z:
 z.writestr('mimetype','application/epub+zip',compress_type=zipfile.ZIP_STORED)
 z.writestr('META-INF/container.xml',container,compress_type=zipfile.ZIP_DEFLATED)

 for c in chapters:z.writestr('OEBPS/chapter_'+c['num']+'.xhtml',c['html'],compress_type=zipfile.ZIP_DEFLATED)

CAST={
'N':('Narrator / Uatu','Even, observant, low, patient, mature. Never arch or ominous; grief emerges from precision.','Neutral American baritone; unhurried, rich.','Less is more: do not perform the lesson.'),
'PETER':('Peter Parker','Queens-shaped wit, quick and overfull when scared; almost no swearing; a simple low register with victims.','Adult tenor, warm, intelligent, lightly nasal.','Joke-density inversely tracks confidence.'),
'FELICIA':('Felicia Hardy','Warm contralto, patient, seductive only when choosing to frame a scene; stops using pet names at danger.','Low female register; soft smile under the consonants.','Emphasize choice and control, not generic seductress.'),
'MJ':('Mary Jane Watson','Bright, fast social performer becomes precise working executive; voice lowers in private; minimal profanity.','Agile mid-female register with light Queens undertone.','Tiger is intimate, not a catchphrase.'),
'JOHNNY':('Johnny Storm','Mid-high, charismatic showman becomes brief, direct, earnest when no camera can help.','Youthful bright male tenor.','Never play him as unintelligent.'),
'WADE':('Wade Wilson','Rapid, theatrical, irreverent. Jokes may snap off into unnerving silence. Keep wounded civilians outside the joke.','Rough male middle register, highly elastic pacing.','Do not impersonate a named film actor.'),
'ORTIZ':('Captain Elena Ortiz','Weathered clinical command, dry humor. Rationed softness with patients.','Grounded female low mid-register.','Calm is a practiced behavior, not lack of feeling.'),
'FINCH':('Lieutenant Ruth Finch','Clipped, economical, skeptical. Never needless yelling.','Lower female register, flat vowels.','Admit uncertainty without sounding uncertain of authority.'),

md=[]
md.append('# EARTH-GK / THE LAST GOOD SHIFT - FULL-CAST PERFORMANCE SCRIPT\n')
md.append('**Format:** director and character-casting master, companion to the clean EPUB. Not a generated ElevenLabs performance.\n')
md.append('**Instructions:** Every NARRATOR line is spoken by the narrator. Named dialogue lines use the assigned voice, with bracketed tags used as v4/v3 performance prompts; speaker labels are editorial metadata and are NOT spoken. Split lines into speaker-directed segments for production. Any ambient sound cues listed in the director notes are nonspoken editorial guidance.\n')
for c in chapters:
 md.append(f'\n## {c["num"]} - {c["title"]}\n')
 for s in c['segments']:
  name='NARRATOR' if s['speaker']=='N' else voices[s['speaker']]
  direction=(s['direction']+' ') if s['direction'] else ''
  t=s['text'] if s['speaker']=='N' else s['text'][1:-1]
  md.append(f'**{name}**\n\n{direction}{t}\n')
(OUT/'Earth_GK_Full_Cast_Performance_Script.md').write_text('\n'.join(md),encoding='utf8')

# Machine-readable timeline for a dialogue renderer with text & voice assignment.
segments=[]
for c in chapters:
 for n,s in enumerate(c['segments'],1):
  segments.append({'chapter':int(c['num']),'chapter_title':c['title'],'sequence':n,'speaker_id':s['speaker'],'speaker_name':voices[s['speaker']], 'delivery_tag':s['direction'],'spoken_text':s['text'] if s['speaker']=='N' else s['text'][1:-1]})
(OUT/'Earth_GK_Dialogue_Timeline.json').write_text(json.dumps({'title':'Earth-GK: The Last Good Shift','format':'audio drama production timeline','segments':segments},indent=2,ensure_ascii=False),encoding='utf8')

# Professional casting and production notes.

Nobody gets an uncomplicated moral victory.','', '## Principal voice casting']

Distinct voices welcome, but small roles can share casting if budget requires.','', '## Scene and sound map (editorial only)','Prologue: low city air, far traffic, then silence.','Chapter 1: engine idle, radio squelch, tired street noise, muffled alarm.','Chapter 2: the afterparty emptied out; ventilation motor; a door engaging; no music needed.','Chapter 3: voices at a working perimeter.  final.setnchannels(1);final.setsampwidth(2);final.setframerate(22050)
 def silence(seconds):
  global frames
  n=int(22050*seconds);final.writeframes(b'\x00\x00'*n);frames+=n
 def utterance(text,code):
  global frames
  text=text.replace('\u2019',"'").replace('\u2014',' - ').replace('Earth-GK','Earth G K')
  v,s,p=voices.get(code,voices['N'])
  with tempfile.NamedTemporaryFile(suffix='.wav',delete=False) as temp:
   tempname=temp.name
  try:
   subprocess.run(['espeak','-v',v,'-s',str(s),'-p',str(p),'-a','170','-w',tempname,text],stdout=subprocess.DEVNULL,stderr=subprocess.DEVNULL,check=True,timeout=20)
   with wave.open(tempname,'rb') as wav:
    if wav.getnchannels()!=1 or wav.getsampwidth()!=2 or wav.getframerate()!=22050:
     raise ValueError(f'WAV mismatch at {text[:20]}: {wav.getparams()}')
    while True:
     buf=wav.readframes(22050*10)
     if not buf:break
     final.writeframes(buf);frames+=len(buf)//2
  finally:os.unlink(tempname)

 silence(1.1)
 lastch=None
 for i,s in enumerate(segments):
  ch=s['chapter']
  if ch!=lastch:
   if lastch is not None:silence(1.2)
   start=int(frames*1000/22050)
   title=('Prologue. ' if ch==0 else f'Chapter {ch}. ')+s['chapter_title']
   ms_chapters.append({'chapter':ch,'title':title,'start':start})
   utterance(title,'N')
   silence(.5)
   lastch=ch
  utt=s['spoken_text']
  utterance(utt,s['speaker_id'])
  silence(.2 if s['speaker_id']=='N' else .28)
  if i%50==0:
   print(f'segments {i+1}/{len(segments)}, approximate running time {frames/22050/60:.1f} min',flush=True)
 silence(1.4)
length_ms=int(frames*1000/22050)
for i,ch in enumerate(ms_chapters):ch['end']=ms_chapters[i+1]['start'] if i+1<len(ms_chapters) else length_ms

subprocess.run(cmd,check=True,timeout=300)
print('FINISHED:',audio, audio.stat().st_size,'bytes',round(length_ms/60000,1),'min',len(ms_chapters),'chapters',flush=True)

# Temporary uncompressed reference is not needed for end user.
raw_wav.unlink()

[93] tool (execution_output, 780 chars)
(*args, **kwargs)
(*args, **kwargs)
    def __init__(self, *args, **kwargs):
        if len(args) == 1 and len(kwargs) == 0 and \
                isinstance(args[0], type(self)):
            other = args[0]
            # ask the sub class to fill in our data
            other._to_other(self)
        else:
            for checker, val in zip(self._framespec, args):
                setattr(self, checker.name, val)
            for checker in self._framespec[len(args):]:
                setattr(self, checker.name,
                        kwargs.get(checker.name, checker.default))
            for spec in self._optionalspec:
                if spec.name in kwargs:
                    setattr(self, spec.name, kwargs[spec.name])
                else:
                    break

[101] assistant (text, 2878 chars)
**Earth-GK: Grizzly Knights** is getting the full-cast treatment.

ElevenLabs' Audiobooks platform accepts EPUB manuscripts and supports **Multi cast**, assigning distinct voices to the narrator and characters. Its newer speech models also recognize performance cues such as `[whispers]`, `[laughs]`, and `[angry]`. <Cite refs={["turn225492search7","turn225492search0"]}/>

I'm treating this as an adult alternate Marvel universe in which superpowers don't erase human psychology, emergency-scene realities, institutional failures, or the consequences of going home after a terrible shift.

The first installment is titled **Earth-GK: The Last Good Shift**. Peter, Felicia, MJ, Johnny, and Wade are the central cast, with original EMS, fire, and police personnel who have lives and agency of their own.

<row align="center" gap={3}>
  <icon name="headphones" size="xl" color="secondary"/>
  <box flex="1" gap=0>
    <title size="lg">The Last Good Shift — Volume One</title>
    <text color="secondary" size="sm">10 sections · Approximately 7,500 words · 48-minute scratch audiobook · Full ensemble production materials</text>
  </box>
</row>

Everything below has been generated and checked.

- <icon name="book-open" inline/> **[Read or import the EPUB](sandbox:/mnt/data/Earth_GK_The_Last_Good_Shift.epub)** — designed for ElevenReader and ElevenLabs Audiobooks.
- <icon name="headphones" inline/> **[Listen to the 48-minute audiobook scratch recording (MP3)](sandbox:/mnt/data/Earth_GK_The_Last_Good_Shift_Scratch_Audiobook.mp3)** — playable now, with locally synthesized character voices and ten embedded chapter markers.
- <icon name="package" inline/> **[Download the complete production kit (ZIP)](sandbox:/mnt/data/Earth_GK_Full_Cast_Production_Kit.zip)** — EPUB, cover, casting bible, performance script, and speaker-by-speaker production timeline.
- <icon name="clapperboard" inline/> **[Full-cast performance script](sandbox:/mnt/data/Earth_GK_Full_Cast_Performance_Script.md)** — character assignments and emotional delivery cues.

The MP3 is a **mechanical reference recording**, not a finished ElevenLabs performance. For expressive, natural voices, import the EPUB into ElevenLabs Audiobooks, select **Multi cast**, and use the production bible to refine its character assignments. The script contains additional acting cues for compatible speech-generation workflows.

I built the psychological conflicts into the characters' actions rather than having them recite their dossiers. Peter's jokes become a warning sign. Felicia has to surrender control. Johnny discovers a situation his flames cannot fix. MJ refuses to become someone else's emotional life-support system. Wade is forced to confront what happens when violence isn't the useful answer.

And the emergency crews? They're neither saints nor disposable background characters.

**Welcome to Earth-GK.**

[104] assistant (text, 1023 chars)
**Yes. Absolutely. And I got the central premise wrong.**

You gave me EMS, fire, law enforcement, and military personnel as a **psychological reference point**, not as the setting or subject of the story.

**Earth-GK: Grizzly Knights is a Marvel universe.** Spider-Man, Black Cat, Mary Jane Watson, Johnny Storm, Deadpool, and the larger Marvel world are the story.

The difference from conventional Marvel is that these characters behave like actual people who have spent years doing extraordinary, dangerous, morally complicated things.

They still fight supervillains, deal with cosmic catastrophes, navigate relationships, make terrible decisions, and occasionally save the entire goddamn planet. But they also carry psychological damage, develop maladaptive coping mechanisms, form dysfunctional loyalties, and have to live with the consequences of their actions.

**The superhuman world is the setting. The human psychology is what makes Earth-GK different.**

I inverted those priorities in *The Last Good Shift*.

# EARTH-GK / THE LAST GOOD SHIFT - FULL-CAST PERFORMANCE SCRIPT

**Format:** director and character-casting master, companion to the clean EPUB. Not a generated ElevenLabs performance.

**Instructions:** Every NARRATOR line is spoken by the narrator. Named dialogue lines use the assigned voice, with bracketed tags used as v4/v3 performance prompts; speaker labels are editorial metadata and are NOT spoken. Split lines into speaker-directed segments for production. Any ambient sound cues listed in the director notes are nonspoken editorial guidance.


## 00 - The Watcher Keeps a Count

**NARRATOR**

On Earth-GK, the first lie about any disaster is usually the smallest one. A wrong street number. A unit marked clear while it is still wiping blood off its floor. A supervisor saying there are thirty-one people in a building because thirty-one is the number on the sheet.

**NARRATOR**

From a distance sufficient to contain a galaxy, Uatu watched the eastern coast of North America turn the color of a healing bruise. A storm was moving over New York. It was a weather event, not an omen. The distinction mattered. Omens excused people from checking the wiring.

**NARRATOR**

He had observed stars fall inward. He had observed empires invent astonishingly persuasive reasons not to rescue their own citizens. He had also observed exhausted creatures in cheap uniforms make decisions that deserved monuments, then go home to eat cereal over the sink because the laundromat was closed.

**NARRATOR**

The Watcher did not intervene.

**NARRATOR**

He did, however, keep the count.

**NARRATOR**

At 2:17 in the morning, the official count at the Harbor Street Annex was thirty-one.

**NARRATOR**

It was wrong.


## 01 - Three Bells and No Coffee

**NARRATOR**

At 2:17 a caller reported smoke at a service door beneath the Harbor Street Interchange. At 2:19 someone reported a woman screaming from a stairwell. At 2:21 a third caller said there were children behind a locked door and the lights were going out.

**NARRATOR**

At 2:23, Captain Elena Ortiz of city EMS put down the coffee she had just purchased and told her driver to take the corner gently. There was no lid on the cup. There never was when it mattered.

**Captain Elena Ortiz**

[dry] If this is another transformer smoking in a puddle, I am going to marry a dentist.

**NARRATOR**

Paramedic Nate Bell, thirty-two, a former Army medic and handsome in the way people were handsome before night work rearranged their faces, turned the wheel. He had learned to sleep through mortar fire overseas and still woke at home whenever a neighbor dropped a pan. He never mentioned either fact in the ambulance.

**Paramedic Nate Bell**

[lightly] Can I come to the wedding?

**Captain Elena Ortiz**

[dry] Not if you drive like that.

**NARRATOR**

She had been in emergency medicine long enough to distrust both a quiet radio and an exciting one. What she trusted was a pattern. Three independent callers, one location, three different descriptions: that was a pattern. The number on the dispatch screen was just a number.

**NARRATOR**

Under the interchange, Lieutenant Ruth Finch of the NYPD had already put two officers on the public entrance and another on the service lane. She had the blunt posture of someone who did not ask a crowd to cooperate twice. Her first officer was young enough to be worried about doing the right thing in front of the right people. Her second had worked double overtime and was worried about getting home.

**Lieutenant Ruth Finch**

[commanding] Nobody goes through that gate unless fire says it's safe. I mean nobody. Cameras, politicians, and anybody who tells you they know a commissioner.

**NARRATOR**

The fire department arrived with engine crews, a truck company, and Battalion Chief Thomas Rourke, who emerged from his command vehicle looking at the building as if it had insulted his mother. The south entrance breathed gray smoke. Somewhere beyond it, an alarm gave two notes, gave up, and began again.

**Battalion Chief Thomas Rourke**

[controlled] What do we know?

**Lieutenant Ruth Finch**

[matter-of-fact] Three calls. Security says thirty-one on site. One employee says the lower doors close automatically at midnight.

**Battalion Chief Thomas Rourke**

[flat] Did anyone ask the doors how many people they think are inside?

**NARRATOR**

Finch did not smile. She did write something down.

**NARRATOR**

Spider-Man arrived from above, inverted on a streetlight, thirty seconds after the second engine. He had come to the scene because his phone said fire and his instincts said worse. Both were generally expensive opinions to ignore.

**Peter Parker / Spider-Man**

[bright, forced] Good morning, New York. Love what you've done with the smoke. Very industrial. Little much for a Tuesday.

**NARRATOR**

Rourke looked up without changing expression.

**Battalion Chief Thomas Rourke**

[dry] Are you here to help or to do commentary?

**Peter Parker / Spider-Man**

[chastened] Usually both. I'm trying to diversify.

Rourke nodded. It was the kind of exchange that happens between people who have spent years watching other people improvise certainty.

**NARRATOR**

A security supervisor in a spotless navy polo approached from the side gate. His laminated badge said VANE COMMUNITY OPERATIONS. His shoes had not encountered the kind of night everyone else's had.

--- BIBLE ---
# GRIZZLY KNIGHTS / EARTH-GK
## The Last Good Shift - Audio Production Bible

**Length:** 7,526 words across 10 chapters, including prologue.
**Tone:** Adult disaster drama, black humor, restraint when patients are present, brutal honesty afterward.
**World:** Earth-GK is an original alternate continuity, not a claim about canon or real-world people.

## Performance principle
The characters do not talk like their psychological dossiers. Their coping mechanisms are observable in decisions, speed, silence, and what they cannot say. Do not read stage directions aloud. The fire itself is not a villain. Nobody gets an uncomplicated moral victory.

## Principal voice casting
### Narrator / Uatu
- Vocal identity: Neutral American baritone; unhurried, rich.
- Playing note: Even, observant, low, patient, mature. Never arch or ominous; grief emerges from precision.
- Avoid: Less is more: do not perform the lesson.

### Peter Parker
- Vocal identity: Adult tenor, warm, intelligent, lightly nasal.
- Playing note: Queens-shaped wit, quick and overfull when scared; almost no swearing; a simple low register with victims.
- Avoid: Joke-density inversely tracks confidence.

### Felicia Hardy
- Vocal identity: Low female register; soft smile under the consonants.
- Playing note: Warm contralto, patient, seductive only when choosing to frame a scene; stops using pet names at danger.
- Avoid: Emphasize choice and control, not generic seductress.

### Mary Jane Watson
- Vocal identity: Agile mid-female register with light Queens undertone.
- Playing note: Bright, fast social performer becomes precise working executive; voice lowers in private; minimal profanity.
- Avoid: Tiger is intimate, not a catchphrase.

### Johnny Storm
- Vocal identity: Youthful bright male tenor.
- Playing note: Mid-high, charismatic showman becomes brief, direct, earnest when no camera can help.
- Avoid: Never play him as unintelligent.

### Wade Wilson
- Vocal identity: Rough male middle register, highly elastic pacing.
- Playing note: Rapid, theatrical, irreverent. Jokes may snap off into unnerving silence. Keep wounded civilians outside the joke.
- Avoid: Do not impersonate a named film actor.

### Captain Elena Ortiz
- Vocal identity: Grounded female low mid-register.
- Playing note: Weathered clinical command, dry humor. Rationed softness with patients.
- Avoid: Calm is a practiced behavior, not lack of feeling.

### Lieutenant Ruth Finch
- Vocal identity: Lower female register, flat vowels.
- Playing note: Clipped, economical, skeptical. Never needless yelling.
- Avoid: Admit uncertainty without sounding uncertain of authority.

### Battalion Chief Rourke
- Vocal identity: Middle-aged, plain, unornamented male bass.
- Playing note: Dry, few words, decisive when accountability is at risk.
- Avoid: The command voice means care for crews, not domination.

### Nate Bell
- Vocal identity: Young adult male, warm.
- Playing note: Light, laconic; trained calm that cannot entirely reach life after shift.
- Avoid: No parody of veteran mannerisms.

### Ivy
- Vocal identity: Young female, not babyish.
- Playing note: A child trying to remain strong for a younger sibling.
- Avoid: Nervous, not saccharine.

### Sue Storm
- Vocal identity: Adult female, warm.
- Playing note: Intimate, intelligent, quiet certainty.
- Avoid: No lecture.

## Secondary voices
Bruno: precise and alarmed; Vane security supervisor: evasive, not a cartoon villain; Mr. Cho: short of breath but lucid; fire lieutenant: plain working voice; the client: bureaucratic and self-justifying; MJ assistant: efficient and rattled. Distinct voices welcome, but small roles can share casting if budget requires.

## Scene and sound map (editorial only)
Prologue: low city air, far traffic, then silence.
Chapter 1: engine idle, radio squelch, tired street noise, muffled alarm.
Chapter 2: the afterparty emptied out; ventilation motor; a door engaging; no music needed.
Chapter 3: voices at a working perimeter. Keep cameras distant, not glamorous.
Chapter 4: constrained high-frequency room tone. Let silence feel physically small.
Chapter 5: overlapping command post activity beneath precise dialogue.
Chapter 6: *withhold* the action-movie crescendo. Use abrupt reduction of noise for the collapse, then sparse radio voices.
Chapter 7: pencil and paper, an ambulance door, a phone. Silence on the death notification.
Chapter 8: unhurried diner room tone, far dishware. A quieter phone line to Sue.
Chapter 9: no triumphant theme. An unresolved file is not an unfinished sentence.

## Production setup for ElevenLabs (as documented October 2026)
1. Open the ElevenLabs Audiobooks product and create an audiobook.
2. Upload the clean **Earth_GK_The_Last_Good_Shift.epub** manuscript.
3. Select **Multi cast** and review its AI-detected character assignments.
4. Audition distinct voices for the listed roles; correct any line attribution errors.
5. For finer expressive control, use this separate performance script with a model that supports square-bracket audio tags, such as Eleven v4, on individual lines or dialogue chunks. Audiobooks multi-cast may use different model behavior; do not assume all bracketed tags from a script will be interpreted when placed in an EPUB.
6. Preview high-stakes scenes (chapter 4, chapter 6, chapter 8) before generating the whole piece.
7. Check whether line labels are spoken. They are metadata for the producer, not part of the performance.

## Audio tags and accuracy
The full-cast script uses short inline [tag] directions. ElevenLabs v4/v3 supports audio tags, but exact emotional response varies by selected voice and production workflow. Use punctuation and casting, not tags alone, to carry the performance.

## Source and creative provenance
Peter, Felicia, MJ, and Johnny were interpreted from the user-supplied UATU dossiers. The supplied Wade file contains no substantive dossier and his Earth-GK characterization is original to this treatment. The incident, all supporting emergency-personnel characters, dialogue, narrative, and case outcome are original fiction. The story avoids verbatim reproduction of Marvel comic dialogue.

## Important
This is an unofficial alternate-universe fan-fiction work. No official Marvel or ElevenLabs collaboration, voice synthesis, or rights license is implied. The scratch recording, when supplied, is a mechanical reference read-through rendered locally; it does not represent a finished ElevenLabs audiobook.

# 6. COMMUNICATIONS & LINGUISTIC PROFILE

*Six decades of recorded speech across thought-balloon, dialogue-balloon, panel caption, and intercepted radio/comm chatter. The subject is, by volume, among the most verbose costumed operators in the field — a fact which is itself diagnostic.*

---

## 6.1 BASELINE REGISTER AND IDIOLECT

The subject's native idiolect is outer-borough mid-century American English, college-educated, scientifically inflected, with a persistent residue of working-class Queens cadence that he has never made any observable effort to scrub. Vowel-flattening on words like "coffee," "talk," "Aunt"; intervocalic /t/ surviving in careful speech but going to a soft flap when tired; occasional "youse" surfacing in moments of inattention with old neighborhood contacts. The substrate is Forest Hills, not Manhattan, and the substrate shows whenever fatigue, fear, or familiarity disinhibits the surface management.

Lexically, his active vocabulary is bimodally distributed: a high-register technical register drawn from organic chemistry, polymer science, applied physics, mechanical engineering, and (post-Horizon/Parker Industries) materials science and nanofabrication; and a low-to-mid register of vernacular American comedy, sitcom rhythms, pulp-magazine and old-radio reference, sports patter (Mets-leaning, never Yankees, occasional Knicks self-pity), and a steady drip of pop-culture allusion that ages with him but never fully modernizes — he still reaches first for a Bogart line, a Honeymooners cadence, an old Universal-monster reference, a Twilight Zone framing, before he reaches for whatever is current. This generational lag in reference is real and consistent across writers: it reads as a man whose interior reference library was assembled in late-night TV reruns and library trips during a lonely adolescence, and who has never had the leisure to fully refresh it.

What is conspicuously absent from his idiolect is also diagnostic. He does not curse with any meaningful frequency. "Damn" and "hell" are his ceiling in extremis; under torture or grief he will reach for "God" as exclamation, never as oath. He does not use slurs of any kind, has never been recorded doing so, and visibly winces when others do. He does not use the language of dominance — no "boy," no "kid" as condescension (only as affection), no "listen here," no commanding imperatives outside operational necessity. He does not use the corporate-managerial register even during the Parker Industries period; recordings of his executive me

# COMMUNICATIONS & LINGUISTIC PROFILE — SUBJECT: MARY JANE WATSON

Classification: Longitudinal linguistic surveillance synthesis. Speech samples drawn from Lee/Romita introduction (ASM #42, 1966) through Wells run (present). Cross-referenced against alternate-continuity samples (Ultimate, Renew Your Vows, Spider-Girl flash-forwards) for stability of idiolect under different authorial hands.

---

## 1. BASELINE REGISTER — THE DEFAULT VOICE

Subject's default speaking voice is a mid-Atlantic American English overlaid with light residual Queens — the Queens surfaces only in two contexts: (a) emotional escalation, where dropped *g*'s and tightened vowels return ("nothin'," "talkin'," "ya kiddin' me"), and (b) deliberate code-switch downward when she wants to signal solidarity with a working-class interlocutor or undercut someone's pretension. Both are markers. The baseline she presents in neutral social settings is *acquired* — the speech of someone who consciously polished an accent to be castable, employable, and class-mobile. This is itself a linguistic tell: the standard register is the costume; the borough is the body underneath.

Speech rate is fast — measurably faster than Peter Parker's, faster than most of her female peer set in the canon. She talks *over* silences rather than into them. Pauses are managed: she will fill a beat with a small physical sound (laugh, exhale, a hummed *mm*) rather than allow dead air, because dead air is where the room can turn on her. The few instances on panel where she allows a long silence are diagnostic of either (a) full safety (late-arc Peter, Aunt May after the secret-share) or (b) total shutdown (separation arc, post-stillbirth).

Pitch travels higher in performance-mode (party scenes, public events, first encounters) and drops noticeably when she is alone with a trusted intimate — by the JMS run this drop is overt and Peter notices it on panel. The "real" voice is lower, slower, and approximately a third less verbose per beat than the public voice.

---

## 2. THE PARTY-GIRL REGISTER — INTRODUCTION-ERA AND ITS HALF-LIFE

The Lee/Romita-period voice is its own register and never fully retires; it gets recruited under specific conditions for the rest of her life. Markers:

- Tiger. The single most overdetermined word in the file. First deployed at introduction ("Face it, tiger — you just hit the jackpot"), it persists for sixty publication-years as a *targeted-affection signal* used exclusively for Peter. She does not call other men *tiger*. The word does three jobs at o
