# UATU DOSSIER — SUBJECT: JONATHAN LOWELL SPENCER STORM
## Section 7: Communications & Linguistic Profile

Classification: Behavioral Linguistics, Longitudinal
Source Base: Full canonical record of recorded speech, broadcast appearances, intra-team communication, courtroom and Congressional testimony (Civil War proceedings), recorded interviews, and observable private speech with intimates.

---

### 7.1 Baseline Register: The Default Voice

Subject's resting speech register is mid-Atlantic American with the upstate-New-York vowels never fully scrubbed out — a "Glenville substrate" that surfaces when he is tired, drunk, frightened, or speaking to his sister. In public-facing contexts he speaks in a polished, slightly broadcast-trained cadence picked up across decades of press junkets, talk-show appearances, and the brief Hollywood period; this overlay is thin and peels off under stress within a single sentence.

Pitch: baritone, mobile, with a habitual upward inflection at sentence-end that reads as either question or invitation depending on context. He uses uptalk *strategically* — it pulls the listener forward, demands their continued attention, and conveniently disguises declarative statements as collaborative ones. ("So we go in through the roof? And I take the big guy?") This is not Valley-girl uptalk; it is salesman uptalk, learned young.

Pace: fast at baseline (approximately 180–210 words per minute, against an American conversational median nearer 150), accelerating with excitement and — crucially — also accelerating with grief or threat, which is one of his most reliable tells. He does not slow down when wounded; he speeds up. The pace-as-tell will recur throughout this section.

Volume: moderate to loud; he projects without effort and rarely modulates downward unless deliberately performing intimacy. He does not whisper well. When forced to be quiet (stealth ops, hospital rooms), he becomes physically uncomfortable in a visible way.

Vocabulary: mid-tier active lexicon, surprisingly rich in mechanical, aviation, and combustion vocabulary (carburetors, stoichiometric ratios, flashpoints, autoignition temperatures, aviation phonetics, structural-stress terms), and conspicuously thin in abstract, political, philosophical, and emotional vocabulary. He knows the words for *what burns and how fast;* he does not have words for *what hurts and why.* This asymmetry is the single most diagnostic feature of his speech.

---

### 7.2 Register Switching Across Contexts

============================== Felicia Hardy (Black Cat).md ==============================
SECTION Communications & Linguistic Profile at 386831
Communications & Linguistic Profile

# UATU DOSSIER — FELICIA HARDY / BLACK CAT
## Section 6: Communications & Linguistic Profile

---

### 6.1 Baseline Voice

The subject's natural speaking voice — across forty years of canon and every writer who has handled her — sits in a narrow, identifiable band: mid-tempo, low-affect, contralto-warm, smiling-through-the-line. She rarely raises her voice. She rarely speaks quickly. The default delivery is the half-turned-away aside — a remark addressed nominally to the room but engineered to land on one specific person, who is meant to feel singled out by the privacy of it. She speaks to be overheard *by the right ear only*.

Sentence length is short to medium. She avoids the long-clause sentence; her grammar is tight, declarative, and almost entirely free of hedges ("I think," "maybe," "sort of" appear only when she is deliberately performing softness). The absence of hedging is a tell in itself — most speakers of her demographic and apparent affect use them as social lubricant; she does not need the lubrication because she is not seeking permission to speak. The grammar of someone who decided long ago she would never again wait to be invited.

Vocabulary is wider than she lets on. She codeswitches *downward* far more often than she codeswitches up — she will play to a fence's level, to a mark's level, to a thug's level. With Peter she occasionally lets a stray polysyllable or art-history reference slip and then half-buries it with a smirk, as if embarrassed to have shown the inventory. Empire State coursework, autodidactic reading in art and gemology, and a working museum-curator's vocabulary all live under the cat-burglar patter. The patter is the costume; the vocabulary is the face.

---

### 6.2 The Pet-Name System

The single most cited verbal habit in her file. She names people before she answers them. "Lover," "sweetheart," "honey," "sugar," "darling," "kitten," "tiger," and — reserved almost exclusively for Peter and a small handful of equivalents — "Spider." The pet-name is not affection. It is a *frame-setting device*:

- It establishes that *she* is the one who gets to name.
- It collapses the interlocutor into a class of object (lover, kitten) that she controls the membership of.
- It pre-empts being named first, which would be a frame-loss.
- It functions as an affectionate soft put-down when deployed to strangers, equals, or adversaries.

The hierarchy is meaningful. "Spider" is reserved, intimate, and almost never said in front of others. "Lover" is the public-private hybrid — said in

---- README
GRIZZLY KNIGHTS / EARTH-GK - THE LAST GOOD SHIFT
================================================

Audio production kit, volume one. Adult fiction. Not an official Marvel work.

START HERE:
1. For a natural AI audiobook with multiple character voices: open ElevenLabs Audiobooks, upload Earth_GK_The_Last_Good_Shift.epub, and choose Multi cast.
2. Review character assignments against the Audio Production Bible and Full-Cast Performance Script.
3. The script has Eleven v4/v3-style bracketed acting directions that can be used in compatible text-to-speech workflows. Speaker labels must not be spoken.
4. A scratch full-cast MP3 is supplied separately. The MP3 is a 48-minute LOCAL, MECHANICAL TTS REFERENCE, not the finished ElevenLabs performance.

THIS ZIP CONTAINS:
- Clean, navigable EPUB with cover
- Director's performance script (Markdown)
- Production bible, voice casting & sound map (Markdown)
- Dialogue timeline (JSON; exact speaker/line mapping)
- Chapter timings (JSON)
- Original cover art (JPEG)

The Wade source upload had no substantive character data. This version of Wade is original Earth-GK characterization. Everything depicted in the Harbor Street incident is fiction.
---- BIBLE
# GRIZZLY KNIGHTS / EARTH-GK
## The Last Good Shift - Audio Production Bible

**Length:** 7,526 words across 10 chapters, including prologue.
**Tone:** Adult disaster drama, black humor, restraint when patients are present, brutal honesty afterward.
**World:** Earth-GK is an original alternate continuity, not a claim about canon or real-world people.

## Performance principle
The characters do not talk like their psychological dossiers. Their coping mechanisms are observable in decisions, speed, silence, and what they cannot say. Do not read stage directions aloud. The fire itself is not a villain. Nobody gets an uncomplicated moral victory.

## Principal voice casting
### Narrator / Uatu
- Vocal identity: Neutral American baritone; unhurried, rich.
- Playing note: Even, observant, low, patient, mature. Never arch or ominous; grief emerges from precision.
- Avoid: Less is more: do not perform the lesson.

### Peter Parker
- Vocal identity: Adult tenor, warm, intelligent, lightly nasal.
- Playing note: Queens-shaped wit, quick and overfull when scared; almost no swearing; a simple low register with victims.
- Avoid: Joke-density inversely tracks confidence.

### Felicia Hardy
- Vocal identity: Low female register; soft smile under the consonants.
- Playing note: Warm contralto, patient, seductive only when choosing to frame a scene; stops using pet names at danger.
- Avoid: Emphasize choice and control, not generic seductress.

### Mary Jane Watson
- Vocal identity: Agile mid-female register with light Queens undertone.
- Playing note: Bright, fast social performer becomes precise working executive; voice lowers in private; minimal profanity.
- Avoid: Tiger is intimate, not a catchphrase.

### Johnny Storm
- Vocal identity: Youthful bright male tenor.
- Playing note: Mid-high, charismatic showman becomes brief, direct, earnest when no camera can help.
- Avoid: Never play him as unintelligent.

### Wade Wilson
- Vocal identity: Rough male middle register, highly elastic pacing.
- Playing note: Rapid, theatrical, irreverent. Jokes may snap off into unnerving silence. Keep wounded civilians outside the joke.
- Avoid: Do not impersonate a named film actor.

### Captain Elena Ortiz
- Vocal identity: Grounded female low mid-register.
- Playing note: Weathered clinical command, dry humor. Rationed softness with patients.
- Avoid: Calm is a practiced behavior, not lack of feeling.

### Lieutenant Ruth Finch
- Vocal identity: Lower female register, flat vowels.
- Playing note: Clipped, economical, skeptical. Never needless yelling.
- Avoid: Admit uncertainty without sounding uncertain of authority.

### Battalion Chief Rourke
- Vocal identity: Middle-aged, plain, unornamented male bass.
- Playing note: Dry, few words, decisive when accountability is at risk.
- Avoid: The command voice means care for crews, not domination.

### Nate Bell
- Vocal identity: Young adult male, warm.
- Playing note: Light, laconic; trained calm that cannot entirely reach life after shift.
- Avoid: No parody of veteran mannerisms.

### Ivy
- Vocal identity: Young female, not babyish.
- Playing note: A child trying to remain strong for a younger sibling.
- Avoid: Nervous, not saccharine.

### Sue Storm
- Vocal identity: Adult female, warm.
- Playing note: Intimate, intelligent, quiet certainty.
- Avoid: No lecture.

## Secondary voices
Bruno: precise and alarmed; Vane security supervisor: evasive, not a cartoon villain; Mr. Cho: short of breath but lucid; fire lieutenant: plain working voice; the client: bureaucratic and self-justifying; MJ assistant: efficient and rattled. Distinct voices welcome, but small roles can share casting if budget requires.

## Scene and sound map (editorial only)
Prologue: low city air, far traffic, then silence.
Chapter 1: engine idle, radio squelch, tired street noise, muffled alarm.
Chapter 2: the afterparty emptied out; ventilation motor; a door engaging; no music needed.
Chapter 3: voices at a working perimeter. Keep cameras distant, not glamorous.
Chapter 4: constrained high-frequency room tone. Let silence feel physically small.
Chapter 5: overlapping command post activity beneath precise dialogue.
Chapter 6: *withhold* the action-movie crescendo. Use abrupt reduction of noise for the collapse, then sparse radio voices.
Chapter 7: pencil and paper, an ambulance door, a phone. Silence on the death notification.
Chapter 8: unhurried diner room tone, far dishware. A quieter phone line to Sue.
Chapter 9: no triumphant theme. An unresolved file is not an unfinished sentence.

## Production setup for ElevenLabs (as documented October 2026)
1. Open the ElevenLabs Audiobooks product and create an audiobook.
2. Upload the clean **Earth_GK_The_Last_Good_Shift.epub** manuscript.
3. Select **Multi cast** and review its AI-detected character assignments.
4. Audition distinct voices for the listed roles; correct any line attribution errors.
5. For finer expressive control, use this separate performance script with a model that supports square-bracket audio tags, such as Eleven v4, on individual lines or dialogue chunks. Audiobooks multi-cast may use different model behavior; do not assume all bracketed tags from a script will be interpreted when placed in an EPUB.
6. Preview high-stakes scenes (chapter 4, chapter 6, chapter 8) before generating the whole piece.
7. Check whether line labels are spoken. They are metadata for the producer, not part of the performance.

## Audio tags and accuracy
The full-cast script uses short inline [tag] directions. ElevenLabs v4/v3 supports audio tags, but exact emotional response varies by selected voice and production workflow. Use punctuation and casting, not tags alone, to carry the performance.

## Source and creative provenance
Peter, Felicia, MJ, and Johnny were interpreted from the user-supplied UATU dossiers. The supplied Wade file contains no substantive dossier and his Earth-GK characterization is original to this treatment. The incident, all supporting emergency-personnel characters, dialogue, narrative, and case outcome are original fiction. The story avoids verbatim reproduction of Marvel comic dialogue.

## Important
This is an unofficial alternate-universe fan-fiction work. No official Marvel or ElevenLabs collaboration, voice synthesis, or rights license is implied. The scratch recording, when supplied, is a mechanical reference read-through rendered locally; it does not represent a finished ElevenLabs audiobook.

from pathlib import Path
from collections import Counter
from html import escape
from PIL import Image, ImageDraw, ImageFont
from lxml import etree
import zipfile, json, re, csv, os, textwrap, hashlib

BASE=Path('/mnt/data/earth_gk_work')
OUT=Path('/mnt/data')
raw=(BASE/'manuscript.txt').read_text(encoding='utf8')
chapters=[]
voices={
 'N':'Narrator / Uatu', 'PETER':'Peter Parker / Spider-Man', 'MJ':'Mary Jane Watson',
 'FELICIA':'Felicia Hardy / Black Cat', 'JOHNNY':'Johnny Storm / Human Torch',
 'WADE':'Wade Wilson', 'ORTIZ':'Captain Elena Ortiz', 'FINCH':'Lieutenant Ruth Finch',
 'ROURKE':'Battalion Chief Thomas Rourke', 'BELL':'Paramedic Nate Bell',
 'SECURITY':'Vane Security Supervisor', 'ASSISTANT':'MJ Assistant',
 'BRUNO':'Bruno', 'GIRL':'Ivy', 'IVY':'Ivy', 'FIRE_LT':'Fire Lieutenant',
 'CLIENT':'Vane Contractor Representative', 'CHO':'Mr. Cho',
 'SUE':'Sue Storm',
}
for i,line in enumerate(raw.splitlines(),1):
 line=line.strip()
 if not line: continue
 if line.startswith('###|'):
  _,num,title=line.split('|',2)
  chapters.append({'num':num,'title':title,'segments':[]})
  continue
 if not chapters: raise ValueError('body before chapter: '+str(i))
 parts=line.split('|',2)
 if len(parts)==2:
  who,body=parts; tag=''
 elif len(parts)==3:
  who,tag,body=parts
 else: raise ValueError('malformed line: '+str(i))
 if who not in voices: raise ValueError('unmapped voice: '+who)
 if not body: raise ValueError('empty paragraph')
 if who!='N' and not (body.startswith('"') and body.endswith('"')):
  raise ValueError(f'dialogue must be quoted: {i}: {body}')
 chapters[-1]['segments'].append({'speaker':who,'direction':tag,'text':body})
assert len(chapters)==10
count=Counter(s['speaker'] for c in chapters for s in c['segments'])
wordcount=sum(len(s['text'].split()) for c in chapters for s in c['segments'])
print('Chapters:',len(chapters),'words',wordcount,'speakers',len(count),'segments',sum(count.values()))

 d.line((0,y,W,y),fill=(int(9+10*factor),int(21+6*factor),int(23+3*factor)))

d.rectangle((70,70,W-70,H-70),outline=(96,121,96),width=4)
d.line((120,170,1480,170),fill=(175,145,83),width=4)
d.line((120,2200,1480,2200),fill=(175,145,83),width=4)

 path=f'{fontdir}/{name}.ttf'
 return ImageFont.truetype(path,sz)
serif=font('DejaVuSerif',118); serifBig=font('DejaVuSerif-Bold',138)

def center(text,y,f,fill,spacing=0):
 if spacing:
  widths=[d.textbbox((0,0),ch,font=f)[2] for ch in text]
  total=sum(widths)+spacing*(len(text)-1)
  x=(W-total)/2

###|04|The Geometry of an Exit
N|Peter entered through an opening a fire crew had already cleared. He did not try to outrun them; he was growing reluctantly competent at learning the difference between speed and useful speed. The first level smelled of wet cement and scorched insulation. His spider-sense kept insisting that the building was a problem too large to punch.
N|A firefighter shouted directions. He gave back the location he was heading to, because Ortiz had taught him once, with the savage patience of someone teaching a child not to touch a stove, that being hard to track was not an endearing quality at a rescue scene.

with wave.open(str(raw_wav),'wb') as final:

 def silence(seconds):
  global frames
  n=int(22050*seconds);final.writeframes(b'\x00\x00'*n);frames+=n
 def utterance(text,code):
  global frames
  text=text.replace('\u2019',"'").replace('\u2014',' - ').replace('Earth-GK','Earth G K')
  v,s,p=voices.get(code,voices['N'])

   subprocess.run(['espeak','-v',v,'-s',str(s),'-p',str(p),'-a','170','-w',tmp,text],stdout=subprocess.DEVNULL,stderr=subprocess.DEVNULL,check=True,timeout=20)
   with wave.open(tmp,'rb') as wav:
    if (wav.getnchannels(),wav.getsampwidth(),wav.getframerate())!=(1,2,22050):
     raise ValueError('Unexpected audio format: '+str(wav.getparams()))
    while True:
     buf=wav.readframes(22050*10)
     if not buf:break
     final.writeframes(buf);frames+=len(buf)//2
  finally:os.unlink(tmp)

=====  Peter Benjamin Parker (Spider-Man).md 592860  =====
FOUND Communications & Linguistic Profile OFFSET 440493 
 Communications & Linguistic Profile

# 6. COMMUNICATIONS & LINGUISTIC PROFILE

*Six decades of recorded speech across thought-balloon, dialogue-balloon, panel caption, and intercepted radio/comm chatter. The subject is, by volume, among the most verbose costumed operators in the field — a fact which is itself diagnostic.*

---

## 6.1 BASELINE REGISTER AND IDIOLECT

The subject's native idiolect is **outer-borough mid-century American English, college-educated, scientifically inflected, with a persistent residue of working-class Queens cadence** that he has never made any observable effort to scrub. Vowel-flattening on words like "coffee," "talk," "Aunt"; intervocalic /t/ surviving in careful speech but going to a soft flap when tired; occasional "youse" surfacing in moments of inattention with old neighborhood contacts. The substrate is Forest Hills, not Manhattan, and the substrate shows whenever fatigue, fear, or familiarity disinhibits the surface management.

Lexically, his active vocabulary is **bimodally distributed**: a high-register technical register drawn from organic chemistry, polymer science, applied physics, mechanical engineering, and (post-Horizon/Parker Industries) materials science and nanofabrication; and a low-to-mid register of vernacular American comedy, sitcom rhythms, pulp-magazine and old-radio reference, sports patter (Mets-leaning, never Yankees, occasional Knicks self-pity), and a steady drip of pop-culture allusion that ages with him but never fully modernizes — he still reaches first for a Bogart line, a Honeymooners cadence, an old Universal-monster reference, a Twilight Zone framing, before he reaches for whatever is current. This generational lag in reference is real and consistent across writers: it reads as a man whose interior reference library was assembled in late-night TV reruns and library trips during a lonely adolescence, and who has never had the leisure to fully refresh it.

What is conspicuously **absent** from his idiolect is also diagnostic. He does not curse with any meaningful frequency. "Damn" and "hell" are his ceiling in extremis; under torture or grief he will reach for "God" as exclamation, never as oath. He does not use slurs of any kind, has never been recorded doing so, and visibly winces when others do. He does not use the language of dominance — no "boy," no "kid" as condescension (only as affection), no "listen here," no commanding imperatives outside operational necessity. He does not use the corporate-managerial register even during the Parker Industries period; recordings of his executive meetings show him reverting to teacher-explainer mode rather than CEO-directive mode within minutes. He does not use therapy-language — "boundaries," "trauma," "processing," "holding space" — and this absence is striking given how much of it would apply to him. The vocabulary of self-examination has not been issued to him, and he has not requisitioned it.

---

## 6.2 REGISTER-SWITCHING ACROSS CONTEXT

The subject operates in at least **six discrete speech registers**, switched fluently and often within a single conversation:

**(a) Masked combat patter.** The densest, most performative register. Rapid-fire, joke-per-beat, structured in setup-punchline rhythm with frequent callbacks to earlier lines in the same fight. Vocabulary skews toward absurd similes ("you punch like my Aunt May after three espressos"), forced rhymes, mock-formality ("Mister Octopus, *if* I may"), and a recurring trick of **giving his opponents nicknames they did not ask for and which they cannot prevent from sticking** (Doc Ock, Shocker, Mysterio-as-fishbowl, Rhino-as-large-gentleman). The patter has two functions, both load-bearing: it regulates his own fear (rate of joke-production correlates inversely with felt safety — *the funnier he is, the more terrified he is*), and it operates as cognitive-load atta

=====  Felicia Hardy (Black Cat).md 528239  =====
FOUND Communications & Linguistic Profile OFFSET 386831 
 Communications & Linguistic Profile

# UATU DOSSIER — FELICIA HARDY / BLACK CAT
## Section 6: Communications & Linguistic Profile

---

### 6.1 Baseline Voice

The subject's natural speaking voice — across forty years of canon and every writer who has handled her — sits in a narrow, identifiable band: **mid-tempo, low-affect, contralto-warm, smiling-through-the-line**. She rarely raises her voice. She rarely speaks quickly. The default delivery is the half-turned-away aside — a remark addressed nominally to the room but engineered to land on one specific person, who is meant to feel singled out by the privacy of it. She speaks to be overheard *by the right ear only*.

Sentence length is short to medium. She avoids the long-clause sentence; her grammar is tight, declarative, and almost entirely free of hedges ("I think," "maybe," "sort of" appear only when she is deliberately performing softness). The absence of hedging is a tell in itself — most speakers of her demographic and apparent affect use them as social lubricant; she does not need the lubrication because she is not seeking permission to speak. The grammar of someone who decided long ago she would never again wait to be invited.

Vocabulary is wider than she lets on. She codeswitches *downward* far more often than she codeswitches up — she will play to a fence's level, to a mark's level, to a thug's level. With Peter she occasionally lets a stray polysyllable or art-history reference slip and then half-buries it with a smirk, as if embarrassed to have shown the inventory. Empire State coursework, autodidactic reading in art and gemology, and a working museum-curator's vocabulary all live under the cat-burglar patter. The patter is the costume; the vocabulary is the face.

---

### 6.2 The Pet-Name System

The single most cited verbal habit in her file. She names people before she answers them. **"Lover," "sweetheart," "honey," "sugar," "darling," "kitten," "tiger,"** and — reserved almost exclusively for Peter and a small handful of equivalents — **"Spider."** The pet-name is not affection. It is a *frame-setting device*:

- It establishes that *she* is the one who gets to name.
- It collapses the interlocutor into a class of object (lover, kitten) that she controls the membership of.
- It pre-empts being named first, which would be a frame-loss.
- It functions as an affectionate **soft put-down** when deployed to strangers, equals, or adversaries.

The hierarchy is meaningful. "Spider" is reserved, intimate, and almost never said in front of others. "Lover" is the public-private hybrid — said in front of others *as if* private, to manufacture an audience-effect. "Sweetheart" and "sugar" are the workplace pet-names, deployed to fences, marks, henchmen, and police she is currently outrunning. "Kitten" is condescending and used on women she is dismissing. "Darling" is the high-society register, used when she is dressed up. She has never, in any era, used her own father's name without softening it; she has also never called Peter "Pete," which is data — "Pete" would be Mary Jane's register, and Felicia knows it.

When she stops using pet-names mid-conversation and switches to the actual proper noun — "Peter," cold — the temperature of the scene has changed and the partner has approximately one beat to recalibrate.

---

### 6.3 The Quip Architecture

She makes jokes constantly. The structure is consistent enough to map:

1. **The setup is the threat or the wound.** She does not riff out of nothing; she riffs *on* the dangerous content. A gun pointed at her produces a comment on the gun. A confession of feeling produces a comment on the confession.
2. **The punchline collapses the threat into a sexual or aesthetic register.** "Is that for me?" applied indiscriminately to weapons, wallets, and emotional vulnerability.
3. **The smile is held a beat longer than the joke earns.*

# COMMUNICATIONS & LINGUISTIC PROFILE — SUBJECT: MARY JANE WATSON

Classification: Longitudinal linguistic surveillance synthesis. Speech samples drawn from Lee/Romita introduction (ASM #42, 1966) through Wells run (present). Cross-referenced against alternate-continuity samples (Ultimate, Renew Your Vows, Spider-Girl flash-forwards) for stability of idiolect under different authorial hands.

---

## 1. BASELINE REGISTER — THE DEFAULT VOICE

Subject's default speaking voice is a mid-Atlantic American English overlaid with light residual Queens — the Queens surfaces only in two contexts: (a) emotional escalation, where dropped *g*'s and tightened vowels return ("nothin'," "talkin'," "ya kiddin' me"), and (b) deliberate code-switch downward when she wants to signal solidarity with a working-class interlocutor or undercut someone's pretension. Both are markers. The baseline she presents in neutral social settings is *acquired* — the speech of someone who consciously polished an accent to be castable, employable, and class-mobile. This is itself a linguistic tell: the standard register is the costume; the borough is the body underneath.

Speech rate is fast — measurably faster than Peter Parker's, faster than most of her female peer set in the canon. She talks *over* silences rather than into them. Pauses are managed: she will fill a beat with a small physical sound (laugh, exhale, a hummed *mm*) rather than allow dead air, because dead air is where the room can turn on her. The few instances on panel where she allows a long silence are diagnostic of either (a) full safety (late-arc Peter, Aunt May after the secret-share) or (b) total shutdown (separation arc, post-stillbirth).

Pitch travels higher in performance-mode (party scenes, public events, first encounters) and drops noticeably when she is alone with a trusted intimate — by the JMS run this drop is overt and Peter notices it on panel. The "real" voice is lower, slower, and approximately a third less verbose per beat than the public voice.

---

## 2. THE PARTY-GIRL REGISTER — INTRODUCTION-ERA AND ITS HALF-LIFE

The Lee/Romita-period voice is its own register and never fully retires; it gets recruited under specific conditions for the rest of her life. Markers:

- **Tiger.** The single most overdetermined word in the file. First deployed at introduction ("Face it, tiger — you just hit the jackpot"), it persists for sixty publication-years as a *targeted-affection signal* used exclusively for Peter. She does not call other men *tiger*. The word does three jobs at once: it claims him, it diminishes the threat-content of an emotional moment with mock-predator playfulness, and it preserves plausible deniability — *I'm not being sincere, I'm being cute*. When she uses *tiger* in a serious sentence, the seriousness is being smuggled. Track frequency: high in courtship, dips during marriage-routine, spikes around reconciliations and goodbyes. Its appearance in a goodbye is a near-certain marker that the goodbye is not final in her own internal model.
- **Daddy-O, Buster, Sport, Mister, Webhead, Web-head, Hot Stuff.** A rotating arsenal of mock-diminutives, period-coded to the 60s/70s in origin but kept alive into modern continuity as deliberate retro-cute affect. They are *all* deflection-vocatives: she nicknames people to manage them.
- **Italicized exclamations of delight** — "*Wow*," "*Crazy*," "*Wild*," "*Far out*" in early canon; updated to "*God*," "*Jesus*," "*Wow, okay*" later. The function is constant: she is performing surprise/pleasure at a volume designed to fill the room.
- **Self-third-person.** "MJ doesn't do mornings." "MJ needs coffee." Used sparingly, always under mild stress, always to put narrative distance between the speaking-self and the experiencing-self. A subtle but consistent dissociative-flag in the corpus.

 Communications & Linguistic Profile

# UATU DOSSIER — SUBJECT: JONATHAN LOWELL SPENCER STORM
## Section 7: Communications & Linguistic Profile

Classification: Behavioral Linguistics, Longitudinal
Source Base: Full canonical record of recorded speech, broadcast appearances, intra-team communication, courtroom and Congressional testimony (Civil War proceedings), recorded interviews, and observable private speech with intimates.

---

### 7.1 Baseline Register: The Default Voice

Subject's resting speech register is **mid-Atlantic American with the upstate-New-York vowels never fully scrubbed out** — a "Glenville substrate" that surfaces when he is tired, drunk, frightened, or speaking to his sister. In public-facing contexts he speaks in a polished, slightly broadcast-trained cadence picked up across decades of press junkets, talk-show appearances, and the brief Hollywood period; this overlay is thin and peels off under stress within a single sentence.

**Pitch:** baritone, mobile, with a habitual upward inflection at sentence-end that reads as either question or invitation depending on context. He uses uptalk *strategically* — it pulls the listener forward, demands their continued attention, and conveniently disguises declarative statements as collaborative ones. ("So we go in through the roof? And I take the big guy?") This is not Valley-girl uptalk; it is salesman uptalk, learned young.

**Pace:** fast at baseline (approximately 180–210 words per minute, against an American conversational median nearer 150), accelerating with excitement and — crucially — **also accelerating with grief or threat,** which is one of his most reliable tells. He does not slow down when wounded; he speeds up. The pace-as-tell will recur throughout this section.

**Volume:** moderate to loud; he projects without effort and rarely modulates downward unless deliberately performing intimacy. He does not whisper well. When forced to be quiet (stealth ops, hospital rooms), he becomes physically uncomfortable in a visible way.

**Vocabulary:** mid-tier active lexicon, surprisingly rich in **mechanical, aviation, and combustion vocabulary** (carburetors, stoichiometric ratios, flashpoints, autoignition temperatures, aviation phonetics, structural-stress terms), and conspicuously thin in **abstract, political, philosophical, and emotional vocabulary.** He knows the words for *what burns and how fast;* he does not have words for *what hurts and why.* This asymmetry is the single most diagnostic feature of his speech.

---

### 7.2 Register Switching Across Contexts

Subject operates in at least seven distinguishable speech registers, switching between them rapidly and often within a single conversation.

**Register A — Public/Press ("The Torch Voice").** Polished, quotable, structured in soundbites of 6–12 words. Heavy use of self-effacing humor ("Yeah, well, somebody had to do it and Reed's hair was already on fire — figuratively, for once"). Compulsively credits the team in interviews, almost always in the order *Reed, Sue, Ben, me,* which is not the order of operational importance but the order of family seniority — a tell about who he is performing for. He flirts with interviewers regardless of their gender, age, or interest level; this is autonomic, not predatory.

**Register B — Combat/Operational.** Sharp, clipped, aviation-flavored. Uses real radio discipline when it matters ("Torch in, ten o'clock high, target acquired"). Drops the comedy almost entirely *during* engagement but resumes it instantly between exchanges — sometimes mid-sentence as the threat resolves. Call-outs to teammates use first names with Sue and Reed, *"Ben"* never *"Grimm"* with Ben, and surnames or codenames with non-family allies. The shift from Register A to Register B is one of the cleanest tells he produces: the uptalk vanishes, sentence length halves, and the Glenville vowe

Instead he throttled down and let the flame contract to a controlled halo around his body. Beneath the apparition were eight tiny quadrotors, shedding reflective dust into the air. He could see the heat signatures clearly through the fog of light.
JOHNNY|[operational, into comms]|"Reed, eight projectors, thermal signature real. Phantom skull fake. Traffic-light damage real. Tell the police I'm not putting a nova blast into the river."
REED|[approving]|"I wasn't going to suggest it."
BEN|[over comms, dry]|"That's a first."
N|Johnny flicked a thin blue plume across the lead drone. The lens housing softened; the picture shuddered. Then the entire display grid went dark, briefly revealing a second, enormous face made from ordinary advertising panels. Mysterio smiled down at him from the skyline.
BECK|[broadcast, amused]|"The Human Torch. Always grateful for a spotlight."
JOHNNY|[automatic showman]|"You should've called my agent. My rates are criminal."
N|The smirk began to slip. Down on the sidewalk, a boy was looking up at the skull instead of watching for a truck. Johnny dropped, caught the boy at the edge of the curb, and landed as gently as a falling comet could manage.
BOY|[awestruck]|"I thought it was real."
JOHNNY|[quiet]|"Me too, for a second. That's all he needs."
N|The boy's mother ran into frame and embraced him. Nobody filmed the moment when Johnny looked away.
N|Above them, the advertisement panels blinked back to life with a message addressed to five people and one very confused city.
N|FRIDAY. 2:13 A.M. FINAL PERFORMANCE. NO REFUNDS.
###|04|The Man Who Won't Stay Dead
N|Wade Wilson had been offered a great deal of money to murder an optical engineer. That was an appalling sentence. It was also, Wade considered, a reasonably accurate description of his Wednesday.
N|His employer had supplied a photograph, a hotel room number, and a warning that Dr. Saira Vale was responsible for a proprietary security breach. A pleasant-voiced intermediary had suggested the job was a clean extraction. Wade had asked whether clean meant hospital clean or the kind of clean you saw in apartments after a murder investigation. The intermediary had stopped being pleasant.
WADE|[speaking to himself, brisk]|"All right, Mr. Wilson. Today's goals: acquire one scientist, do not be turned into a tasteful red smear, avoid additional existential dread before lunch. Achievable. Love a checklist."
N|He reached the hotel room by the balcony because the hallway was full of armed men pretending to be building maintenance. Three of them opened fire at the sight of him. A burst took him through the chest. He staggered, looked down at the hole in his suit, and sighed theatrically.
WADE|[exasperated]|"Okay. First, tailoring is expensive. Second, if you're maintenance, you're doing the wrong kind of preventative work."
N|He dropped the first guard with the flat of a sword and shot the second in the leg. The third kept firing after Wade had told him twice to stop. Wade shot him in the chest. The gunfire ended. One man lay dying on an expensive carpet, and the bill for that choice existed whether Wade was capable of paying it or not.
N|Dr. Vale emerged from the bathroom holding a shower rod like a spear. She had made her peace with several things in the last twenty minutes. A red man with two swords was not one of them.
VALE|[shaking but defiant]|"Did they send you to kill me?"
WADE|[slows]|"Technically, yes. In the interest of full transparency, I've been reviewing my career choices in real time."
VALE|[bitter]|"I wrote the safety interlock. Beck removed it. They sold the interface to the city and then used it to collect reaction data. There is a pilot test on Friday. People will run toward places that aren't exits. He knows what that does to a crowd."
N|Wade's smile, concealed under cloth, was suddenly work he had no energy for.
WADE|[dry, controlled]|"And Fisk?"
VALE|[urgent]|"Fisk financed deployment. Beck controls the system. The prism coordinates the master keys, but it's only one of three. They can activate from an independent rig under the old Astor platform."
N|The name landed like a stone in Wade's stomach. Six weeks earlier he had killed a programmer named Daniel Cho, a contractor who had refused to hand over a private research drive. Wade had accepted the client's description of Cho as a violent black-market broker. He had not checked. Cho's widow had received a box of his effects and an assurance that the investigation was ongoing.
N|Vale watched Wade recognize something. She was an engineer, not a mind reader; there was still enough in his posture to tell.
VALE|[sudden suspicion]|"You know what happened to Daniel."
WADE|[no joke]|"I was there."
VALE|[horrified]|"You killed him."
N|Wade didn't deny it. His healing factor repaired a bullet wound as they spoke. It could not repair the part of the conversation that mattered.
WADE|[plain]|"Yes."
N|Vale stepped back. The shower rod trembled. She looked toward the balcony, calculating whether the drop would kill her faster than the man in red.
WADE|[quiet]|"You have every reason to want me dead. You also have people coming up the stairs to finish the job. I'm going to move to that corner. You get to decide whether I take you out of here."
N|He moved. She stayed still for three breaths, then crossed the carpet toward the balcony. A grenade bounced against the hall door.
VALE|[furious]|"If you touch me without asking, I will jump."
WADE|[immediate]|"Understood. May I offer a deeply unpleasant piggyback ride?"
N|She hesitated exactly once, grabbed his shoulder, and allowed him to carry her through the open balcony door. The explosion turned the hotel curtains to flame behind them. They fell ten stories together. Wade placed himself beneath her and hit the roof of a parked armored van with a noise that would have made a metalworker look up.
N|Vale rolled off him, gasping. Wade looked at the sky. The van's roof had acquired his outline.
WADE|[weakly cheerful]|"Okay. I need to update the checklist. The dread is coming in early."
N|He put Dr. Vale in contact with a lawyer who had once represented him and still disliked him professionally. Then he called a number he'd saved under the contact name HUMAN FLAMETHROWER, ANNOYING.
JOHNNY|[on phone, distracted]|"If this is about the suit I burned, send the invoice to Reed."
WADE|[too bright]|"Good news, Candle Boy. I found the optical engineer behind your holographic fishbowl problem. Bad news, I may have committed a murder for her employer before I realized I was an accessory to the worst magic show since somebody pulled a rabbit out of a subpoena."
JOHNNY|[silence, then level]|"Are you joking?"
WADE|[flat]|"No."
N|Johnny did not ask for a funny version. He took the address and called Sue. For once, Wade found that more exhausting than a gunshot.
###|05|Mary Jane Doesn't Need a Cape
N|Wilson Fisk invited Mary Jane Watson to dinner at his private club because he believed everyone with a public-facing career could be bought with enough access. She accepted because she knew his belief was a better doorway than any disguise.
N|The dining room had three chandeliers, six enormous windows, and the hush of a place where a stranger's parking ticket might become another person's economic policy. Fisk received her at a table large enough to accommodate several arguments at once.
FISK|[pleasantly formal]|"Ms. Watson. You've brought a camera crew in the past. I admire your decision to come alone."
MJ|[bright, socially polished]|"They keep asking to film you. I keep telling them the camera adds ten pounds and you have a reputation to consider."
N|Fisk allowed himself a small smile. A lesser man might have tried to dominate the conversation with a correction. He preferred to wait for the moment when correction would matter.
FISK|[level]|"I understand you are reviewing the public-alert contracts. A performer has taken advantage of a vulnerable civic system. Unfortunate. We intend to help the city recover."
MJ|[light]|"How generous. There's a lovely symmetry to the company selling the lock and billing the city for changing it."
FISK|[quiet]|"Be careful, Ms. Watson. You're talking about matters with consequences far beyond your publicity work."
N|She set down her water glass. For one second, her father's old condescending tone was in the room, wearing a different man's voice. She did not perform her way around it.
MJ|[low, exact]|"I've spent twenty years getting powerful men to say something true while they thought they were explaining the world to me. This meeting is either useful to both of us or we're finished."
N|Fisk looked at her then, not at the glossy public image he had purchased from background investigators. For the first time he behaved as though the person across the table might be a risk.
FISK|[controlled]|"And what would make it useful?"
MJ|[businesslike]|"The City's emergency alert rollout uses your subcontractors. Beck is using the platform to stage attacks, and people have already been hurt. I need the original vendor chain, the test schedule, and the independent broadcast keys."
FISK|[cold courtesy]|"Those are allegations."
MJ|[unblinking]|"Then you'll have a pleasant time disproving them under oath."
N|She left before dessert. The waiter brought the check to an empty chair.
N|At the Baxter Building, the conference room looked like the aftermath of an argument between a research laboratory and an airport lounge. Peter occupied one corner, trying to hold his own injured wrist still. Felicia rested against the window in a borrowed black jacket. Johnny paced because a stationary Johnny was statistically rarer than a meteor shower. Dr. Vale had been given a cup of tea and enough distance from Wade to say yes to remaining in the room.
N|Wade sat in a chair with both swords on the table. Ben Grimm leaned on the back of it until Wade removed them.
BEN|[gravelly]|"House rules. No swords on the furniture."
WADE|[sourly playful]|"Really? This is the world's most advanced research building and you're worried about the furniture?"
BEN|[without warmth]|"I'm worried about you. Furniture's just easier to replace."
N|Mary Jane entered, put her coat on a chair, and slid a stack of documents across the table. No one gave her a briefing. She had been writing one on the way over.
MJ|[crisp]|"Fisk has three contractors. City Mirror owns the public displays, a shell corporation called Halcyon owns the drone fleet, and an Oscorp affiliate owns the optical hardware. Beck owns the fear. He does not own the broadcast infrastructure outright."
REED|[interested]|"That's very useful."
MJ|[dry]|"It was printed on the invoices."
N|She placed a second document beside the first: a purchase order marked LIVE MASS-EVACUATION STRESS TEST, FRIDAY 02:13. Peter read it twice.
PETER|[quietly]|"He wants the whole city to run."
VALE|[careful]|"Not the whole city. Ten mapped corridors. Commuter density, emergency perception, individual response under stress. The prism Felicia took can interrupt one command network, but Beck can switch to his underground rig. The hardware there also generates directed sound. The illusions can make a wall look like an exit. The wall will still be there when you hit it."
N|Johnny stopped pacing.
JOHNNY|[sharp]|"Then I burn the rig."
REED|[measured]|"You cannot simply burn a network from the outside. There are power supplies under public transit infrastructure, liquid cooling loops, a distributed projector mesh. A fire could trap passengers in the actual tunnels."
JOHNNY|[frustrated]|"I know how fire works, Reed."
SUE|[firm, quiet]|"Then use that knowledge. We need you to keep the actual structures safe while the others find the illusion."
N|Felicia spread the prism over a map. Its green light tracked the old Astor Station, closed decades earlier but never truly empty. The hardware's circuit paths spiraled beneath the city in geometry that was much too elegant for municipal procurement.
FELICIA|[considering]|"I can get into the vault. But I won't be your obedient little accessory, Reed. I choose my own exits."
MJ|[without looking up]|"Good. I need someone who notices exits. Beck's entire business model is lying about them."
N|A moment passed. Felicia regarded her with the surprise she reserved for someone saying exactly the right thing without asking for approval.
PETER|[trying to lighten it]|"Terrific. We're a team. Nobody has tried to kill anybody for almost five minutes. Personal best."
N|Wade raised a hand.
WADE|[serious under joke]|"I want to be clear that we're counting from when I arrived, not from the earlier chapter of my miserable life."
N|Dr. Vale's cup rattled as she set it down. Wade looked away.
N|Peter turned to him. He knew what Wade could do. He also knew what it meant to stand in a room with someone whose wrong decision had killed a person who was never coming back.
PETER|[hard]|"Tell us what you did for Fisk. All of it."
WADE|[low]|"I killed Daniel Cho. A programmer. They told me he was moving weapons. I didn't look. I took money for not looking."
N|Johnny exhaled. Ben's knuckles tightened against the chair. Peter seemed about to speak, then didn't.
MJ|[controlled, not kind]|"Dr. Vale decides whether you help her. We need the engineering. Nobody's asking her to forgive the murderer."
VALE|[through anger]|"He can carry equipment. He doesn't come near the controls."
N|Wade nodded once. There was no clever line available that wouldn't make the room worse.
N|An alarm chirped on Reed's tablet. Across Manhattan, every public screen carried the same image of Mysterio in his shimmering helmet. Beneath his head ran a countdown.
N|TWO HOURS TO CURTAIN.
N|Beck had started early. The city had not bought a ticket. It was already inside the theatre.

###|06|Everybody Gets the Monster They Ordered
N|At twelve minutes past midnight, the Empire State Building turned into a gigantic green hand.
N|The hand closed around the antenna, bent the skyline down like a toy, and began reaching toward Times Square. It was forty stories of carefully rendered nonsense. It could not touch a person. The pedestrians who bolted across Broadway could touch taxis, curb edges, and one another. They did.
N|At the same moment, a subway platform on the East Side appeared to fill with water. Commuters climbed turnstiles and crushed against doors that had been programmed to display EXIT on the wrong side of the station. The real doors remained locked for construction. The advertisements on three buildings displayed a smiling message from City Mirror: PLEASE REMAIN CALM.
N|New Yorkers responded with the language they reserved for anyone who told them to remain calm.
N|Spider-Man swung above Forty-Second Street and nearly lost his grip when a gigantic purple face floated through the clouds. It resembled Galactus, the Devourer of Worlds, except the proportions were wrong and the eyes belonged to a billboard model for dental implants.
PETER|[fast, furious]|"That is the least convincing cosmic god I've ever seen. And I once fought a man whose superpower was being a wheel."
N|His spider-sense informed him of the real threats. Three people running under an active construction lift. A delivery van careening toward a bus stop. An armed drone with mass and velocity cutting through the phony monster's neck. He could not follow every signal. He chose.
N|Two webs hauled the construction lift sideways. He released the third web toward the van, missed, recovered, and caught its bumper. The drone struck his back before he could finish the move. He crashed through a bus shelter in a spray of safety glass.
N|The people he'd pulled from the construction zone ran past him, staring at a flying mountain that wasn't there.
PETER|[through pain, into comms]|"Beck's got whole blocks in this. Real drones among the fake monsters. If you see something ridiculous, don't ignore it. Verify whether it can break your ribs first."
JOHNNY|[over comms, brisk]|"I have eight drones over the river, and an extremely rude dragon with the thermal signature of a parking garage. I'm taking down the drones. Leave the dragon to the tourists."
N|Johnny arced over the East River, a slim blue streak in the dark. In a dozen phone videos it looked as though the Human Torch had challenged an enormous winged creature to single combat. In reality he was disabling projectors the size of furniture, one at a time, while keeping his heat plume far enough from the harbor traffic not to ignite anything with people in it.
N|The false dragon opened its mouth. Sue's voice came out.
SUE|[recorded illusion, distressed]|"Johnny, don't leave me."
N|The sound made him hesitate at exactly the wrong point in the turn. His flame flared white. It came close enough to a real ferry that passengers ducked from the heat.
JOHNNY|[short, alarmed]|"Shit. Sorry. Ferry, take the south channel. I'm pulling away."
N|He slowed, killed the extravagant white fire, and flew lower in a narrow band of orange heat that he could control with his eyes closed. The camera helicopters lost their glamorous shot. That was probably good for the helicopters.
N|Beneath Astor Place, Felicia Hardy had reached a corridor that didn't appear on any city map. Bruno had supplied the blueprints, an access badge, and several unrepeatable comments about property management. An armored guard stood outside the technical entrance. He saw a woman approaching through the dark and raised his rifle.
GUARD|[commanding]|"Hands where I can see them."
FELICIA|[pleasant]|"Aren't they?"
N|She turned a cartwheel off the wall, touched the rifle with one fingertip as she passed, and let bad probability take care of its magazine catch. The weapon emptied its cartridges onto the floor. She kicked the guard's legs out from under him and caught his head before it struck a pipe.
FELICIA|[to unconscious guard]|"I hate paperwork. Don't make me fill out a medical form."
N|The corridor around her changed. One moment she stood under fluorescent lights. The next she was in a college hallway with polished floors and a door she hadn't opened in years. A man's voice spoke from inside. Beck hadn't invented her fear. He had read enough to copy the shape of it.
N|Felicia stopped moving. The building smelled of paint and old metal, but the image reached past the smell to somewhere she didn't normally visit without choosing to. She gripped a copper conduit and counted the screws under her thumb. One. Two. Three. Real.
FELICIA|[cold, unhurried]|"You don't get to use that room."
BECK|[from speakers, too intimate]|"Every audience has a wound, Ms. Hardy. I merely light it."
FELICIA|[flat]|"And every thief has a knife. You should have checked your inventory."
N|She slid the blade through the projector's power coupling. The hallway vanished, taking its voice with it. She heard the machines around her now: motors humming, coolant moving, hundreds of small relays clicking in time like beetles beneath a floor.
N|In an adjoining station, Wade found a security officer not yet old enough to shave convincingly. The man's rifle wavered. A company badge hung crookedly from his vest.
GUARD|[terrified]|"Don't come closer."
N|Wade raised both hands. His mouth prepared a joke, then abandoned it. He remembered the hotel carpet. He remembered Daniel Cho. Every shortcut ended with somebody else paying.
WADE|[slow, plain]|"I'm not here for you. Take the stairs behind you. Keep your hands empty."
N|The guard fled. Wade let him go. Then a second man emerged from the dark and fired. Wade shot back. The man fell behind a control console, alive and swearing. Wade crawled toward him through a hail of sparks, removed the man's gun, and wrapped a tourniquet around the wound with the efficiency of somebody who'd done this too many times to mistake it for redemption.
N|Dr. Vale had made herself available from Reed's lab but still refused to be in a room with Wade. The communication channel gave her enough distance to work.
VALE|[over comms, urgent]|"Wilson, do not touch the cooling controls. They feed a commuter tunnel above you. Beck built a bypass that turns off mechanical ventilation if the core loses power. If you pull the wrong plug, you'll make the real hazard."
WADE|[strained]|"Love that for us. Building designed by a supervillain, maintained by idiots, secured by a murder contractor. Five-star rating."
N|He looked at a red switch marked EMERGENCY ISOLATION and found himself grateful that somebody had printed the words in large letters. He left it alone.
N|At the Baxter Building, Mary Jane watched Reed's city model light up in red corridors. She wasn't going to fly across Manhattan. She wasn't going to punch Beck, and she was not going to pretend a stolen password made her a genius engineer. She did know how to make the largest audience in New York look at the same thing at the same time.
MJ|[rapid, focused]|"Reed, City Mirror has an accessible public-alert takeover path, right? The standard municipal emergency channel."
REED|[without looking up]|"Yes, but the authenticated payload would have to be approved and its display zones mapped."
MJ|[sharp]|"Good. The city has a duty officer for that. I already have her on the line. Stop explaining why the form is complicated and tell me which buttons have to be pressed."
N|There was a pause. Then Reed smiled, with the delighted surprise of a scientist observing an elegant solution he had not produced himself.
REED|[respectful]|"Six confirmations. I'll walk you through them."
N|MJ picked up her phone and asked for the person authorized to tell the city what was real. It took three calls, one threat of a public records request, and the deployment of a tone she had inherited from years of bad auditions. Finally, a woman answered from an operations center.
DISPATCH|[tired, professional]|"City alert desk. I need your credentials."
MJ|[measured]|"You'll have them. And you'll have the evidence to stand behind this message when somebody asks why you sent it."
N|The first honest citywide alert was sent at 12:31. It said, in ordinary words, that some projected hazards were false, that physical hazards still existed, and that the nearest safe place was not necessarily the brightest exit sign. For six blocks, the crowd stopped running toward a lie.
N|It did not save everyone. A man on the East Side had already died after going through a construction barrier he could not see. A woman had been injured in a stairwell. No one in the room said they were winning.
N|On the primary broadcast channel, Mysterio raised his arms to an invisible audience.
BECK|[theatrical, angry]|"Ladies and gentlemen, the critics have arrived early."
###|07|A Very Bad Team-Building Exercise
N|The abandoned Astor station had a secret. It was not a haunted tunnel or a forgotten laboratory. It was a working server farm disguised as utility equipment, powered by stolen contracts and watched by men who thought the word proprietary excused almost anything.
N|The prism Felicia carried could cancel the synchronization key for one network. Beck had built a second and third because he was, among other things, a thorough bastard. Reed could find the frequencies. Dr. Vale could tell them which lines were genuine. None of that put a hand on the physical controls three levels underground.
N|Felicia crossed a narrow gantry above the core. She saw the second key embedded in a rotating lens drum beyond a bank of blue-white capacitors. She could reach it by climbing the guardrail. She could also reach it through a side passage with a locked gate and a perfectly ordinary floor. She chose the gate. Today she had enough trouble without making gravity her understudy.
FELICIA|[into comms]|"Prism two is in the west core. I can see it. There's a human-sized gap and a lock that costs more than most apartments."
PETER|[over comms, quick]|"I'm three blocks out. Can you wait?"
FELICIA|[dry]|"What do you think, Spider? I'm a statue with a savings account?"
N|She put away the lock picks and drove the heel of her boot against the gate's latch. The metal held. Her probability field made the latch spring lose a pin on the second impact. The gate opened. A heavy bolt fell from above, missed her shoulder, and struck an insulated cable, sending a flood of sparks across the gantry.
N|She hated, increasingly, the fact that luck never sent an invoice until afterward.
FELICIA|[into comms, irritated]|"Reminder to me: when this is over, have somebody sensible build me a field meter."
REED|[over comms]|"Probability deformation doesn't behave like a continuous field in the conventional sense."
FELICIA|[sweetly lethal]|"Then have somebody sensible explain it to you first."
N|Up on the street, Johnny fought three armored drone clusters while Ben Grimm held the masonry of a collapsing storefront together with both hands. The illusion made it look like an enormous monster was tearing through the building. The storefront had actually failed when a drone's kinetic charge struck an old support.
BEN|[grunting]|"Torch! I could use a hand that's not on fire!"
JOHNNY|[from above]|"I only brought the two, big guy. Both are on fire."
N|Johnny dropped, extinguished his flames just before landing, and wedged himself under a cracked lintel beside Ben. The weight was not imaginary. For a moment he was simply a man holding a bad piece of the world up with somebody he loved.
BEN|[low]|"That's it. Hold. You can talk later."
N|Johnny held. He wanted to fly because flying made a body feel like it had escaped. Here, the only useful thing was not moving.
JOHNNY|[through clenched teeth]|"You are absolutely going to tell everyone this was my best angle."
BEN|[almost smiling]|"Kid, I seen ya from a lot of angles. None of 'em are your best."
N|Their laughter came out ugly and grateful. Then city crews shored the opening, and Johnny moved away before they could see his hands shaking.
N|At a broadcast booth above Astor Place, MJ stood beside a municipal operator and watched video from the subway. She had insisted that each emergency announcement separate what the cameras showed from what ground personnel verified. People were calling her alarmist, dishonest, and a corporate tool within the same thirty seconds. She counted that as a credible start.
N|An incoming call displayed an unlisted number.
FISK|[over phone, measured]|"Ms. Watson. You've created considerable public confusion."
MJ|[very controlled]|"No. Somebody created the confusion. I'm naming it."
FISK|[mild threat]|"I could make certain your work is available to a much wider audience than you're accustomed to."
MJ|[coldly amused]|"That's normally how public work functions."
N|Fisk waited. MJ could hear him calculating a better angle.
FISK|[pleasantly menacing]|"People with no powers are safest when they remember what they are."
MJ|[flat]|"And people with power are dangerous when they forget who they answer to."
N|She ended the call. Then she stared at the dark screen for half a second longer than she wanted anyone to see. There was fear. She had learned long ago that fear and obedience were not the same thing.
N|Spider-Man dropped into the Astor station as a column of projected flame filled the stairwell. His spider-sense gave him no warning from the fire because the fire was not there. It did warn him about the jagged steel plate suspended above it. He webbed the plate in place and made a mental note to thank the evolutionary process for granting him a sixth sense that, while not perfect, at least remained unimpressed by special effects.
N|Wade was waiting in the machine hall beside an injured guard whose bleeding he had stopped.
WADE|[quiet, trying for humor]|"Hi, Spider-Man. Big fan. Terrible timing. We have a haunted screensaver, an electrical hazard, and the world's saddest human burrito."
PETER|[cold]|"Did you shoot him?"
WADE|[matter-of-fact]|"He shot me first. Yes. He's alive. I'm hoping that makes the meeting less horrible."
N|Peter looked at the guard, the blood, the abandoned rifle. His wrist ached. He could not make himself like Wade, and he could not deny the guard had a chance because Wade had stopped shooting.
PETER|[short]|"Get him out. Do not come back with the guns."
WADE|[a little wounded, hides it]|"You know, I get that a lot."
N|Peter headed for the main platform. Beyond the doorway stood Uncle Ben Parker, hands in the pockets of his old coat, looking at him with an expression Peter had spent years imagining.
BEN_PARKER|[illusion, gentle]|"Petey. You don't have to keep running."
N|Spider-Man stopped. It was the kindest sentence anybody could have used against him. The voice had the right warmth, the right worn edges, the small pause before his name. A shadow moved where no shadow should have moved. The dead man stood beneath a light that wasn't real.
N|Peter raised one trembling hand, then lowered it. He loved that voice. He didn't have to give a machine permission to use it.
PETER|[low, grieving, steady]|"You're not him. You never got to know him."
N|The false Ben dissolved into a row of projector lenses. Peter tore the bank from the wall and ran toward the real machinery. He did not feel better. There was no reason he should have.
###|08|The Fishbowl Breaks
N|The main chamber of Beck's theatre was three stories high. Lens rings circled a central column, projecting entire streets onto hanging curtains of engineered vapor. In one pane, Manhattan drowned. In another, the Fantastic Four lay dead. In a third, Peter Parker watched himself failing to catch a falling woman again and again, each replay a fraction slower than the last.
N|Mysterio occupied a raised platform at the center. The costume was immaculate: green armor, purple cape, globe helmet swirling with smoke. He bowed as Spider-Man stepped from the service passage.
BECK|[thrilled]|"Ah, there he is. Leading man. Always late to rehearsal."
PETER|[ragged joke]|"Sorry, fishbowl. Your invitation was in my spam folder between the vitamin ads and your last eight comeback tours."
BECK|[cruelly delighted]|"There it is. The joke. You make one when you're frightened, two when you're cornered, and twelve when you're about to fail someone. I can write you from memory."
N|Peter scanned the chamber, marking the projector housings, ventilation shafts, physical cabling. He knew the tricks, but Beck had arranged them in layers. Every false wall concealed a real object, and every real object could be presented as something else.
PETER|[tense]|"You went to all this trouble to humiliate me?"
BECK|[offended]|"You? No. You're the supporting part. I wanted a city to see what happens when heroes stop controlling the narrative. People will remember what I showed them long after they remember who ran the projectors."
N|That was the nearest thing to honesty Beck had offered all night. He did not want Peter dead. He wanted Peter alive long enough to witness an audience believe the wrong story.
N|The platform opened. A real armored drone fired a steel cable at Spider-Man, masked by the projection of a fragile paper bird. Peter ducked, swung, and hit the drone from below. Its engine smashed into a lens ring. A dozen versions of Aunt May appeared around the room, each shouting his name from a different fire.
N|Peter knew they were false. They still made him look.
PETER|[fast, near panic]|"Can you please pick one emotional theme? The production design is getting a little desperate!"
N|From a side passage, Felicia vaulted onto the projection gantry. The phantom Aunts vanished under her shadow. She landed without drama, knife in hand, and saw the prism nest behind Beck's platform.
FELICIA|[smiling, with menace]|"Hello, Quentin. Pretty room. Terrible taste."
BECK|[mock intimate]|"Felicia. I've made something special for you."
N|The walls became her childhood home, then a college corridor, then a room she had locked away. Her breath stopped for a beat. She heard the voice again. She had already found the way out once; this time Beck changed where it was.
FELICIA|[cold]|"I don't care what pictures you bought. I know this building."
N|She slashed a cable. One of the false walls winked out. Behind it was an empty shaft. Her second step almost went into open air. She caught the railing by one hand and hung above the core. Her probability field made a loose screw fall from the far gantry, striking the rotating lens drum. The entire room shuddered. Beck's composition began to unravel.
N|Spider-Man saw her fall. He fired a web to catch her. At that precise instant, Beck projected a second Felicia falling in the opposite direction. One was a picture. One was a person. Peter followed the spider-sense, not the shape, and caught the real woman by the wrist.
PETER|[straining]|"Please don't make me choose between two cats. One was plenty!"
FELICIA|[breathless, very real laugh]|"Stop talking and pull!"
N|He hauled her onto the platform. Her hand brushed his forearm for an instant too long to be tactical. The room had not made either of them less complicated.
N|Above the city, Johnny Storm took Reed's coordinates and flew into the most dangerous part of the projection canopy. The temperature in the center of the false sky was ordinary night air. He changed the wavelength of his flame until it scattered the optical particles without flash-heating the building surfaces. The dragon, the giant hand, the drowned skyline all began to dissolve. The cameras caught a shape nobody had ordered: the Human Torch, almost motionless, painting a clean band of air through a city-sized lie.
JOHNNY|[over comms, focused]|"Reed, I'm washing the display out. Tell me when the cooling fans stop."
REED|[over comms]|"You're right at the safe limit. Ten seconds, perhaps twelve."
JOHNNY|[tight]|"Then we've got ten."
N|His body wanted motion. He held his position. The flame was not an escape tonight. It was a tool, and he was the one holding it.
N|MJ's emergency broadcast interrupted Beck's feed on every municipal screen Reed and the city operators could reach. Her face appeared without theatrical lighting, without a logo, with fatigue she had chosen not to hide.
MJ|[broadcast, calm and urgent]|"New York, this is Mary Jane Watson with the city emergency desk. The giant figures are projections. Some drones and falling structures are real. Stay away from damaged buildings. Follow verified street-level directions, not images. You do not need to outrun a monster that isn't there."
N|Beck roared at the interruption. The villain who could project whole cities had forgotten to plan for somebody using the public microphone without acting.
BECK|[shouting]|"Cut her feed!"
N|On the lower platform, Wade came up through a maintenance hatch without his guns. He had taken the injured guard to a transit officer, and he had removed his swords at Spider-Man's request. Now he carried a manual coolant valve wheel the size of a dinner plate.
WADE|[dry]|"Hi. Someone requested a nonviolent entrance. I brought a plumbing supply. Please tell me this is the correct genre."
VALE|[over comms]|"That's the bypass wheel. Don't turn it until I tell you."
WADE|[obedient]|"Words I don't often hear from engineers. I can wait."
N|He crouched beside the conduit. His healing factor wasn't going to help anyone if he turned the wrong piece of machinery and put smoke into an occupied train tunnel.
N|Beck lunged for the manual override. Felicia cut him off, but he triggered a final charge with a hidden switch. The central lens assembly sheared loose, dragging cabling and a hundred kilograms of equipment toward the platform where he stood. The override would burn the authentication records and end the show. It would also crush him.
PETER|[urgent]|"Felicia, get the keys! I'll take Beck!"
FELICIA|[instant]|"He's going to burn everything!"
PETER|[firm]|"I know!"
N|Peter shot a web into the falling gantry and pulled Beck clear, knowing he was giving up the only immediate route to destroying the evidence. He could hear a voice in his head saying the perfect decision had to save the person and the proof. There wasn't enough time for perfect. He took the person.
N|Felicia made her own decision. She threw herself through the collapsing lens cage, grabbed the physical authentication cartridge, and let a wave of probability break the bracket above her. A shard of glass caught her upper arm. The cartridge came free. A sprinkler ruptured, and steam blossomed across the platform. She staggered toward Peter with blood on her sleeve and the stolen proof held high.
FELICIA|[through pain, fierce]|"There. Everyone happy?"
N|Wade heard Vale's voice in his ear.
VALE|[immediate]|"Now. One quarter turn clockwise. Slowly."
N|He followed each word. The cooling system shuddered, redirected, and held. No plume of hot vapor went into the commuter tunnel. Above them, the citywide illusion went to pieces. The cloud monster disappeared. The false exits blinked out. Ordinary advertisements returned with the comforting indifference of commercial life.
N|Beck lay under Spider-Man's knee, furious and breathing. His globe helmet had cracked, exposing one frightened human eye. The man who had made fear into a public utility had forgotten what it was like to be seen without the special effects.
BECK|[hoarse]|"You should have let it finish."
PETER|[exhausted]|"You should have hired a better writer."
N|It was a weak joke. Peter let it be weak. He tightened the webbing around Beck's hands and sat down hard on the cracked concrete. He had saved the villain. He had not saved every person the villain had hurt. No one would be writing a neat ending for that.
###|09|Nobody Gets the Same Ending
N|By dawn the numbers stopped changing quite so quickly. Two people had died during the false evacuation. More than forty had been injured. The final count would be revised by agencies that needed names, addresses and verification rather than a dramatic concluding paragraph. A thousand recordings showed monsters that hadn't been there. The injuries were there. So were the grief and the lawsuits.
N|The first police briefing named Quentin Beck as the principal suspect in the attacks. The second confirmed an inquiry into City Mirror's contracts and Fisk-affiliated subcontractors. Neither briefing promised Fisk would be convicted. MJ, who had seen powerful men survive worse documents than these, refused to call an investigation a verdict.
N|At a hearing three days later, she placed the procurement records on the table beside the stolen authentication cartridge and a signed engineering declaration from Dr. Vale. She did not sell them as proof of every allegation. She separated what the hardware showed, what the contracts showed, and what witnesses still had to establish.
MJ|[publicly controlled]|"An emergency message is supposed to reduce uncertainty. This network manufactured it. That decision was made by people. Ask for their names, ask who approved the tests, and don't let the spectacle become a substitute for the paperwork."
N|Fisk's lawyers described her as a celebrity with no engineering degree. The next morning, the city ledger ran above that comment in every major newspaper. It was not justice. It was a very good start on making evasion expensive.
N|Johnny returned to the Baxter Building and found Ben sitting in the ruined kitchen beside a new toaster. Ben had placed a note on it: DO NOT IMPROVE.
JOHNNY|[mildly offended]|"I feel like there's a subtext here."
BEN|[gruff, affectionate]|"Yeah. The subtext is you owe me breakfast."
N|Johnny leaned against the counter. Without a crowd or a camera, the room was almost unbearable. He had flown close enough to a ferry to frighten its passengers. He had helped save the city. Neither event canceled the other.
JOHNNY|[low]|"I got too hot over the river. I almost hit them."
BEN|[steady]|"I saw."
JOHNNY|[careful]|"You gonna tell Sue?"
BEN|[flat]|"Already did. Told her you saw it and corrected before somebody paid for it. If you're askin' whether I think you're a screw-up, that's a different question."
JOHNNY|[after a pause]|"Yeah. I guess it is."
N|Ben cut a slice of bread and dropped it into the ordinary new toaster. He did not demand a confession. He made breakfast. Johnny stayed for it.
N|At a small apartment in Queens, Peter Parker sat across from Mary Jane and tried to describe why he'd gone after Beck instead of the evidence. He started with duty, moved into physics, and was halfway through an explanation involving angles when MJ held up her palm.
MJ|[soft but firm]|"I know why you saved him. I was there. What I don't know is whether you know you're allowed to stop explaining the decision after you've made it."
PETER|[tired]|"Two people died, MJ."
MJ|[without softening]|"Yes. And if you keep treating the dead as a debt you can repay by never sleeping, there will be another person I lose without anybody having to kill him."
N|He looked toward the window. That was his oldest move: find the city before the person in the room could finish speaking.
PETER|[very quiet]|"I don't know how to make it smaller."
MJ|[honest]|"I didn't ask you to. I asked you not to make me carry it in silence with you."
N|She moved her cup aside but didn't move closer. He stayed in his chair. It wasn't reconciliation and it wasn't rejection. It was two adults in a kitchen choosing not to flee during an ordinary difficult conversation. Neither wore a costume, which made the whole thing strangely dangerous.
N|Felicia spent the afternoon recovering from stitches in the apartment above one of her safehouses. Bruno insisted on inventorying the stolen goods. She told him to skip the green prism. It was in evidence now. One of the most valuable objects she'd ever taken had ended up in a police locker.
BRUNO|[amused]|"You're a terrible thief."
FELICIA|[smiling]|"Don't tell anyone. It would ruin my career."
N|A message from Peter appeared on her phone: You saved the proof. Thank you. She typed three answers, deleted each one, and sent a photograph of the ruined sleeve of her jacket instead.
N|Her second message followed a minute later: You owe me a new one, Spider.
N|She didn't leave town. That was not a cure for anything. It was simply a choice she made that afternoon.
N|Wade turned over his weapons, his contract records, and a statement concerning Daniel Cho to an attorney. The statement could expose him to prosecution. It could also allow Cho's widow to challenge the story the client had sold her. He did not attempt a dramatic apology on the woman's doorstep. A letter from a killer was not necessarily a gift the recipient had asked for.
N|He paid for a witness advocate and gave the lawyer permission to disclose the truth when the family wanted it. On the way out, he passed a vending machine that rejected his money three times. He almost shot it. Instead he bought a soda from a corner store like an ordinary human being, which he found humiliating.
WADE|[to the can, with great dignity]|"We are trying new things, beverage. Respect the journey."
N|The soda tasted terrible. It was one of the few consequences of his week that he was qualified to complain about.
###|10|The City, Unedited
N|New York was not cured by dawn, by hearings, by a captured man in a broken globe helmet, or by the newspaper headline that eventually read THE CITY THAT LIED. It was still a place where a commuter could be late, an alarm could be wrong, and somebody in an expensive suit could make a profitable argument that nobody had authorized the danger.
N|It was also a place where Spider-Man took a night off and spent forty minutes trying not to look at his phone. Mary Jane didn't praise him for it. She let him choose a movie and told him when his choice was terrible.
N|Johnny Storm went to the garage with Ben Grimm and rebuilt a motorcycle neither of them needed. They argued about carburetors. When the argument turned loud, it was because both were enjoying themselves. Sue heard them through the workshop wall and smiled without making it an event.
N|Felicia Hardy returned a stolen necklace to a woman who hadn't known it was missing. The return wasn't altruism in the grand sense. The necklace reminded her of something beautiful that deserved to remain in its owner's drawer. She robbed a corrupt shipping magnate the next night. Growth did not require her to become somebody else.
N|Wade Wilson sat in a lawyer's office, made one joke too many, apologized to a person who hadn't asked him to, and then stayed for the difficult questions. Nobody applauded. There was no money in it. He showed up anyway.
N|At the Daily Bugle, J. Jonah Jameson announced that the entire Mysterio incident proved masked vigilantes were a menace. Then he spent six minutes yelling about why Spider-Man hadn't stopped the incident sooner. His producers did not see a contradiction. Ratings were excellent.
JONAH|[furious, thunderous]|"If he's going to swing around my city in a mask, I expect results! And when he gets results, I expect a reasonable explanation of who authorized them!"
N|Peter watched the clip on his phone in the kitchen. He laughed, a small, tired, surprised laugh that wasn't meant to keep anyone from asking how he felt.
PETER|[warmly]|"I think I finally understand the man. He just wants the city to file a complaint in triplicate."
MJ|[from the other room, teasing]|"He'd want the fourth copy for himself."
N|Somewhere downtown, a lawyer sealed a drive containing the City Mirror contracts. Somewhere uptown, Fisk held a meeting about risk exposure. And in the evidence room, the last stolen prism glowed faintly in its case, harmless for now and uncomfortably beautiful.
N|Uatu the Watcher had observed empires breaking under the weight of their own myths. He knew this city had always told stories about its heroes, and that the heroes often believed the versions told about themselves. There was no universal correction for that mistake.
N|There were, however, people who chose to check what was real. People who returned to a difficult room instead of running. People who accepted that saving someone did not confer ownership of them, or of their gratitude.
N|Above Queens, a red-and-blue figure crossed the skyline just after sunset. He wasn't on patrol. He'd remembered that Aunt May had asked him to bring home bread. In a city that lied for a living, that very small, very ordinary errand turned out to be entirely true.

N|Not from the harbor. Not from history. Only from the eyes of eleven men standing inside a decommissioned subway station beneath Midtown, each of whom had been paid enough to sign a nondisclosure agreement and too little to ask what it meant.
