bash -lc cat > /mnt/data/build_thursday_clause.py <<'PY'
from pathlib import Path
import re, json, html, zipfile, datetime, textwrap, uuid, os

ROOT=Path('/mnt/data')
base='Earth_GK_The_Thursday_Clause'
source=(ROOT/(base+'_master.txt')).read_text('utf-8')
lines=source.splitlines()
chapters=[]
chapter=None
seg=None
for raw in lines:
    line=raw.strip()
    if line.startswith('### '):
        chapter={'title':line[4:].strip(),'segments':[]}
        chapters.append(chapter)
        seg=None
    elif line.startswith('@'):
        if chapter is None: raise ValueError('Segment before chapter')
        m=re.fullmatch(r'@([A-Z_.]+)\s+\[([^\]]+)\]',line)
        if not m: raise ValueError('Bad tag '+line)
        seg={'speaker':m.group(1),'cue':m.group(2),'text':''}
        chapter['segments'].append(seg)
    elif line and seg is not None:
        seg['text']+=((' ' if seg['text'] else '')+line)
for ch in chapters:
    for s in ch['segments']:
        if not s['text']: raise ValueError('Empty segment')

speakers={
 'FELICIA.NARRATION': {'name':'Felicia Hardy — Narrator','voice_id_key':'felicia_hardy','type':'narration','description':'First-person, warm contralto, unhurried, velvet-over-blade. Every line of non-dialogue belongs only to Felicia.'},
 'FELICIA.DIALOGUE': {'name':'Felicia Hardy — Dialogue','voice_id_key':'felicia_hardy','type':'dialogue','description':'Same actor as narration; shorter and more pointed, with guarded pet names and rare unmasked vulnerability.'},
 'WADE': {'name':'Wade Wilson / Deadpool','voice_id_key':'wade_wilson','type':'dialogue','description':'Elastic, theatrical, American; jokes accelerate under stress, but unforced vulnerability is clear and low. Not a constant clown voice.'},
 'PETER': {'name':'Peter Parker / Spider-Man','voice_id_key':'peter_parker','type':'dialogue','description':'New York adult tenor, rapid self-correcting patter in combat, gentler and quieter under genuine danger or intimacy.'},
 'FAKE_PETER': {'name':'Chameleon impersonating Peter','voice_id_key':'false_peter','type':'dialogue','description':'Cast or direct near Peter voice, but noticeably too rested, smooth and confident. Do not make it cartoonishly evil.'},
 'MJ': {'name':'Mary Jane Watson','voice_id_key':'mary_jane_watson','type':'dialogue','description':'Quick, bright, socially poised; authoritative without volume. Slows to a lower intimate register for boundaries.'},
 'JOHNNY': {'name':'Johnny Storm / Human Torch','voice_id_key':'johnny_storm','type':'dialogue','description':'Brash and boyishly warm, speed and buoyancy. Under acute load becomes lucid, precise and competent.'},
 'SHOCKER': {'name':'Herman Schultz / Shocker','voice_id_key':'shocker','type':'dialogue','description':'Irritated, nasal-edged and defensive, loud because he knows he is not respected.'},
 'CHAMELEON_AS_FELICIA': {'name':'Chameleon impersonating Felicia','voice_id_key':'false_felicia','type':'dialogue','description':'An almost-correct imitation of Felicia with imprecise tempo and faux-intimacy; distinct from the true narrator.'},
 'MARISOL': {'name':'Marisol — Server','voice_id_key':'marisol','type':'dialogue','description':'Dry, unflappable Midtown service-worker briskness and warmth.'},
 'SALES_DIRECTOR': {'name':'Ms. Vale — Sales director','voice_id_key':'ms_vale','type':'dialogue','description':'Professional polish stretched tight over growing apprehension.'},
 'BRUNO': {'name':'Bruno — Felicia’s crewmate','voice_id_key':'bruno','type':'dialogue','description':'Sleepy, sharp, understated; warm allegiance camouflaged with irritation.'}
}
segments=[]
for ci,ch in enumerate(chapters,1):
    for si,s in enumerate(ch['segments'],1):
        if s['speaker'] not in speakers: raise ValueError(s['speaker'])
        s['id']=f'{ci:02d}-{si:03d}'
        s['chapter']=ci
        segments.append(s)

words=sum(len(re.findall(r"\b[\w']+\b",x['text'])) for x in segments)
assert len(chapters)==7
assert all(x['speaker']=='FELICIA.NARRATION' for x in segments if x['speaker'].endswith('.NARRATION'))
assert all(x['speaker'].startswith('FELICIA.') for x in segments if 'NARRATION' in x['speaker'])
assert segments[0]['speaker']=='FELICIA.NARRATION'
assert 'eleven-thirty' in segments[0]['text'].lower() and 'cheesecake factory' in segments[0]['text'].lower()
assert 'Thursday' in segments[0]['text']

# Clean manuscript (no tech tags), then directed script
manus=['# Earth-GK: The Thursday Clause','*A Grizzly Knights story — told entirely by Felicia Hardy*','',
       'Unofficial Marvel alternate-universe fiction. The characters are used in a noncommercial, transformative fan story.','']
for ch in chapters:
    manus.append('## '+ch['title'])
    manus.append('')
    for s in ch['segments']:
        manus += [s['text'],'']
(ROOT/(base+'_Manuscript.md')).write_text('\n'.join(manus).strip()+'\n','utf-8')

perf=['# EARTH-GK: THE THURSDAY CLAUSE — FULL-CAST PERFORMANCE SCRIPT',
      'Narrative POV: **Felicia Hardy only**. All unattributed narration is first-person Felicia, not a Watcher or an omniscient narrator.',
      'The cues in brackets are **director guidance**, not spoken words. They are not guaranteed provider-specific synthesis syntax.',
      'The **same Felicia voice** must perform narration and her dialogue. The impostor voices are separately cast/directed.',
      'There is no canonical clinical diagnosis asserted for Peter. Earth-GK characterizes attraction and relationship possibilities, not assumed consent.','']
for ch in chapters:
    perf += ['## '+ch['title'],'']
    for s in ch['segments']:
        perf += [f"**[{s['id']}] {speakers[s['speaker']]['name'].upper()}** — *{s['cue']}*",s['text'],'']
(ROOT/(base+'_Full_Cast_Script.md')).write_text('\n'.join(perf).strip()+'\n','utf-8')

bible=['# EARTH-GK: THE THURSDAY CLAUSE — AUDIOBOOK PRODUCTION BIBLE','',
       '## Brief','',
       f'- Format: first-person noir-comedy Marvel fanfiction, {len(chapters)} chapters, {words:,} spoken words, {len(segments)} isolated performance segments.',
       '- Time and place: 11:30 a.m. Thursday at a Cheesecake Factory in Midtown Manhattan, followed by a boutique heist and its personal aftermath.',
       '- Felicia and Wade have been secret once-weekly day-drinking shopping best friends for seven years. Both have a crush on Peter.',
       '- Cast ALL spoken dialogue separately; Felicia alone narrates the scene, her thoughts, and transitions. No external omniscient narrator.',
       '- Genre: adult superhero action-comedy with romantic ambiguity, meaningful consent, and unromantic consequences of hypervigilance.',
       '- Unofficial noncommercial fiction; characters belong to their respective owners.','',
       '## Character voices / direction','']
for key,v in speakers.items():
    bible.append(f"- **{v['name']}** (`{key}`): {v['description']}")
bible += ['', '## Directing principles','',
         '1. **Felicia POV is inviolable.** Her narration is collected, exact and occasionally wicked. She may infer, but cannot know another person’s unspoken thoughts. Let a flicker of pain break through only when her control fails.',
         '2. **Wade is not the joke.** He jokes, and his timing can be obscene; his loyalty and sexual/romantic interest are genuine. There is a deliberate sharp tone change during the Shocker fight and confession.',
         '3. **Peter is not a diagnosis.** He is an extremely competent hero whose self-blame and fear of receiving affection create comic and painful friction. His attraction to multiple genders/people is not itself pathology.',
         '4. **MJ has independent agency.** She manages danger, names what she wants, and never consents by proxy. Do not cast her as a jealous stereotype or a relationship adjudicator.',
         '5. **Fake Peter and fake Felicia** should almost pass. Their difference is behavioral tempo: the counterfeit Peter is too comfortable accepting and exploiting attention; the counterfeit Felicia is theatrical without the real woman’s precision.',
         '6. **All Marvel powers matter.** Spidey’s disabled warning ability changes his tactical behavior, Wade absorbs danger he can recover from, Johnny manages heat with skill, and Felicia’s craft provides the critical extraction. The action is not an EMS rescue narrative.',
         '7. **No dramatic music under confessions.** Leave room tone and actual silences. Brisk comic cross-talk earns those silences.',
         '', '## Recommended sound palette','',
         '- Opening: midmorning restaurant air, ceramic, ice, subdued chatter. No musical quotation from existing films.',
         '- Vesper and Vale: soft shoe impacts, jewelry case taps, shutter drop with ringing decay.',
         '- Asterion: low subtle pressure/oscillation that vanishes instantly at extraction; do not overplay.',
         '- Finale: near-silence in fitting room, then restaurant environment on the next Thursday.',
         '', '## ElevenLabs / casting handoff','',
         '- The EPUB is the clean reading copy; it is **not** polluted with acting tags.',
         '- The full-cast script and `Cast_Timeline.json` are the authoritative segmentation and speaker map for a multi-voice render.',
         '- Cues like `[quiet]` and `[dry]` are directorial descriptions and need adaptation to whichever synthesis model or editing workflow is used. They should never be read aloud as dialogue.',
         '- Do not use an unauthorized real performer voice clone; select or license original synthetic/acted voices.',
         '- The local scratch audio is only a timing/structure audition, not an ElevenLabs or human-performed recording.','']
(ROOT/(base+'_Production_Bible.md')).write_text('\n'.join(bible),'utf-8')

cast_timeline={
 'title':'Earth-GK: The Thursday Clause',
 'narrator':'Felicia Hardy',
 'word_count':words,
 'chapters':[{'index':i,'title':ch['title'],'segment_ids':[s['id'] for s in ch['segments']]} for i,ch in enumerate(chapters,1)],
 'speakers':speakers,
 'segments':[{'id':s['id'],'chapter':s['chapter'],'speaker':s['speaker'],'voice_id_key':speakers[s['speaker']]['voice_id_key'],'performance_cue':s['cue'],'text':s['text']} for s in segments]
}
(ROOT/(base+'_Cast_Timeline.json')).write_text(json.dumps(cast_timeline,indent=2,ensure_ascii=False)+'\n','utf-8')

# Standalone EPUB3 with EPUB2 NCX fallback
uid='urn:uuid:59e1294a-f1ce-4bb3-8497-26028f7154c6'
cover_html='''<!DOCTYPE html><html xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1999/xhtml" lang="en"><head><meta charset="utf-8"/><title>Cover</title><link rel="stylesheet" type="text/css" href="style.css"/></head><body class="cover"><div class="rule"></div><p class="eyebrow">GRIZZLY KNIGHTS · EARTH-GK</p><h1>THE<br/>THURSDAY<br/><em>CLAUSE</em></h1><p class="tagline">No heroics before dessert.</p><div class="rule"></div><p class="byline">A FELICIA HARDY STORY</p><p class="small">A first-person, full-cast Marvel alternate-universe adventure</p></body></html>'''
intro_html='''<!DOCTYPE html><html xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1999/xhtml" lang="en"><head><meta charset="utf-8"/><title>About the Story</title><link rel="stylesheet" type="text/css" href="style.css"/></head><body><section class="front"><h2>About this edition</h2><p>Felicia Hardy tells this story. css='''@charset "UTF-8";
:root{color-scheme:light}

def doc(title,body):
    return f'<!DOCTYPE html><html xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1999/xhtml" lang="en"><head><meta charset="utf-8"/><title>{html.escape(title)}</title><link rel="stylesheet" type="text/css" href="style.css"/></head><body>{body}</body></html>'
chapter_html=[]
for i,ch in enumerate(chapters,1):
    paras=[]
    for s in ch['segments']:
        is_dialogue=(speakers[s['speaker']]['type']=='dialogue')
        cssclass='dialogue' if is_dialogue else 'narration'
        paras.append(f'<p class="{cssclass}" data-speaker="{s["speaker"]}" id="s{s["id"]}">{html.escape(s["text"])}</p>')
    content=f'<section epub:type="chapter" xmlns:epub="http://www.idpf.org/2007/ops" class="chapter-start" id="chapter{i}"><h2>{html.escape(ch["title"])}</h2>\n'+ '\n'.join(paras)+'</section>'
    chapter_html.append(doc(ch['title'],content))

navitems='\n'.join(f'<li><a href="chap_{i:02d}.xhtml">{html.escape(ch["title"])}</a></li>' for i,ch in enumerate(chapters,1))
nav='''<!DOCTYPE html><html xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1999/xhtml" xmlns:epub="http://www.idpf.org/2007/ops" lang="en"><head><meta charset="utf-8"/><title>Contents</title><link rel="stylesheet" type="text/css" href="style.css"/></head><body><nav epub:type="toc" id="toc"><h2>Contents</h2><ol>'''+navitems+'</ol></nav></body></html>'
manifest='\n'.join(f'<item id="chap{i}" href="chap_{i:02d}.xhtml" media-type="application/xhtml+xml"/>' for i in range(1,len(chapters)+1))
spine='\n'.join(f'<itemref idref="chap{i}"/>' for i in range(1,len(chapters)+1))
opf=f'''<?xml version="1.0" encoding="utf-8"?>
<package xmlns="http://www.idpf.org/2007/opf" version="3.0" unique-identifier="bookid" xml:lang="en">
 <metadata xmlns:dc="http://purl.org/dc/elements/1.1/" xmlns:dcterms="http://purl.org/dc/terms/">
  <dc:identifier id="bookid">{uid}</dc:identifier><dc:title>Earth-GK: The Thursday Clause</dc:title>
  <dc:creator>Grizzly Knights</dc:creator><dc:language>en-US</dc:language>
  <dc:description>A full-cast superhero story narrated exclusively by Felicia Hardy, beginning at 11:30 Thursday at a Midtown Cheesecake Factory.</dc:description>
  <dc:rights>Unofficial noncommercial transformative fanfiction. Characters are property of their respective owners.</dc:rights>
  <meta property="dcterms:modified">2026-10-28T00:00:00Z</meta>
 </metadata>
 <manifest><item id="css" href="style.css" media-type="text/css"/>
 <item id="cover" href="cover.xhtml" media-type="application/xhtml+xml"/>
 <item id="intro" href="about.xhtml" media-type="application/xhtml+xml"/>
 <item id="nav" href="nav.xhtml" media-type="application/xhtml+xml" properties="nav"/>
 <item id="ncx" href="toc.ncx" media-type="application/x-dtbncx+xml"/>
 {manifest}
 </manifest>
 <spine toc="ncx"><itemref idref="cover"/><itemref idref="intro"/>{spine}</spine>
</package>'''
ncx_items='\n'.join(f'''<navPoint id="navPoint{i}" playOrder="{i}"><navLabel><text>{html.escape(ch['title'])}</text></navLabel><content src="chap_{i:02d}.xhtml"/></navPoint>''' for i,ch in enumerate(chapters,1))
ncx=f'''<?xml version="1.0" encoding="UTF-8"?>
<ncx xmlns="http://www.daisy.org/z3986/2005/ncx/" version="2005-1"><head><meta name="dtb:uid" content="{uid}"/><meta name="dtb:depth" content="1"/><meta name="dtb:totalPageCount" content="0"/><meta name="dtb:maxPageNumber" content="0"/></head><docTitle><text>Earth-GK: The Thursday Clause</text></docTitle><navMap>{ncx_items}</navMap></ncx>'''
container='''<?xml version="1.0" encoding="UTF-8"?><container version="1.0" xmlns="urn:oasis:names:tc:opendocument:xmlns:container"><rootfiles><rootfile full-path="OEBPS/content.opf" media-type="application/oebps-package+xml"/></rootfiles></container>'''
epubpath=ROOT/(base+'.epub')
with zipfile.ZipFile(epubpath,'w') as z:
    z.writestr('mimetype','application/epub+zip',compress_type=zipfile.ZIP_STORED)
    for n,content in [
      ('META-INF/container.xml',container),('OEBPS/content.opf',opf),('OEBPS/toc.ncx',ncx),('OEBPS/style.css',css),('OEBPS/cover.xhtml',cover_html),('OEBPS/about.xhtml',intro_html),('OEBPS/nav.xhtml',nav)]:
       z.writestr(n,content,compress_type=zipfile.ZIP_DEFLATED)
    for i,c in enumerate(chapter_html,1): z.writestr(f'OEBPS/chap_{i:02d}.xhtml',c,compress_type=zipfile.ZIP_DEFLATED)

# Verify XML, structure, and that actor names are not inserted into book prose
from lxml import etree
with zipfile.ZipFile(epubpath) as z:
    assert z.testzip() is None
    assert z.namelist()[0]=='mimetype'
    assert z.getinfo('mimetype').compress_type==zipfile.ZIP_STORED
    for n in z.namelist():
       if n.endswith(('.xhtml','.opf','.ncx','.xml')): etree.fromstring(z.read(n))
    for i,ch in enumerate(chapters,1):
       h=z.read(f'OEBPS/chap_{i:02d}.xhtml').decode('utf-8')
       assert h.count('data-speaker=')==len(ch['segments'])
       assert '<h2>' in h
       assert '[warm,' not in h and '[dry]' not in h
print(json.dumps({'chapters':len(chapters),'segments':len(segments),'spoken_words':words,'speakers':list(speakers),'epub_kb':epubpath.stat().st_size//1024,'validation':'passed'},indent=2))

bash -lc cat > /mnt/data/render_thursday_scratch.py <<'PY'
import json, subprocess, io, sys, math, zipfile, os, tempfile, time
from pathlib import Path
from concurrent.futures import ThreadPoolExecutor, as_completed
from pydub import AudioSegment

ROOT=Path('/mnt/data')
base='Earth_GK_The_Thursday_Clause'
timeline=json.loads((ROOT/(base+'_Cast_Timeline.json')).read_text())
segments=timeline['segments']
profiles={
 'FELICIA.NARRATION':('en-us+f3',158,48,145),
 'FELICIA.DIALOGUE':('en-us+f3',164,49,149),
 'WADE':('en-us+m2',188,58,150),
 'PETER':('en-us+m3',184,47,151),
 'FAKE_PETER':('en-us+m3',155,46,153),
 'MJ':('en-us+f4',174,61,143),
 'JOHNNY':('en-us+m4',187,62,146),
 'SHOCKER':('en-us+m6',172,44,157),
 'CHAMELEON_AS_FELICIA':('en-us+f2',156,49,151),
 'MARISOL':('en-us+f1',177,57,148),
 'SALES_DIRECTOR':('en-uk-rp+f3',155,52,149),
 'BRUNO':('en-us+m5',158,39,150)
}

def render(tup):
 i,s=tup
 voice,speed,pitch,amp=profiles[s['speaker']]
 # --stdout causes eSpeak to render wav bytes directly; no text file needed.
 r=subprocess.run(['espeak','--stdout','-v',voice,'-s',str(speed),'-p',str(pitch),'-a',str(amp),s['text']],capture_output=True,timeout=90)
 if r.returncode:
     raise RuntimeError((i,s['speaker'],r.stderr.decode('utf-8',errors='replace')[:250]))
 audio=AudioSegment.from_wav(io.BytesIO(r.stdout))
 return i,audio

start=time.time()
results=[None]*len(segments)
with ThreadPoolExecutor(max_workers=10) as ex:
 futures=[ex.submit(render,(i,s)) for i,s in enumerate(segments)]
 for count,f in enumerate(as_completed(futures),1):
  i,a=f.result()
  results[i]=a
  if count%60==0: print('rendered',count,'of',len(segments),'elapsed',round(time.time()-start,1),flush=True)

sound=AudioSegment.silent(duration=480,frame_rate=22050).set_channels(1).set_sample_width(2)
chapter_starts=[]
previous_chapter=None
for s,a in zip(segments,results):
 ch=s['chapter']
 if previous_chapter!=ch:
  if previous_chapter is not None:
    sound += AudioSegment.silent(duration=1050,frame_rate=22050).set_channels(1).set_sample_width(2)
  chapter_starts.append({'chapter':ch,'title':timeline['chapters'][ch-1]['title'],'start_ms':len(sound)})
  previous_chapter=ch
 sound += a
 # Natural but audible paragraph boundaries.
 pause=105 if s['speaker'] in ('WADE','PETER','MJ','JOHNNY','SHOCKER','FAKE_PETER','FELICIA.DIALOGUE') else 185
 sound += AudioSegment.silent(duration=pause,frame_rate=22050).set_channels(1).set_sample_width(2)
print('combined',round(len(sound)/60000,2),'minutes', 'elapsed',round(time.time()-start,1),flush=True)
# WAV intermediate, then one MP3 compression with ID3 chapters
wav=ROOT/(base+'_Scratch_temp.wav')
sound.export(str(wav),format='wav')
del sound,results
length_ms=int(round(float(subprocess.check_output(['ffprobe','-v','error','-show_entries','format=duration','-of','default=nw=1:nk=1',str(wav)]).strip())*1000))
metadata=[';FFMETADATA1','title=Earth-GK: The Thursday Clause — Local Scratch Full Cast','artist=Grizzly Knights (computer synthesized audition)','album=Earth-GK','comment=Scratch voices only. Not ElevenLabs, not human-performed.']
for i,ch in enumerate(chapter_starts):
 end=chapter_starts[i+1]['start_ms'] if i+1<len(chapter_starts) else length_ms
 metadata.extend(['[CHAPTER]','TIMEBASE=1/1000',f"START={ch['start_ms']}",f'END={end}',f"title={ch['title']}"])
meta=ROOT/(base+'_Scratch_ffmetadata.txt')
meta.write_text('\n'.join(metadata)+'\n')
mp3=ROOT/(base+'_Local_Full_Cast_Scratch.mp3')
cmd=['ffmpeg','-hide_banner','-nostdin','-loglevel','error','-y','-i',str(wav),'-i',str(meta),'-map','0:a','-map_metadata','1','-map_chapters','1','-codec:a','libmp3lame','-b:a','64k','-ar','22050','-ac','1',str(mp3)]
subprocess.run(cmd,check=True,timeout=150)
print('MP3 done',mp3.stat().st_size,'elapsed',round(time.time()-start,1),flush=True)
probe=json.loads(subprocess.check_output(['ffprobe','-v','error','-print_format','json','-show_chapters','-show_entries','format=duration,size:stream=codec_name',str(mp3)]))
assert len(probe['chapters'])==7
assert float(probe['format']['duration'])>1000
(ROOT/(base+'_Chapter_Timings.json')).write_text(json.dumps({'duration_ms':int(float(probe['format']['duration'])*1000),'chapters':chapter_starts,'audio_file':mp3.name,'format':'MP3 with ID3 chapters, local eSpeak synthetic audition only'},indent=2)+'\n')
wav.unlink()
meta.unlink()
print('VERIFIED',{'duration_min':round(float(probe['format']['duration'])/60,1),'chapters':len(probe['chapters']),'audio_mb':round(mp3.stat().st_size/1e6,1),'elapsed_seconds':round(time.time()-start,1)},flush=True)

from pathlib import Path
import json,zipfile,subprocess,hashlib
from lxml import etree
R=Path('/mnt/data')

required=[
 b+'.epub',
 b+'_Manuscript.md',
 b+'_Full_Cast_Script.md',

 b+'_Chapter_Timings.json',
 b+'_Local_Full_Cast_Scratch.mp3'
]

 assert p.exists() and p.stat().st_size>500, name
D=json.loads((R/(b+'_Cast_Timeline.json')).read_text())
assert len(D['chapters'])==7
assert len(D['segments'])==243
assert all(s['speaker'].startswith('FELICIA.') for s in D['segments'] if 'NARRATION' in s['speaker'])
assert D['segments'][0]['speaker']=='FELICIA.NARRATION'
assert all(s['text'] for s in D['segments'])
roles=set(s['speaker'] for s in D['segments'])
assert {'FELICIA.NARRATION','WADE','PETER','FAKE_PETER','MJ','JOHNNY','CHAMELEON_AS_FELICIA','SHOCKER'} <= roles
with zipfile.ZipFile(R/(b+'.epub')) as epub:

  assert len(p)==len(ch['segment_ids'])
  assert not any('FELICIA.NARRATION' in ''.join(t.itertext()) for t in p)
probe=json.loads(subprocess.check_output(['ffprobe','-v','error','-show_chapters','-show_entries','format=duration,size','-of','json',str(R/(b+'_Local_Full_Cast_Scratch.mp3'))]))

mins=float(probe['format']['duration'])/60
first=D['segments'][0]['text']
assert 'eleven-thirty' in first and 'Cheesecake Factory in Midtown' in first and 'Thursday' in first
assert any('seven years' in x['text'] or 'seventh year' in x['text'] for x in D['segments'])
assert any('crush on you' in x['text'] for x in D['segments'])
assert any('attracted to men' in x['text'] for x in D['segments'])
readme=f'''EARTH-GK: THE THURSDAY CLAUSE — COMPLETE AUDIOBOOK PRODUCTION KIT

STORY
  7 chapters, {D['word_count']} spoken words, {len(D['segments'])} tagged segments,
  {len(set(s['voice_id_key'] for s in D['segments']))} character/role voice IDs.

Narrative point of view: Felicia Hardy only. She narrates her own actions,
observations, and inferences, while each other person speaks in their own voice.

Start: 11:30 on Thursday morning at a Cheesecake Factory in Midtown.
Felicia and Wade are already drinking. They are secret weekly shopping BFFs
for seven years, and both have feelings for Peter Parker.

FILES
 - {b}.epub : Clean, readable EPUB3 with EPUB2-compatible chapter nav.
 - {b}_Manuscript.md : Editable reading text with no acting tags.
 - {b}_Full_Cast_Script.md : Every line attributed and directed.
 - {b}_Production_Bible.md : Cast characterizations and sound design notes.
 - {b}_Cast_Timeline.json : Speaker-keyed segment text and metadata.
 - {b}_Chapter_Timings.json : Timing markers for the local scratch audio.
 - {b}_Local_Full_Cast_Scratch.mp3 : {mins:.1f} minute, seven-chapter locally
    synthesized audition with differentiated voices. Not a professional
    performance and NOT created using ElevenLabs.

ELEVENLABS HANDOFF
  For natural full-cast narration, import the EPUB to an authorized audiobook
  workflow, then consult the script and JSON for character voice assignments.
  Only Felicia is narrator. The two impostors should sound almost (not exactly)
  like the characters they mimic. Bracketed directions are not to be spoken.
  The text is original fanfiction; use legally permitted/authorized voices,
  rather than cloning identifiable actors without their consent.

QA

Unofficial noncommercial Marvel fanfiction; not endorsed by Marvel.

(R/(b+'_README.txt')).write_text(readme)
required.append(b+'_README.txt')
zipf=R/(b+'_Complete_Audiobook_Kit.zip')
with zipfile.ZipFile(zipf,'w',compression=zipfile.ZIP_DEFLATED,compresslevel=5) as z:
 for n in required:z.write(R/n,arcname=n)
with zipfile.ZipFile(zipf) as z:
 assert z.testzip() is None
 assert set(z.namelist())==set(required)
 assert len(z.namelist())==8
print(json.dumps({'epub_bytes':(R/(b+'.epub')).stat().st_size,'audio_minutes':round(mins,2),'audio_bytes':(R/(b+'_Local_Full_Cast_Scratch.mp3')).stat().st_size,'kit_bytes':zipf.stat().st_size,'files':required,'status':'all checked'},indent=2))

## Earth-GK: The Thursday Clause

*An original, seven-chapter Marvel misadventure, narrated entirely by Felicia Hardy.*

**[Read the EPUB](sandbox:/mnt/data/Earth_GK_The_Thursday_Clause.epub)**

It begins at exactly 11:30 on Thursday morning, with Felicia and Wade already drinking at the Cheesecake Factory in Midtown. They've been secretly doing this every week for seven years.

There are shopping bags, expensive cocktails, terrible romantic decisions, and an exceptionally convincing counterfeit Peter Parker.

The full-cast dialogue includes Wade, Peter, MJ, Johnny Storm, Chameleon, Shocker, and several supporting characters. But **Felicia is the only narrator**, and the story never leaves her perspective.

Here's the opening:

> At eleven-thirty on a Thursday morning, Wade Wilson was halfway through his second cocktail at a Cheesecake Factory in Midtown, wearing sunglasses that cost more than the restaurant's espresso machine and explaining why Peter Parker needed a boyfriend.
>
> “Not a boyfriend instead of a girlfriend. That is a false binary, and I am a complicated modern gentleman. I mean a boyfriend in addition to the six thousand other things the man thinks he has to be.”
>
> I held up my glass. The ice had surrendered. So had the first drink.

The audio and complete production package are ready.

Seven years of long lunches, expensive mistakes, and shopping trips that began with exquisite intentions and ended with one of us carrying something that the authorities would describe as evidence. We had rules. No costumes. No jobs. No killing anyone in a retail establishment. Absolutely no trying to fix Peter Parker before dessert.

We had simply discovered a little corner of Manhattan where no one needed either of us to perform our job description. He didn't need to be the joke. I didn't need to be the cat. We could eat too much, drink something with a ridiculous name, and discuss a man neither of us had the slightest intention of possessing. 
 Not that possession had ever been our strongest subject.

# NEVER AGAIN

*A Grizzly Knights / Earth-GK novel*

## Prologue — The Child in the Church

The first thing Erik Lehnsherr noticed was the silence.

Not peace. He had lived long enough to know the difference.

The machines had stopped moving. Beyond the broken windows of the church, Genosha burned in long orange columns, and the sea carried the ash inland. It was the kind of silence that followed a gunshot in a crowded room. People were waiting for the next one.

A little girl sat against the altar with both hands over her ears. She had blue scales along her temples and forearms, fine and iridescent as the underside of a fish. The rest of her wore a school uniform: one shoe, one sock, a yellow cardigan buttoned crookedly over a white blouse. A school uniform seemed an especially cruel thing to find in the wreckage of a nation. It made the catastrophe look scheduled.

Erik knelt.

“My name is Erik,” he told her.

She watched his helmet. At eight or nine she was old enough to know what it meant and young enough to hope its owner could do anything.

“I know,” she said. “You're Magneto.”

“That's what people call me. What's your name?”

“Nia.”

“Are you hurt, Nia?”

She shook her head too quickly.

He had been a father. He knew the meaning of that particular lie. He did not challenge it yet.

A tremor passed through the building. Erik felt every nail in the pews, every length of rebar beneath the floor, the broken bell suspended above them by a support he had made of nothing more substantial than attention. The bell weighed more than the girl did. He concentrated on it because concentration was easier than rage.

“Where's my dad?” Nia asked.

There it was. The next gunshot.

Erik had no answer. There were bodies beneath the streets. Some were so still that the metal in their blood no longer told him anything useful. Others were moving, calling, reaching from spaces where rescue would require hours. He could tell Nia none of that.

“I don't know,” he said.

The girl's face changed. Not because he had frightened her. Because he had told the truth, and she was deciding whether to forgive him for it.

“Everybody else said he's coming.”

“Everybody else should not have said that.”

She began to cry with such exhausted discipline that the sound barely escaped her mouth.

Erik sat on the cold stone beside her. He did not pick her up. Children who had been grabbed, pulled, and dragged through a catastrophe deserved the dignity of a choice.

“May I?” he asked, offering his hand.

She took two fingers. After a moment she climbed into his lap and held the scorched edge of his cape.

“Hush, baby,” he whispered. “Ain't no one touching you now. Not while I'm breathing.”

He meant it. That was the problem with the most dangerous promises. They were often sincere.

Outside, a Sentinel lay crushed against the steps. Its severed eye was still blinking. Erik reached across the church with his power and turned the great red lens toward the ground. It resisted. Something inside the machine was still transmitting.

A tiny voice came from beneath the sound of rain.

“Did they hate me?”

Erik looked at Nia. There was ash in the ridges of her scales.

“They didn't know you.”

“That wasn't what I asked.”

He closed his eyes.

“No,” he said, though he could not promise the truth of it. “Not you, Nia. They hated an idea they made of you.”

For a while she was quiet. Then she said, “That's stupid.”

“Yes,” Erik said. “It is.”

The severed Sentinel eye blinked one final time. Within its ruined circuitry he felt a pattern, an elegant sequence of tiny magnetic switches. Not merely a weapon. A filing system. A machine that had been sent here to kill and to remember whom it killed.

A white symbol glimmered on the inside of the lens: a red diamond cut into four smaller shapes.

Erik knew that symbol. He had spent decades hoping never to see it again.

A voice rose from the back of the church, where the survivors gathered beneath the least damaged part of the roof.

“Magneto,” a man called. “What do we do now?”

The question passed through the room. It was in the faces of the injured, the parents with no children, the children with no parents. They looked at him as if he were a door in a building with no exits.

He held Nia closer and felt how little she weighed.

Above them, the bell began to ring without a human hand.

Not for worship.

For the dead.

And in the ringing, Erik heard his mother.

Not her voice. Not yet.

The sound of a window rattling in a house in Mississippi, a very long time ago.

## Chapter One — The House with No Curtains

Mississippi, 1959.

When Erik was eleven, his mother taught reading in the front room of a house with no curtains.

The neighbors sometimes asked why. Everyone in the county had curtains. Privacy was an ordinary precaution, especially for a family whose name no one pronounced correctly and whose worship took place on a different day from everyone else's.

Miriam Lehnsherr would point to the window and say, “A curtain lets people pretend they didn't see.”

It was not always a political statement. Sometimes it was simply about the chickens getting into her tomatoes. But she said it with the same calm certainty in either case, and Erik loved her for that.

His father, Isaac, worked on engines and farm equipment in a shed behind the house. He could take a broken tractor apart and put it together without naming a single prayer, but on Friday evenings he washed his hands twice, put on a clean shirt, and spoke the old words with a care that made Erik understand language could be a kind of shelter.

Isaac's people had fled Europe. Miriam's family had been Black and Jewish in the South longer than anyone in the county cared to count. Their son had his mother's brown eyes and his father's stubborn way of standing with both feet squarely planted when he was challenged. He had been told that certain things in the world belonged together. He had spent most of his childhood discovering that the world disagreed.

He preferred the workshop to the classroom. Tools had honest intentions. A wrench wanted to turn. A pulley wanted to lift. People wanted many things and said something else.

Miriam came home one afternoon with a split lip and half a loaf of bread wrapped in brown paper.

Erik was standing on a stool, trying to make a compass point north with a magnet he had found in the shop.

“Who did that?” he asked.

“Somebody who wanted me to remember he had a hand.”

“Who?”

“Finish your arithmetic.”

He hated that answer. It was an adult's way of closing a door without moving.

When she noticed his expression, Miriam set down the bread and knelt so they could see eye to eye.

“There are men who want you frightened,” she told him. “And there are people who need you careful. Don't confuse those two things.”

“Then tell me his name.”

“So you can do what?”

Erik had no reply. He would discover years later that having no reply did not mean he had no plan.

The trouble had begun with a registration book. The county had a rule for every person who wanted to vote and a second, unwritten rule for who was permitted to satisfy the first. Miriam was helping people prepare for the questions they would be asked. She kept carbon copies. She wrote down who had been turned away. Her front room filled after dusk with men and women who spoke quietly until she put a pencil in their hands.

One of them was Mr. Bell, a widower with a leg damaged in the war. He had fought overseas and come home to discover that his uniform could be folded into a drawer but his skin could not.

“Mrs. Lehnsherr,” he said one night, “you're going to get yourself killed.”

Miriam passed him another sheet of paper.

“Then you'd better learn your state constitution, Mr. Bell. I'm not going to be here to read it to you forever.”

People laughed. Erik, listening from the hall, did not.

A week later, the sheriff came to the house with two deputies. He removed his hat in the doorway, which might have looked like manners to a stranger. Erik knew it as the gesture men made before they wanted something.

“Mrs. Lehnsherr,” the sheriff said, “you've been keeping records you have no business keeping.”

“They're names,” Miriam replied. “They belong to the people who gave them to me.”

“And you've been filling folks' heads with ideas.”

“They brought their heads with them.”

One deputy laughed before he remembered not to.

Erik was in the workshop. He could hear every word through the open window. A handful of screws began rolling toward his left palm. He stared at them, astonished, and they stopped.

The sheriff left an envelope on the table. Inside was a photograph of the front of the house, taken at night. A white cross had been drawn over the window where Miriam taught.

Isaac wanted them to leave.

“Just for a month,” he said. “We can go north. There's a cousin in Detroit.”

Miriam took his hand across the table.

“And the people who have no cousin?”

“This isn't about them,” Isaac said. “This is about keeping you alive.”

“It can be about both.”

Erik listened from the stairs. That night he heard his parents argue without raising their voices. Quiet anger frightened him more than shouting.

Three days later, the county road was blocked by two trucks. Miriam had gone to deliver the registration copies to a lawyer in the next town. Erik had insisted on coming. She made him stay in the truck while she walked toward the men, because she'd recognized one of them and thought recognition would protect them.

He remembered the road dust in the headlights. He remembered his mother saying, “You are not taking my papers.” He remembered his own pulse beating through his fingertips as the loose bolts in the truck door began to turn.

There were too many men.

A hand pulled him from the passenger seat. He struck the ground hard enough to taste dirt. Someone pinned his shoulders. Somewhere nearby, his mother called his name once.

Afterward, memory became a thing with gaps cut into it. He remembered a rope passing over a branch, but not who threw it. He remembered the sheriff's hat on a truck seat. He remembered light moving through leaves. He remembered the sound of grown men laughing in the dark.

He remembered the truck's axle tearing loose from its housing as if it had decided to become a living animal.

Metal screamed. A door folded inward. The headlights went out. Men ran and fell. For one magnificent, terrible instant, Erik thought the world had learned to obey him.

It had not learned to give anything back.

By dawn Miriam was dead.

The county called it an unfortunate incident involving persons unknown. The sheriff said no one had seen enough to identify anyone. There were witnesses in every house along the road. Curtains remained closed.

Isaac brought their son home in the back of a neighbor's pickup. Erik sat with his mother's ring clenched in his fist. He had found it in the dirt. He would spend sixty-seven years wondering whether it had fallen or been pulled off.

That Friday, Isaac lit the candles alone.

Erik stood at the window. He kept expecting the glass to break or the road to give up another truckload of men. His father said the prayer for the dead with a voice that failed only once.

“What happened to Mama?” Erik asked, though he knew.

“They murdered her.”

“Who?”

Isaac looked at the window. He could not make himself say the names either.

Erik touched the metal latch. It rose, trembling, and hovered between his fingers.

His father turned and saw it.

Neither spoke for a long time.

At last Isaac said, “Your mother did not die so that you would spend your life dying with her.”

Erik closed his hand around the latch until it bent.

He was eleven. He believed his father had just asked him to forget.

He would be an old man before he understood that Isaac had asked something much harder.

## Chapter Two — The Price of Being Reasonable

Westchester County, New York. Present day.

The first children arrived at the school at four in the morning. They came in three buses and a borrowed delivery van, with blankets from an airport and the smell of seawater caught in their clothes.

Charles Xavier was waiting in the entrance hall.

He wore a dark suit without a tie. He had not slept. He had spent the previous nine hours hearing the minds of the living trying to locate the minds of the dead, which was a different kind of scream and, after enough hours, almost impossible to distinguish from his own grief.

A boy with a glass-clear left arm refused to leave the bus. The driver looked helplessly at the headmaster.

Charles moved his chair forward, stopped a few feet from the doors, and addressed the boy directly.

“Good morning. I'm Charles. You're welcome here.”

The boy watched his eyes.

“Are you reading my mind?”

“No.”

“Can you?”

“Yes.”

“Then don't.”

“I won't.”

The boy remained in his seat. Charles waited. It took almost five minutes. There were days when five minutes was a small price for trust and days when it was all a person had to spare. That morning, it was the most important thing he could give.

Ororo Munroe came in from the drive with sleet melting in her white hair. She had brought the storm down gently to keep the roads passable; the sky had seemed reluctant to cooperate with anything gentle.

“Thirty-four,” she said. “Nine need medical attention. Two have family we can contact. The rest have nobody we've been able to find.”

Charles looked toward the stairwell, where a teacher was guiding a girl with feathered hands to a bedroom.

“Thirty-four is not enough.”

“No,” Ororo said. “It isn't.”

She did not comfort him. Ororo had been a leader long enough to know the difference between comfort and absolution.

In his office an encrypted message waited. The subject line read: INTERNATIONAL HUMANITARIAN RESPONSE — MUTANT CHILD SAFETY. There was a proposal for emergency coordination, identification, and tracking of displaced mutant minors across national borders. The accompanying photographs showed the dead in Genosha and the survivors waiting for food.

The plan was called the Mercy Initiative.

It had been drafted before the attack.

Charles read that line three times. He checked the metadata, then the old-fashioned date in the file footer. He knew plenty of people who kept a proposal ready for tragedy. There were too many tragedies for that fact alone to prove a conspiracy.

Still, something in the language turned his stomach. Children were called subjects. Family reunification was called disposition. Registration was described as voluntary, then as a prerequisite for the delivery of food, medication, and safe travel. A child who declined the scan would be categorized as unaccounted for.

There was a list of approved security partners. On the list, under a holding company with a pleasant maritime name, sat a corporate logo Charles recognized from old intelligence reports: a red diamond.

He reached for the telephone.

Ororo answered before the second ring.

“I'm in the building, Charles.”

“Then come back. There's something I want you to see.”

She found him examining a photograph of a portable clinic and its volunteers in blue jackets. A doctor stood near the rear of the image, his face half-hidden by the shadow of a canopy. He had dark hair combed neatly back and a smile that suggested he had practiced being reassuring.

“Nathaniel Essex,” Ororo said.

“He has survived three centuries by persuading people that cruelty becomes respectable when it wears a laboratory coat.”

“Do we know it's him?”

“No. We know the face. We know the symbol. We know he profits when frightened people mistake measurement for mercy.”

Ororo read the plan.

“They want the children first.”

“They always do.”

She looked at him. “What does Erik know?”

Charles folded his hands.

“Enough to be dangerous.”

“Charles.”

“He will see this as proof that every warning he has ever given was correct.”

“Perhaps,” Ororo said. “Some of them were.”

The words remained in the room after she left.

Charles had spent his life insisting that cooperation between mutants and humans was possible. He still believed it. He had seen it. He had seen police officers put down their weapons, doctors refuse unlawful orders, neighbors shelter children at terrible cost. The existence of decency was not an argument that institutions were decent. He knew the distinction as a matter of history. He had not always honored it as a matter of strategy.

He was a Black man who had come of age when doors were shut in his face even as strangers insisted the country was getting better. He had earned places in rooms whose members congratulated themselves for allowing him to enter. He understood the performance of progress; sometimes, to his shame, he had mistaken the performance for the thing itself.

His mother's favorite warning returned to him: Never confuse an invitation with a welcome.

He opened the Mercy Initiative again.

The document included a public launch. Two days from now, at noon, governments and private relief organizations would demonstrate the first universal mutant-identification network. The demonstration would take place in Manhattan, at the United Nations, where cameras could be counted on to turn even a bad idea into a ceremony.

Charles called a meeting of the X-Men.

Then he opened the bottom drawer of his desk and took out a telephone he had never used for school business.

The number inside belonged to Erik.

He knew his old friend would not answer.

He called anyway.

## Chapter Three — The Man in the Doorway

New York City, 1982.

Charles had first met Erik Lehnsherr outside a hearing room where a senator was explaining why identifying mutants was a public-health necessity.

They were younger men then. Charles walked with a cane, though on bad days he used his chair. Erik's hair was dark and close-cropped, his shoulders broad, his patience already rationed like a scarce commodity.

The senator had called mutant registration a way to prevent misunderstandings. A mother in the gallery had stood and asked what the government meant by misunderstanding when a child could be put on a list before committing any crime. Security removed her for speaking out of turn.

Erik watched the guards take her by the elbows.

Charles watched the guards' faces. Both men rose at the same instant, for different reasons.

“Stop,” Charles said calmly.

The guards stopped.

For one moment, all four people in the doorway stood motionless. A change had passed across their expressions too quickly for anyone else to notice. Charles felt the trespass as he made it: the small, perfectly deliberate pressure of one mind inside another, changing an intention by force.

The woman pulled away and walked back to her seat.

Erik looked at Charles.

“You made them do that.”

“I prevented an unnecessary escalation.”

“You made them.”

Charles did not like being understood so exactly by a stranger.

“And you,” he said, “were about to tear the doors off their hinges.”

“Wouldn't have had to touch their thoughts.”

They stared at each other. Then, to Charles's astonishment, Erik laughed.

The sound had a little warmth in it and no forgiveness whatsoever.

Later they ate in a diner that had not yet decided whether it welcomed either of them. The waitress brought coffee to Erik and tea to Charles, correctly guessing nothing about their preferences but correctly sensing the danger of asking.

“What do you do?” Charles asked.

“Repair things.”

“Machines?”

“When they're willing.”

“And when they aren't?”

“Still machines.”

Charles smiled. Erik did not, though he seemed to consider it.

“You're a telepath,” Erik said.

“Yes.”

“How many people did you read in there?”

“Fewer than I could have.”

“That wasn't my question.”

“No,” Charles admitted. “It wasn't.”

Erik stirred his coffee with a spoon that had been bent almost in half. Charles had noticed it happen. He had also noticed Erik's effort to conceal the movement.

“You think restraint makes you good,” Erik said.

“I think restraint gives us room to decide what good requires.”

“My mother had restraint.”

Charles's smile vanished.

Erik told him about a county road. He did not give all the details. He gave enough. His voice did not change as he spoke. That was what Charles remembered afterward. There was no theatrical grief to respond to, no invitation to set himself above the pain by comforting its owner.

“I was eleven,” Erik said. “There were men with badges. Men without badges. Didn't make a difference.”

“I'm sorry.”

“I'm not asking you to be.”

“I know.”

“Do you?”

Charles considered the question. He had grown up with means his father used as insulation against a world that could still reach through the walls. He knew segregation from train cars and universities, from the way senior men lowered their voices to say a perfectly ordinary insult. He had once heard his grandmother describe a funeral no one wanted to discuss. He knew that men with badges could become men with ropes. But he had not been on Erik's road.

“No,” he said at last. “Not the way you do.”

It was the first answer Erik appeared to trust.

They talked until the afternoon crowd arrived. About politics, about engineering, about schools, about whether a frightened population could be persuaded to recognize its own children in faces it had been taught to fear. Charles believed that it could. Erik believed that it sometimes would and sometimes would not, and that a child's safety could not depend on which day it happened to be.

When they rose to leave, Erik saw a boy outside the window holding a homemade cardboard sign. The sign said MUTANTS ARE PEOPLE. A man in a suit took the cardboard away and tore it in half. The boy did nothing. His mother put a hand on his shoulder and led him down the street.

Charles felt the scene open like a bruise in Erik's mind. He could have looked deeper. He did not.

“I'm opening a school,” Charles said. “For children like that.”

“A school won't stop the man with the sign.”

“No. But it might teach the boy that the man's opinion doesn't define him.”

Erik studied him for a long time.

“I could build you a better security gate,” he said.

Charles offered his hand.

“I'd appreciate that.”

Erik shook it. His grip was warm, surprisingly gentle.

For a while, that was how they began: a man who could enter any mind choosing not to, and a man who could bend any lock choosing to build one.

Neither yet understood how often they would fail their own principles.

## Chapter Four — The Mercy of Numbers

By morning, someone had written the names of the dead on the church wall.

There were one hundred and twelve names in charcoal before the charcoal ran out. Then the writer used a burned stick. Then somebody brought a marker from a first-aid box, and the list continued across the plaster, around a broken window, and down toward the floor. By noon it was impossible to read from one end without moving your feet.

Erik stood in front of it with Nia beside him.

“Should my dad go up there?” she asked.

“If you want his name there.”

“But you said you don't know.”

“Then we make a place for not knowing.”

He took a piece of chalk from the shelf where hymnals had been kept and wrote, beneath the list, MISSING. He drew a line down the wall. A dozen people stepped forward without being asked. They had names too.

Lorna Dane arrived carrying batteries, bottled water, and an expression Erik knew from her childhood. She was his daughter. She had inherited enough of his magnetic gift to know when he was holding a building together and enough of his temper to resent having to ask.

“You've been awake all night,” she said.

“Your powers have improved, if you can tell that by looking.”

“The church roof is floating three inches above the walls.”

Erik glanced up. He had not noticed.

Lorna gently took some of the strain. The building settled with a long wooden sigh.

“There,” she said. “Now you're free to be impossible about something else.”

He might have smiled on another day.

“Show me the machine.”

She led him through the nave to a table made from two doors laid across a pair of pews. On it lay the shattered Sentinel eye he'd torn from the wreckage. Beside it stood a battered field scanner Lorna had rescued from a laboratory.

“The machine was recording while it attacked,” she said. “Not military targeting in the ordinary sense. It was classifying. Age, height, visible mutation, family group, response to fear. Whatever it couldn't identify, it tried to provoke until it got a usable reading.”

“Who designed it?”

“The same people who call themselves humanitarians every time there's a camera nearby.”

She put the red diamond on the scanner display.

Erik's hand closed so hard that all the nails on the table stood straight up.

“Essex,” he said.

“Possibly. I can't prove the chain.”

“He doesn't need to be here to be responsible.”

“That's a statement for a courtroom. I need something a courtroom can't ignore.”

He looked at his daughter. There was something infuriating about seeing his own conscience come back to him with better discipline.

“What have you found?”

“The Sentinels were collecting records. Most of the living have identifiers stored in the wreckage. There are still transmitters on the island. Somebody's waiting for the rest of us to sign up.”

She turned the scanner toward him. It displayed the word MERCY, written in an elegant, restrained typeface. Under it was a promise: NO CHILD LEFT UNACCOUNTED FOR.

“An account,” Erik murmured. “Of a child.”

He had heard that grammar before. He had heard men debate which door a woman should enter, which school a boy should attend, whose name deserved a mark in a county ledger. The word safety was always nearby. He had learned to distrust any promise that required the promised person to be numbered before they could be protected.

Nia was reading over his elbow.

“Will they find my dad with that?”

Lorna's expression softened. “Maybe. It isn't safe to let them scan you first.”

“Then why do people want it?”

“Because people are frightened,” Erik said. “And because it sounds like help.”

Nia considered this with the seriousness of a child deciding whether an adult was explaining something or making excuses.

“Can we have help without the bracelets?”

“Yes,” Lorna said. “We're going to try.”

A messenger came in from the courtyard. The surviving council members had assembled and wanted Magneto to speak. There were armed patrols at the docks, rumors of another Sentinel group at sea, and a crowd outside demanding evacuation.

Erik went out without his helmet.

The crowd filled what remained of the square. Some wore homemade masks. Others had no energy left to conceal themselves. A man with skin like dark glass held a woman whose left leg was bound in splints. A teenage girl floated several inches above the cobbles and seemed too exhausted to come down.

Erik climbed the steps of the damaged council building. He felt the broken steel under his feet, the way a sailor feels the rocking of a ship.

“They came here to make a list,” he said. No microphone was necessary. Speakers along the square had awakened as he passed. “They killed our neighbors. Our children. Our families. And the machines that did it were taught to remember what they saw.”

The square grew very still.

“We will not let them take that memory away and turn it into a weapon against the survivors. We will find those responsible. We will protect every child we can reach. If they come again, we will meet them.”

Someone shouted, “What if the governments let them?”

Erik heard his mother's front door shake in the wind of a Mississippi afternoon.

“Then the governments will learn,” he said, “that their permission was never the source of our right to live.”

For one breath, it was a speech about protection.

Then a metallic roar came from the harbor. A Sentinel, half-submerged beneath the wreck of a ferry, dragged itself upright. Its head was missing. One arm remained; the other had fused with the ferry's bow. The machine used the surviving arm to point toward the square.

The crowd broke.

Erik stepped forward and caught the machine in the air. Its massive body hung above the harbor like an insect in amber. He compressed it slowly. First the fingers folded. Then the shoulders. Then the torso shrieked inward around whatever intelligence it carried.

The people below cheered when it fell as a small, useless heap of metal.

Erik did not look at them. He looked at the heap.

Inside, a transmitter was still trying to speak.

And somewhere far away, someone was answering.

## Chapter Five — The House That Reads You

The Mercy Initiative held its first press briefing in a conference room where all the plants were artificial.

Ororo noticed the plants before she noticed the security. Their leaves were too uniform, and the soil had no smell. Whoever had arranged the room believed the appearance of life would be sufficient.

She wore a black suit with silver cuffs. At her side walked Kurt Wagner, who had decided that passing as human would be more trouble than the occasion deserved. His blue skin and tail drew looks. He smiled at each person who stared.

A young aide came toward them.

“I'm sorry, sir, but we have a dress code.”

Kurt looked down at his plain white shirt and neatly pressed trousers.

“Ah. Then I have misunderstood the invitation. I thought it was to protect children, not the upholstery.”

Ororo touched his arm. He stopped before he could make the aide cry.

They took seats near the back. Onstage a woman representing an international relief coalition thanked the donors who had made emergency shelters possible. Behind her a screen showed children wrapped in foil blankets. A caption read: THE FUTURE NEEDS SAFETY.

Then Nathaniel Essex stepped into the light.

He wore a pale gray suit and a little pin shaped like a red diamond. He did not look old. The man had lived through dynasties, wars, scientific revolutions, and an astonishing number of self-inflicted deaths, but his face remained smooth and his movements economical.

“Good morning,” he said. “My name is Doctor Nathaniel Essex. I come to you as a physician and as a scientist who has seen too much preventable suffering.”

Ororo heard Kurt breathe out through his nose.

Essex described the registration device as a temporary wellness band. It would identify children during evacuations, help match them with family, and suppress dangerous surges of uncontrolled power during episodes of distress. For frightened parents, it would offer a direct route to licensed assistance.

The words came cleanly, like polished stones placed one after another across a stream.

A reporter asked whether families could refuse.

“Of course,” Essex said. “Participation is entirely voluntary.”

“And if they refuse?”

“Then we cannot guarantee placement in a protected facility. You understand the difficulty. We are accountable for the safety of everyone within our care.”

A few people nodded. They had been given a problem in the form of a choice and were grateful to believe someone else had solved it.

Ororo raised her hand.

Essex saw her immediately. He had probably recognized her from the first moment she entered.

“Miss Munroe,” he said. “We are honored. Would you like to comment on our work?”

“I have a question. How does the band distinguish a child's uncontrolled power from a child's refusal to comply?”

An attentive silence followed.

“The apparatus measures the biological signature of activation,” Essex replied. “It does not judge the child's intentions.”

“Then it cannot know whether that power is dangerous.”

“It can estimate risk.”

“Risk to whom?”

He gave her a small professional smile.

“To everyone, Miss Munroe.”

Kurt leaned forward. “A child who turns blue when frightened has become a risk by being afraid. A child whose body produces fire becomes a risk by having a nightmare. The bracelet does not need to judge their intentions, Doctor. You have judged them already.”

Essex regarded him with the cool interest one might give a specimen that had interrupted a lecture.

“You are asking us to ignore measurable data.”

“No,” Ororo said. “We're asking whom you intend to control with it.”

The moderator intervened. The cameras moved away. The audience was invited to applaud the arrival of a shipment of blankets.

In the corridor afterward, Essex approached them without his entourage.

“Ororo,” he said, as though they had parted amicably at a dinner party. “Your instinct to protect the young does you credit.”

“My instinct to distrust men who call children specimens has also served me well.”

“I have not used that word today.”

“Not in front of the cameras.”

His eyes flickered, not with anger, but with amusement that was close to pleasure.

“What an exhausting world it must be,” he said, “when every structure feels like a prison.”

Kurt stepped forward. “It is often exhausting when one has escaped several prisons.”

Essex glanced at his tail, then returned his attention to Ororo.

“The dead of Genosha have given us a terrible obligation. If my work can prevent another massacre, does it matter that you dislike me?”

“It matters what your work does,” she answered. “We're going to find out.”

He bowed and walked away.

Kurt watched him enter an elevator. “He did not deny collecting samples.”

“No,” Ororo said. “He gave us permission to suspect him. He knows suspicion alone won't stop the demonstration.”

She lifted her phone. Lorna had sent a burst of data from Genosha. A second message waited from an old friend of the school: a hospital administrator in Manhattan whose daughter was a mutant and who had been asked to prepare for a citywide electrical event during the launch.

Ororo felt the weather alter, a pressure change too small for a human nose to catch.

“They're preparing for an interruption,” she said.

Kurt looked at her. “What kind?”

“The kind that makes their devices look necessary.”

She called Charles.

“Find Erik,” she said. “We have less time than the invitation claims.”

## Chapter Six — Two Chairs, One Table

Erik refused to meet Charles at the school.

Instead he chose a maintenance building on the eastern shore of Genosha. Someone had built it as a water-pumping station, and someone else had converted its upper floor into a place where dockworkers could drink tea during a night shift. The roof was gone. The sea moved visibly through the broken walls. It was impossible to pretend the location was neutral.

Charles arrived in an X-Men aircraft piloted by Ororo. She offered to come inside. He declined. He had asked Erik for a conversation between old friends; he did not wish to turn up with a delegation.

Erik was waiting at a table. There were two chairs.

“You've chosen a pleasant place,” Charles said.

“The view is honest.”

Charles wheeled to the opposite side of the table. Erik moved the chair out of the way without touching it.

“I understand you've met Nathaniel Essex's latest charitable enterprise,” Erik said.

“He's using the massacre to justify a program that was prepared before the massacre.”

“And you came to tell me the people behind it are basically reasonable?”

“No. I came because I'm afraid you're preparing to destroy the wrong thing.”

Erik's mouth tightened.

“That would be new. Usually you begin by telling me what I feel.”

“I've learned not to.”

“After a remarkably long apprenticeship.”

The old warmth between them existed only as an outline, like a photograph faded until the bodies were almost transparent. It was not gone. That, perhaps, made the conversation more dangerous.

Charles set an unsealed folder on the table. Inside were photographs of Mercy bands, a schedule, and a diagram of the network through which they would communicate. He explained the findings carefully: the bands could be used for rescue. They could also mark a target, limit a child's powers, and transmit information without the child's knowledge. When the public demonstration began, a second system embedded in the same network would be activated.

“Where?” Erik asked.

“Manhattan. The equipment is distributed. The central relay is beneath the river, connected to the old industrial transmission lines.”

“Then I remove the relay.”

“If you do it with the force you used yesterday, you'll destroy the power feeding several hospitals and an underground rail line.”

“I can separate the circuits.”

“Perhaps. But Essex has deliberately tied their control signals together. We do not yet know which cables are carrying what.”

“Since when did not knowing become an argument for doing nothing?”

“It never was.”

“Then why do you keep bringing it to me?”

Charles stared at him. He had an answer ready, a practiced sentence about the need to protect innocent lives. He saw how efficiently Erik would dismantle it. He put it away.

“Because the children in those hospitals matter too,” he said.

Erik looked toward the sea.

“Do you think I don't know that?”

“I know that you do. I also know what happened in the square yesterday.”

“You've been reading my mind?”

“No. There were witnesses.”

“A dead machine attacked a crowd. I stopped it.”

“You did. Then you spoke to the survivors about teaching the governments a lesson. They heard a promise that someone would finally make the world pay. Essex wants them to hear that. He wants everyone to hear it.”

Erik pushed the folder back across the table.

“Essex is not responsible for what I say.”

“No. You are.”

For a moment the small screws along the table's edge began to turn.

Charles did not retreat.

“You think I don't remember the hearings,” Erik said. “All the polite men explaining why the lists were necessary. All the lawyers saying the law would protect us when it finished studying the problem. They used to ask my mother to trust the sheriff who'd come to her house with a warning.”

“I remember what you told me.”

“No. You remember a story I told. I remember her hand on my shoulder.”

Charles took the words without defending himself. He knew Erik had never wanted to be told he was understood. He had wanted someone to act with the knowledge that he was not.

“When I was a boy,” Charles said, “my mother taught me to watch the people who invited us into rooms. She knew they might mean well and still refuse us when it mattered. My father thought money would protect us from the worst of it. He bought doors with locks and taught me to stand near the front. I spent years believing that if I could get into the right room, I could change the rules.”

“You got into a great many rooms.”

“Yes.”

“And?”

“Some people are alive because of what changed there.”

Erik considered that.

“Some are dead because of what didn't.”

“Yes,” Charles said.

The admission surprised Erik. He had been prepared to argue with an older version of his friend, a man who explained suffering in the hopeful grammar of institutions. The man opposite him was exhausted and less certain of his own goodness. He seemed no less determined.

“Do you remember the first time we met?” Charles asked.

“Two men stopped a woman from being arrested. One by stealing two minds. One by nearly taking the courthouse apart.”

“I was wrong to enter those guards' thoughts without permission.”

“They were hurting her.”

“That does not erase what I did.”

Erik's eyes narrowed. “Are you asking me to apologize for the doors?”

“No. I'm asking whether we can save more people if neither of us pretends our power makes us infallible.”

Outside, the waves struck a submerged wall and threw white water against the sunken dock.

A small voice came from the stairwell.

“Are you two done fighting?”

Nia stood in the doorway with a tin cup. Behind her Lorna looked distinctly unapologetic.

“I said you were talking,” Lorna told Erik. “She assumed the rest.”

Nia approached the table. “I made tea. Well, I carried tea. Someone else made it.”

Charles accepted a cup with both hands.

“Thank you.”

Nia looked at Erik. “Can he help find my dad?”

Charles met her eyes.

“I can help look,” he said. “I won't go into anyone's thoughts without asking. But there are other ways. Your name, the places you went, the people who saw you.”

She nodded, measuring him against a standard neither man could see.

Then she turned to Erik.

“People keep asking what you want to do to the bad men,” she said. “I don't want to be the reason you hurt somebody.”

Erik went still.

Nia looked immediately worried that she had said something forbidden. He lowered himself until his eyes were level with hers.

“Why would you think you were?”

“Because you tell everybody you have to make them afraid for us.”

He could not say she had misunderstood. Children often heard the shape of a statement more clearly than the adults who made it.

“I want you safe,” he said.

“Then ask me what safe is.”

He looked at her. He had rescued her from a church full of broken glass and frightened her all over again with the size of his promises.

“All right, Nia,” he said. “What would safe be?”

She thought about it.

“A room where I know who can come in. And where I can go out if I want.”

Charles turned his cup slowly in his hands.

“A reasonable beginning,” he said.

Erik looked at the ruined doorway. He could put a hundred tons of steel across it with a thought. He could not give the girl the feeling she was asking for by doing so.

“Then we'll build that room,” he said.

The answer was smaller than a declaration of war. For the first time that day, it felt possible to keep.
