## Chapter Seven — What Mercy Measures

The first Mercy band was opened in the basement of a church in Queens.

It looked harmless on the table: a thin silver strip lined with soft blue material, small enough for a child's wrist. A friendly green light glowed beneath a symbol of two hands reaching toward each other. Beside it lay a box of granola bars, a packet of tissues, and a leaflet explaining that no family would be forgotten.

Rogue studied it with her arms folded. Her hair fell over the collar of a leather jacket she had bought when she still believed a good jacket could keep the world from getting close.

“Whoever designed that thing never had to explain to a scared kid why a grown man needed their wrist,” she said.

Lorna moved a magnifier over the band. A filament answered her attention by lifting from the strip like a silver eyelash.

“It's not just a tracker,” she said. “It records the changes in a mutant's powers when their body is stressed. It can signal the bands around it, so the network learns from every response. It keeps learning even after the band is removed.”

“Because they've already taken the reading,” Rogue said.

“Because the child is the reading.”

On the wall, Kurt had taped photographs of the Mercy Initiative's storage sites. There were distribution centers in five boroughs, temporary shelters near the airports, a central demonstration platform at the United Nations, and a buried relay under the East River. None of the sites was officially a detention center. All had security personnel trained to restrict movement during an emergency.

Ororo studied the map.

“Essex has done this before. Call a boundary a shelter. Call a restraint a precaution. Offer an escape route and count the people who take it.”

Rogue picked up one of the leaflets. “And the food comes with a bracelet. That's the generous part.”

“There's worse,” Lorna said.

She connected the band to a small instrument. Its green light changed to amber. A diagram appeared on the wall: a moving line that branched into hundreds of points, each a different biological pattern. Some pulsed with a familiar electromagnetic signature.

“These came from Genosha,” Lorna said. “The dead Sentinels were transmitting while they killed. They're matching samples from the massacre with new scans. It's an identification model trained on the people who couldn't escape.”

For a moment nobody spoke.

Rogue looked down at the little silver band. “Lord have mercy.”

“That's what they named it,” Kurt said quietly.

A phone rang. It was Doctor Lena Ward, director of emergency operations at an East Side hospital. Her daughter had begun changing the color of her hands when she was frightened, and she had turned away the first Mercy recruiter who came to the hospital's pediatric floor. The recruiter had returned with a supervisor and a suggestion that the hospital might lose grant money if it refused cooperation.

“You're going to want to hear this,” she told Ororo. “We've been ordered to prepare the intensive care units for a ninety-second interruption to the municipal grid tomorrow at noon. The notice says it's a test of emergency resilience.”

“Can you isolate your backup systems?” Ororo asked.

“I can. I can't answer for the other hospitals. And our generator in the old wing is waiting for a part. If the transition fails, there's a baby on a ventilator who will not have ninety seconds.”

Ororo looked at Lorna.

“Can the relay be disabled without touching those circuits?”

“Possibly. It's been interleaved with the civic grid on purpose. They're making any attack on the transmitter look like an attack on the city.”

“Then our first job isn't a raid,” Ororo said. “It's a phone call.”

For the next hour they made calls to hospitals, electricians, transit workers, and people who knew the city's old tunnels better than its planners did. Many refused to discuss infrastructure with strangers. Some hung up. A few listened when Doctor Ward identified herself and explained what she had been asked to do.

One of those listeners was a transit supervisor named Gabriel Ortiz. He had two sons, neither of them a mutant. He knew the East River lines because he'd spent twenty years fixing equipment in rooms the public never saw.

“The utility annex under the demonstration platform?” he asked. “That's not supposed to be live yet.”

“It is,” Lorna said.

“Then someone's bypassed every permit in the district.”

“Can you help us get into it?”

“I can tell you where the doors are,” Ortiz said. “I won't let you turn off a tunnel full of passengers without warning them.”

“Good,” Ororo replied. “We don't intend to.”

Ortiz considered that.

“Tomorrow,” he said. “Meet me at the service gate. Bring the hospital lady.”

A plan began to exist. Not because two men with extraordinary abilities had decided what should happen, but because people with ordinary responsibilities were willing to tell the truth about what their jobs required.

That evening, Charles came down the stairs to find Lorna still studying the circuitry. Her hands trembled from fatigue. She had been carrying other people's power in the form of machines for two days.

“You need rest,” he said.

“That's a sentence nobody in this family has ever obeyed.”

“Then allow me to be the first to learn from failure.”

She almost smiled.

Charles looked at the band. “How does it decide when a power has become dangerous?”

Lorna tapped a sequence on the monitor. The answer appeared: ESCALATION PREDICTION.

“That's the genius of it,” she said bitterly. “It doesn't wait for a crime. It predicts fear. If a child is likely to panic, the band treats panic as aggression. The machine can say it acted before anyone was harmed.”

Charles stared at the screen. He had spent his life hearing thoughts people had never wanted to make into actions. He knew how many cruel impulses passed through a frightened person's mind without ever becoming a choice. A world that punished the prediction of a thought could condemn almost anyone.

“There is no consent in this,” he said.

“No,” Lorna replied. “There is only compliance dressed as rescue.”

A notification appeared on the instrument. The Mercy bands had begun receiving a new instruction. Not tomorrow. Tonight. The network was warming itself, preparing to recognize every registered wearer as a point on a map.

Charles felt a chill that had nothing to do with the room.

“Call Ororo,” he said. “We cannot wait for noon to begin.”

## Chapter Eight — The Man on the Throne

Genosha held a memorial that night. Hundreds of candles stood in the broken windows of the church. Others floated down the harbor in small paper boats. Someone had hung cloth over the exposed beams to give the survivors a roof they could pretend was whole.

Erik wanted no part of the ceremony. He attended anyway.

People touched his arm as he passed. Some thanked him. Some asked for news of missing relatives. A man in a torn suit demanded that he execute the officials who had authorized the attack. A woman with three children asked for a way off the island.

Erik could answer the second request. He arranged a transport. The first remained with him as he walked.

Lorna had warned him that the Sentinels were using the survivors' movements to populate the Mercy network. He had sent the information to every mutant leader he trusted. He had asked his people to stop using the public scanners, to remove the bands, to mark the missing by hand. These actions were sensible. They were also slow.

At the church entrance a boy in a suit too large for him asked Erik whether he would promise that Genosha would never be attacked again.

“No,” Erik said.

The boy stared at him.

“I will promise we will fight to stop it,” he continued. “I cannot promise what the attackers will choose.”

The boy looked disappointed. Erik understood. In a crisis, a promise with boundaries sounded like cowardice beside one that claimed the whole world.

A reporter had found a working camera. She raised it when Erik stepped into the nave. Without thinking, he straightened his shoulders and let the metal debris outside move into a shield around the church. The room felt safer. The crowd saw a protector who had decided to be visible.

Then a scream erupted in the square.

One of the disabled Sentinels had come alive again. It rose from the rubble and projected a recording across the church wall: mutant children running from flames; a machine's targeting reticle locking onto their faces. Across the image appeared the words UNREGISTERED CHILDREN AT RISK.

The projection repeated. Each time, the children's faces became a little clearer. The crowd shouted for Erik to stop it. He could have destroyed the machine. Instead he reached into its circuits and pulled out its transmitting core, suspending the small red diamond above his palm.

A signal came through the core. The voice was human, composed, and almost kind.

“Magnus,” Nathaniel Essex said. “Would you prefer a world that knew where its children were?”

Erik's heartbeat slowed. His anger had passed beyond heat into something more exact.

“You're alive,” he said.

“I have always been difficult to eliminate. I hope that is not our primary disagreement.”

“Did you send those machines?”

Essex paused, enjoying the question.

“The systems performed according to their instruction. Whether the resulting tragedy was necessary is a question for those who demanded certainty.”

“Answer me.”

“You have seen what happens when a population is unaccounted for. Help me protect the children who remain.”

Erik crushed the transmitter. The recording vanished. In the silence, the crowd watched him.

He could feel their fear moving through the room. They wanted action, and they wanted him to be the man who did not hesitate. He wanted to be that man too. It was easier than admitting that a single perfect act could not put a mother back in her kitchen or a father back beside a child.

He lifted the wreckage from the square and drew it into a massive structure outside the church. Broken Sentinel limbs locked into a raised platform. Plates of armor curved into a throne. The red and black metal gleamed under the emergency lights.

The people moved back to give it room.

Erik climbed the steps. His helmet settled on his head. For a moment he was aware of the theatricality of it—the great chair, the rising scrap, the camera. He did not stop. The theater had a purpose. If the world could be taught fear through images, perhaps it could be taught fear of him.

“They sent their machines,” he said, and his voice carried across the ruined streets. “They sent their lies and their laws and their promises of a cure. They brought us into the open to count us. And when they had counted enough, they came to kill.”

In front of the church, a thousand faces lifted.

“I will not kneel,” he said. “I will not beg. I will not ask permission to keep my people alive.”

Some cheered. Others wept. A woman raised her sleeping child so the camera could see him.

Erik saw the camera, the enormous throne, the broken machines made into a crown of warning. He heard himself becoming a symbol again.

“You pray to your gods?” he said. “I'm your new one.”

The words landed like an explosion.

A sound rose from the crowd, part triumph and part terror. Erik felt, with a bleak clarity, that he could keep speaking and never again have to hear an objection. The man on the throne could demand obedience. The man holding a child in a church had asked whether he might take her hand.

He was still deciding which man he wanted to be when he saw Nia at the edge of the square.

She had come with a blanket around her shoulders. The camera passed over her and returned to the throne. She was not cheering.

Erik's eyes met hers. She looked away.

A warning rang in his helmet, routed from Lorna's scanner. MERCY NETWORK INITIALIZATION ADVANCED.

He understood then what Essex had arranged: the image of a mutant king threatening humanity would be played alongside a request for a new system of surveillance. The two images would speak to each other. Fear would do the rest.

The world had been offered a villain and a cure in the same hour.

Erik stepped down from the throne.

The crowd seemed disappointed by the ordinary movement of his feet.

He went toward Nia. She did not run. She did not reach for him either.

“That was a big chair,” she said.

“Yes.”

“You scared everybody.”

“I meant to scare the people who hurt you.”

“You scared me too.”

He stood in the cold night with the words between them.

“I know,” he said. “I'm sorry.”

It was a real apology, which meant it could not be followed by a reason she was obliged to accept.

Nia shifted the blanket around her shoulders. “Are you still going to help?”

“Yes.”

“Good.”

She walked past him into the church.

Erik looked back at the metal throne. It would have been so simple to remain there. A throne was a chair made to prevent the occupant from hearing no.

He tore it apart. The metal fell to the ground in orderly stacks, ready to be reused for shelter walls and bridge repairs.

Then he took off his helmet and called Charles.

## Chapter Nine — No Door Without a Handle

The emergency meeting took place in the school library. It was a room designed for young people who argued about books and ate crackers where they weren't supposed to. The maps of Manhattan made it look like a war room. The half-empty mug of chocolate beside Kurt's chair kept it honest.

Erik arrived after midnight. He entered through a side door, without an escort or his helmet. Several students in the hall stopped to look at him. He nodded once. One little boy waved. Erik waved back before he realized what he was doing.

Charles sat at the head of the table. Ororo occupied the chair opposite. Lorna, Rogue, Kurt, and Doctor Ward were assembled around a diagram of the East River utility network. Gabriel Ortiz had joined by secure video. A municipal engineer and two hospital technicians listened from another screen.

“Are we late?” Erik asked.

“We've been waiting for the man who can move the machinery,” Ororo said. “The rest of us have been finding out what the machinery is connected to.”

Erik took an empty chair.

Lorna showed him the relay. The old utility annex beneath Manhattan carried power and communications through a corridor of steel pipes. Essex had installed a field generator inside the annex and threaded its control circuit through equipment that also monitored emergency backup systems. The generator could send suppression commands to every Mercy band in range and call dormant Sentinel parts to life. If it operated for long enough, more units would answer from farther away.

“The relays are hidden inside the same housings as the safety circuits,” Lorna explained. “If we tear everything out at once, the utility grid may trip and the backups may not switch cleanly. We need enough time to isolate four hospitals and two subway lines.”

“How much time?” Erik asked.

“Seventy seconds once the first signal begins,” Ortiz said from the screen. “Maybe less. People are moving in those tunnels. I'm not killing a train full of commuters so we can make a point.”

“Neither are we,” Erik said.

The engineer looked at him without much affection. Erik welcomed it. Suspicion based on what he might do was at least a demand for accountability, which was more than a worshipful crowd could offer.

Doctor Ward folded her arms.

“My hospital can switch. The other three are not ready. One has a ventilator unit running on a damaged backup panel. Another has a newborn care floor with a power feed I don't trust. I'm not authorizing a shutdown unless we know those patients will stay breathing.”

Ororo turned toward her.

“We won't ask you to.”

“We may have to do something at the relay before the hospitals are ready,” Lorna said.

“Then you find another way,” Ward replied. Her voice was shaking. She did not apologize for it. “People keep saying there isn't time, and that they have to make hard choices. Fine. But one of those choices is going to have my signature under it. I'm telling you what I will not sign.”

Erik looked at Charles. His friend was watching Ward with the attention he usually gave a student who had just found the courage to say something difficult.

“She's right,” Charles said.

“You're both right,” Erik replied. “Which is not yet a plan.”

Kurt leaned over the map.

“I can reach the annex before any of you. I cannot carry the whole machine out. But I can get inside and tell Lorna which circuits are where.”

“Rogue and I can handle the physical units if they activate,” Ororo said. “Lorna, can you separate the relay from the civilian feeds?”

“I can do it when I know exactly where the connections are. I cannot do it from a satellite picture.”

Ortiz interrupted. “I know somebody in the river maintenance department. She's been repairing those lines since before the companies took over. If she gets us a survey, you'll have a real map.”

“Call her,” Ororo said.

The engineer opened another screen and began marking the emergency circuits. The meeting stopped feeling like a confrontation between gifted people and started feeling like an actual operation. Every person had a job that could be checked. Every proposed action had someone assigned to stop it if the necessary conditions weren't met.

Charles waited until the room was busy before speaking quietly to Erik.

“I saw your speech.”

“I know.”

“You frightened a great many people.”

“I frightened Nia.”

Charles looked at him.

“She told me,” Erik said. “That was the part I hadn't expected.”

“She trusted you enough to tell you.”

“Doesn't make the words less true.”

“No,” Charles said. “It doesn't.”

Erik watched his daughter arguing amiably with the engineer over a cable. He had imagined that his legacy would be the fear he inspired in enemies. It was more humbling to see Lorna use his gift to keep a hospital alive.

“Essex put me in his story,” Erik said. “I walked right into it.”

“You have a habit of doing that when someone offers you a throne.”

Erik gave him a look.

“That's the old Charles,” he said. “Insufferable and accurate.”

“You invited me to be accurate.”

“I did not.”

“No,” Charles admitted. “You didn't.”

They almost laughed. It was a dangerous relief.

Charles leaned closer.

“There's one part I haven't told the room,” he said. “I can coordinate the people in the hospitals and the tunnels through Cerebro. But only if they permit it. I won't put orders into their minds. I'll open a channel and let each person decide whether to accept it.”

“That takes time.”

“It takes trust.”

“And if they won't trust you?”

“Then they can use the radios.”

Erik looked around the table. The radios would be slower. They might not be enough. Charles knew it and was offering the choice anyway.

“Someone will die because you waited,” Erik said, not as accusation but as a truth they both needed to face.

“Someone might,” Charles replied. “Someone will also die if I start believing my intentions entitle me to make choices inside other people's minds.”

They regarded each other. The difference between them had not vanished. It had become sharper, and therefore more useful to name.

At three in the morning, the hospital technicians confirmed their first successful backup test. Ortiz reported that the river maintenance supervisor had located a service shaft beneath the demonstration platform. Rogue found a way to get a relief convoy near the building without triggering security. Kurt identified a sealed access door he could teleport through if he had a clear view of the space beyond.

At four, Ororo stepped outside and looked at the stars. She could feel a storm gathering off the coast. She could bring it to the city if she needed it. She could not promise it would hurt only the people she wanted to stop.

Erik joined her on the steps.

“You once told me you didn't want to be a goddess,” he said.

“I still don't.”

“Then why do people keep calling you one?”

“Because it's easier than asking whether I need help.”

He stood beside her in the cold.

“Do you?”

“Yes,” she said. “I do.”

He waited.

“I need you to be there when the relay activates,” Ororo said. “And I need you to stop when we say stop, even if you believe you could finish the job alone.”

He thought of the little girl asking for a room with a door.

“I'll try,” he said.

“Erik.”

He met her eyes.

“I will stop,” he said.

For Ororo, that was enough to begin.

## Chapter Ten — Every Light in Manhattan

At eleven forty-two the next morning, a choir of schoolchildren began singing in front of the United Nations, and the Mercy Initiative turned on its machines twelve minutes early.

The children sang beneath a banner that promised to find every missing mutant child. A hundred reporters watched from behind metal barriers. In the front row sat officials who had flown in to demonstrate their concern. A woman introduced herself as the director of international family reunification and spoke about the solemn responsibility of hope.

Then the silver bands worn by twenty children in a staged demonstration lit up red.

The first child to notice was a boy whose fingers had always glowed when he was excited. He looked down at the band. His fingers turned dark. He tried to move them and could not. The presenter smiled and announced that the system had identified an unsafe power event and prevented injury.

The boy screamed.

Above the river, a crane folded in half.

Its steel cables detached from their pulleys and braided themselves into a many-limbed shape. A row of construction panels rose from the water. They locked together around a single red eye. The thing stood taller than the demonstration platform, an improvised Sentinel assembled from every piece of metal Essex had left ready for the call.

The cameras turned toward it.

Three other shapes answered from buildings along the river.

On the street, people began to run. Some ran from the machines. Others ran from the children wearing the bands. A woman tried to pull her son toward a bus, but the band on his wrist tightened, immobilizing the arm he needed to hold her hand.

Rogue was among the security volunteers nearest the platform. She had entered with a false badge and a real emergency plan. She abandoned the badge at once, caught the boy beneath his shoulders, and lifted him clear of the crowd.

“Look at me, sweetheart,” she said. “That bracelet's lying to you. You haven't done a damn thing wrong.”

He was too frightened to answer. She carried him toward the open street.

High above them, Ororo felt the pressure shift as the machines awoke. Their bodies dragged electrical fields through the air, forcing the clouds into shapes no storm had chosen. She pulled rain from the harbor, a curtain heavy enough to blind their optical sensors for several seconds. It would not stop a Sentinel. It would make it hesitate.

“Lorna,” she said into the radio, “we're early.”

“I can see that.”

“Status?”

“Two hospitals ready. One nearly. Doctor Ward says her old wing is still on the damaged panel.”

“Can you hold?”

“I can hold my end. I don't know if Erik can hold the whole city.”

Erik stood on the roof of a warehouse overlooking the East River. He had arrived before the ceremony and spent the morning identifying the metal mass of every machine in the surrounding blocks. The first three Sentinels hung in the air, prevented from moving by his will. The fourth broke from a construction site inland and began moving toward the nearest cluster of bands.

He caught it too.

His shoulders burned. He could manipulate a bridge or a building more easily than a hundred thousand separate objects moving according to a machine's instructions. The Sentinels' components resisted him with fields that reversed direction every fraction of a second. Lorna had warned him the system was designed to anticipate magnetic intervention.

“Charles,” he said into his radio, “the machine has learned my hand.”

“Yes,” Charles answered. “And you can learn its limits. Hold what you can. We are moving the patients.”

“I can end this now.”

“I know.”

A long silence followed. Erik could feel the buried relay waiting. A single massive pulse would silence the Sentinel command network. It would also strike the backup circuits that had not been isolated, throwing hospitals and tunnels into darkness.

“Tell me when,” he said.

“Not yet.”

Erik set his teeth and held.

Below him, a human police officer stood in a crossroads directing pedestrians away from a collapsing scaffold. He had drawn his weapon and was pointing it at a terrified teenager whose skin sparked in the rain. The officer shouted something Erik could not hear. The teenager raised both hands. A Sentinel turned toward the exchange.

Erik could release the officer's weapon and let the machine decide what happened. Part of him wanted to. The old road in Mississippi opened in his mind, headlights cutting through dust, men confident that violence belonged to them.

Instead, he folded the pistol into a harmless knot, shoved the officer and the boy behind a wall of steel, and caught the Sentinel's head in mid-swing. The machine struck his barrier hard enough to crack the pavement.

The officer stared at the twisted weapon in his hands.

The teenager stared at Erik in the sky.

Neither knew what to say.

“Run,” Erik told them both.

They did.

Beneath the river, Kurt appeared inside the utility annex in a cloud of sulfurous smoke. The chamber was waist-deep in water and full of conduits humming with the wrong kind of life. He held a radio in one hand and a flashlight in the other. The first thing he saw was a sign reading AUTHORIZED PERSONNEL ONLY. He found that reassuring. It meant the room had at least once been intended for ordinary people.

“Lorna,” he said, “I am inside. There are six distribution cabinets and three central housings. The middle one is new. Very new.”

“Do not touch it.”

“I had planned to admire it from a distance.”

A red light came on over the door.

Across the room, metallic arms unfolded from the housing. They reached for him like insects discovering a prayer.

“Lorna,” Kurt said, “the room has changed its mind about my presence.”

He vanished before the first arm struck, reappearing behind a bank of switches. He read the identifiers into the radio while the metal crashed against the wall where he'd been standing. Every time he moved, he took with him a breath of clean air and left behind another small cloud of smoke. A mechanical arm caught the edge of his coat and tore it away.

“Rogue,” he said calmly, “I am going to require the assistance of somebody substantially more annoyed than I am.”

“On my way, sugar.”

Aboveground, the Mercy bands began registering every fleeing mutant child as a threat event. The more frightened the children became, the stronger the signal, and the more violent the Sentinels' movements grew. Essex's system was creating the danger it claimed to prevent.

At the hospital, Doctor Ward watched the green light on her backup panel flicker to orange.

Her daughter, Ivy, was on the floor below with a neighbor. Ward had refused to let anyone put a band on her. Ivy's hands changed color when she was frightened; lately they had begun turning the color of the room around her. She liked that about herself. Her mother was determined she would keep liking it.

“Old wing generator still unstable,” a technician said.

“We'll move the patient manually,” Ward replied.

“We don't have time.”

“Then we make time.”

The technician looked toward the monitor where the patient was breathing in measured, mechanical intervals.

Ward picked up the emergency transfer board and handed it to him.

“Tell me what you need.”

At that moment Charles's voice sounded in her ear, through a device she had authorized and tested an hour earlier.

*Doctor Ward. I'm here. I will stay connected only as long as you want. Kurt has mapped the central relay. We need the last hospital circuit isolated before Erik can stop the machines.*

“Understood,” she said aloud. “We're not ready.”

*Then we wait.*

Outside, an improvised Sentinel struck a bridge support. Steel twisted above the river. Erik held the falling section in place, feeling the weight of hundreds of people in the traffic trapped upon it. He could move the metal; he could not order every driver to be calm.

A motorcycle rider crawled beneath a truck and began pulling a child through a broken window. Someone else climbed down to help. Below them, an ambulance had stopped to load the injured even as the sidewalk cracked. People shouted directions, sometimes to strangers they'd been afraid of minutes before.

Erik felt those actions as movements against the steel he was holding. Each one mattered. None was simple.

“Charles,” he said, his voice hoarse, “tell your people I can give them ten minutes. After that the field collapses.”

*They know. They are not wasting it.*

The words did not make him less afraid.

They made him hold for another second.

## Chapter Eleven — The Weight of a Hand

At eleven fifty-one, Nathaniel Essex walked into the flooded annex carrying a black case.

He had arranged his appearance with exquisite timing. Kurt had just finished identifying the six main isolation points. Rogue had forced her way into the lower chamber and was holding a mechanical arm against a wall with one gloved hand while she tore its power feed free with the other. Lorna was listening on the radio, tracing each line through the city's hidden steel.

Essex stepped over a severed conduit as though entering an operating theater.

“Ah,” he said. “Our rescuers. You have damaged a beautiful system.”

Kurt perched on a support beam above the water. “It was a system designed to attack children. I confess that diminishes my appreciation of its beauty.”

“It was designed to remove uncertainty.”

“So is a tomb,” Rogue said.

Essex opened the case. Inside lay a compact drive and a transmitter no larger than a pocket watch. A red diamond flickered on its surface.

“The field is still collecting,” he said. “Every response, every act of resistance, every rescue. You are making the model better. You see, the crisis itself gives us the data that ordinary consent could never provide.”

Ororo's voice came from the radio. “Rogue, get the case. The relay is still linking bands to the Sentinels.”

Rogue released the mechanical arm and lunged. Essex pressed the transmitter.

The chamber's metal walls moved inward. Kurt teleported from the beam to the floor and seized Rogue before the crushing panels could meet. They reappeared behind Essex, but the man had already stepped toward a service exit. He moved without haste. He believed his machines would finish the work.

Lorna's voice crackled through the radio.

“Kurt, the third housing. The conduit at the back. That's where the command signal leaves the room.”

“I see it,” Kurt replied.

He reached for the switch. The panel discharged a blue pulse that threw him against the wall. He landed hard in the rising water, struggling for breath.

Rogue turned on Essex with a sound that was almost a growl.

“Touch him again,” she said, “and we'll see what kind of machine you are when I'm done with you.”

Essex looked fascinated. “Your power would make the most extraordinary research subject.”

Rogue smiled without warmth. “Try me.”

Above the river, Erik felt Kurt's pain as a sudden failure of the plan. Through the radio he heard Rogue's threat and the scrape of metal in water. He had reached the edge of what he could hold. The Sentinels hung in the air on fields that were beginning to shake apart.

“Lorna,” he said, “what do you need?”

“Another thirty seconds. Doctor Ward's team has the patient moving. The power line is still connected.”

Erik closed his eyes. The bridge threatened to buckle. He moved part of his attention from the Sentinels to the smaller pieces of metal around the people on it: seat-belt anchors, door frames, guardrails. He could support them individually if he could keep track of each point.

For one moment he was back on the county road, his hands pinned to dirt, bolts shaking loose in a truck. He remembered the intoxicating certainty that power could make the world answer him.

It had not saved his mother.

Power was a tool, not a verdict. He knew that now with a knowledge no philosophy had given him. His daughter was working beneath the river, his old friend connecting people who had chosen to be connected, and ordinary men and women were carrying patients because their own hands were enough for part of the job. The world had never depended on one man alone. That did not make his part smaller. It made it possible to bear.

“Take your time,” he told Lorna.

She heard the strain in his voice.

“Dad—”

“Do the work.”

At the hospital, Ward's technicians moved the patient into the functioning unit. A nurse sealed the portable ventilation connection and watched the chest rise twice, then a third time. The monitor remained green.

“Ready,” Ward told Charles.

In Charles's mind the network of consenting voices had become a map of human effort. He could hear Doctor Ward, the transit supervisor, the maintenance engineer, and each of the team members who had agreed to let him join their thoughts for the duration of the emergency. None belonged to him. Each could disconnect. They were not soldiers under command. They were people comparing what they knew.

*Hospital four ready,* he told Lorna. *Transit tunnels clear. All civilian feeds isolated.*

“Not all,” Ortiz said. “There's a crew in the inspection shaft near the water intake. They missed the evacuation signal.”

Erik saw the shaft in his mind as a cluster of metal shapes. Three workers were trapped behind a gate being warped by pressure. The Sentinel above had begun pulling on the service bridge. The gate was moving toward them.

“I'll hold the shaft,” Erik said.

“You're holding too much,” Charles replied.

“I know.”

“Erik—”

“I know.”

He shifted the field and felt his left shoulder give. Pain struck down his arm. The bridge sagged. He could save the workers by releasing the last Sentinel. The machine would immediately turn toward the demonstration crowd.

For years he had imagined moral choice as a question of strength: what he was willing to do to prevent a worse catastrophe. The choices before him were smaller and more terrible. He could not protect every person with one gesture. He had to ask for help and trust that someone would answer.

“Ororo,” he whispered.

“I am here.”

“Take the last one.”

The sky above Manhattan blackened. Ororo drew a column of cold air down the river and wrapped it around the Sentinel's remaining arm. Lightning struck the machine's exposed control unit. Its field flickered. She gave a second strike to the river itself, forcing a sheet of steam between the machine and the fleeing crowd.

The Sentinel fell sideways into the water. It did not rise.

“I have it,” Ororo said.

Erik released enough force to free the shaft gate. The three workers crawled out into daylight, coughing, alive.

Then Lorna spoke.

“All ready. Dad, now.”

Erik descended toward the river. Inside the annex, he could feel the relay's components as a pattern of pulsing steel, each one placed exactly where its designer had believed no opposing field could reach without destroying something necessary. Lorna had mapped the separation. Kurt had identified the housing. The hospitals had moved their patients. The transit workers had stopped the trains. The people who would have been hurt had not been treated as an acceptable price.

Erik turned his hand.

The command field collapsed inside the isolated relay. The water around the housing rose in a twisting ring. The metallic arms froze. The silver bands on children's wrists went dark and fell open, their clasps broken by a pressure too precise to bruise skin. The Sentinel fragments above the river lost their coordination and crashed into empty construction lots, where Ororo had cleared the streets.

A sound passed across the city: the low, exhausted groan of a machine being refused.

The Mercy network was silent.

Essex, still in the flooded annex, looked at the dead transmitter in his hand. For the first time his expression was not amused.

Kurt appeared behind him and wrapped a length of steel cable around his wrists. Rogue took the black case. The doctor offered no struggle. He seemed to be calculating whether escape would require the loss of another body, and whether this body was worth the inconvenience.

Then the ceiling opened.

Erik lowered himself through a circle of suspended metal, his cape wet with rain, his face gray with exhaustion. He landed a few feet from Essex and stared at him.

The two men had met before, decades earlier, in a laboratory where the dead were discussed as opportunities. Essex remembered. He always remembered data. He rarely remembered people.

“Magnus,” he said. “You survived.”

“Many didn't.”

“An unfortunate cost. But surely you understand. The world will not stop fearing mutants because you destroy one device.”

“No.”

“Then why not embrace the fear? Make yourself the sovereign of it. I gave you a stage, and you took it beautifully.”

Erik reached for the metal around Essex's body. The cuffs tightened. He could crush the man inside his own suit. Every life lost at Genosha seemed to gather around that possibility. He remembered his mother's death certificate, the men who'd signed it, and how easily they had continued their lives. He remembered the black and red throne, the crowd waiting for him to become something larger than a person.

He had killed before. He was not afraid of blood. He was afraid of how natural this moment felt.

“Erik,” Charles said through the radio.

Erik did not answer.

He looked at Essex. The scientist tried another smile.

“Are you going to make me an example?”

Erik considered the question.

“Yes,” he said.

The cuffs stopped tightening.

“You're going to live long enough to hear the names.”

Essex blinked.

“The names of whom?”

“All of them.”

Rogue held up the black case. The drive inside carried the original records: contract signatures, distribution orders, research logs, and every false consent form used to turn a child's fear into a weapon. The public had seen the machines attack, and now there would be documents with names beside the decisions.

Essex stared at the case. He understood at last why the man had not killed him. A dead scientist could become a martyr or a conspiracy. A living one could be questioned in front of the people he'd treated as material.

Charles's voice came softly through the radio.

*Erik.*

“What?”

*Thank you for stopping.*

Erik looked at the men and women who had brought the system down, at his daughter leaning against a wall with her hands shaking, at Kurt trying to stand without showing how badly he hurt, at Rogue holding the evidence high and dry above the water.

“I didn't stop,” he said. “I changed what I was doing.”

Outside, Manhattan was still full of frightened people. There would be injuries to tend, damaged bridges to repair, lies to answer for. The day was not finished because the machine was gone.

For once, that did not sound like a sentence imposed on him.

It sounded like work.

## Chapter Twelve — Names, Not Numbers

The governments called the incident a failure of oversight. The private foundation called it an unauthorized activation. A committee called for an inquiry, and another committee argued over the first committee's authority. The word unprecedented appeared so often in official statements that Charles began crossing it out whenever he saw it in drafts sent to his office.

It was not unprecedented. That was the first thing he wanted the record to reflect.

The Mercy Initiative was suspended. The bands were collected, although not all of them came back. Several governments continued negotiating their own versions of the program. A few officials resigned. Others insisted they had been misled and asked the public to admire their courage in admitting it. The evidence from Essex's drive went to investigators, independent journalists, and families who wanted to know what their children had been subjected to. Charles's lawyers took great care to keep the children's private medical details out of the public release.

The attack had killed three people in Manhattan. The names were read aloud at a memorial the next week: a woman who had tried to pull a stranger's son away from a collapsing barrier, a delivery driver struck by debris, and a construction worker whose team had not been told the crane was part of the system. Dozens more were injured. The figures changed as people were located and their injuries properly documented. Charles refused to describe the operation as a success without saying those names first.

Ororo did not attend the first press conference. She was in Genosha helping organize the relocation of families whose homes had burned. Wakandan medics had arrived before the diplomats could finish deciding which flags to put on their vehicles. The X-Men worked beside local volunteers, and nobody asked the volunteers to wear an emblem before they were trusted with a stretcher.

Erik returned to the church at dusk.

The names on the wall had grown. There was a section for those found alive, each entry marked with a small green line. There was another for those confirmed dead, the handwriting less steady as the list went on. A third section remained for the missing. Nobody had erased a name simply because the list had become inconvenient.

Nia stood before it with her yellow cardigan folded across both arms.

Lorna had found her father's records that morning. He had died at a transit shelter near the harbor. The identification had been made by two survivors who knew him, not by a machine. There was a piece of ordinary proof as well: a photograph from his wallet, showing Nia with a gap between her front teeth, grinning while he held a cake shaped like a fish.

Erik had offered to tell her himself. Lorna had asked whether Nia wanted him to. She did not. She wanted Lorna to do it, and she wanted Erik to be there afterward.

So he waited.

When she finally approached, her eyes were swollen, and she looked as if she had grown older in the space of a morning.

“He's dead,” she said.

“Yes.”

“I kept thinking that if he was missing, maybe he'd come back.”

“I know.”

She gave him a sharp look.

“You don't know.”

He let the correction stand. Then he said, “I know what it is to hope for someone who cannot come back. That's not the same as knowing what it feels like to be you.”

Nia considered that. It was apparently acceptable.

“Did you find the men who did it?”

“Some of them. Not all.”

“Are you going to kill them?”

“I don't know what will be required to keep them from hurting anyone else. I won't promise you something I haven't decided.”

She studied the broken windows of the church.

“Good,” she said after a moment. “People keep promising things they can't do.”

He had heard enough of that wisdom to know he was not entitled to be proud of it. A child should not have needed to learn it so young.

She reached into her pocket and brought out her father's photograph.

“Can his name go on the wall?”

“Yes.”

“Will you write it?”

“If you want.”

She gave him the chalk. He wrote her father's name in the section for the dead. Nia inspected the letters carefully, corrected the way he had formed one of them, then stepped back.

“That's him,” she said.

Later, Charles found Erik outside the church, where the dismantled metal throne had been repurposed into beams for a new shelter. Workers had a sketch on the ground and were arguing about the placement of the doors. Erik stood among them without his helmet, carrying a heavy joint in one hand. He did not appear to be in charge.

“You're building,” Charles said.

“People need somewhere to sleep.”

“Do they agree on the design?”

“Almost nothing.”

Charles smiled. “A promising institution.”

Erik set the metal down.

“What happens to Essex?”

“His records are being examined. His lawyers have already begun explaining why none of it means what it says. He may have other bodies, other laboratories, other names.”

“Then we're not finished.”

“No.”

“Good.”

Charles looked at him with something like exasperation. “Good?”

“There's a difference between refusing to be finished and refusing to rest.”

“I'm pleased to discover you've learned the distinction.”

“I didn't say I was good at it.”

The sky over Genosha was clearing for the first time since the attack. People moved beneath it carrying water, lumber, sheets, and books. There was no victory parade. There were no speeches. A woman argued with a contractor about the height of the steps to a new school. The contractor listened and changed the drawing.

Charles watched them. “We keep asking the world to see us as human beings,” he said. “Perhaps we should spend more time showing what happens when we let people govern their own lives.”

“That's not a reason to lay down our defenses.”

“I didn't say it was.”

Erik looked at his old friend. There were still wounds between them, old promises neither could retract, and disagreements that would remain even when the buildings were rebuilt. He knew Charles would fight him again if he believed Erik had gone too far. He knew he would fight Charles if the professor mistook a polite hearing for a guarantee of safety.

But this time they had stood together without becoming the same man.

“That will have to be enough,” Erik said.

“For today,” Charles replied.

They sat without speaking for a long time. Not because they had nothing left to argue about. Because the people working around them did not need two old men to fill every silence.

## Epilogue — The Window

Three months later, Nia asked for curtains.

The new school had been built beside the church, where the broken stone remained as a memorial. It was not large. Its walls had been made from the steel of ruined Sentinels, reshaped into beams and panels, with wide windows that overlooked the harbor. Every classroom had an exit children could open from the inside. The doors locked only when the people inside chose to lock them.

Erik had spent more hours than he intended helping with the construction. He was no longer invited to make decisions about the classrooms. The teachers had learned that a man who could lift a bridge was not necessarily qualified to choose the height of a child's desk. He considered this an excellent development.

Nia's room faced the water. She had a bed, a narrow bookshelf, and a small wooden box for her father's photograph. At night the harbor lights shone through the glass and made shapes on the walls.

“I want curtains,” she said. “Blue ones.”

Erik looked toward the window. For a moment he saw his mother's front room in Mississippi: the old table, the pencil stubs, the people who came after dark because a door had been opened to them. Miriam had hated curtains. She had believed they made it too easy for people to pretend they hadn't seen what was happening outside.

“Blue,” he said. “All right.”

He found the fabric with Nia the next day. She chose a deep blue, not unlike the color of her scales, and insisted on a silver thread along the hem. The seamstress made them in an afternoon. Erik fixed a rod over the window and hung the curtains himself.

When he finished, Nia pulled them open. She looked at the harbor. Then she pulled them closed and smiled.

“Now I get to decide,” she said.

“Yes,” Erik replied. “You do.”

She sat on the bed and opened the wooden box. Inside was her father's photograph. Beneath it she kept a list of the things she wanted to remember: his terrible jokes, how he hummed while washing dishes, the way he always pretended to lose when they played cards. She had begun adding new things, too. Lorna had taught her to repair a broken radio. Kurt had taken her to the library. Ororo had shown her how to tell rain from mist before either touched the ground.

“Did your mama like blue?” she asked.

Erik sat in the chair beside her bed. He could see the past without being dragged into it. That was new enough to startle him.

“She liked yellow,” he said. “And she hated it when people put sugar in coffee without asking.”

Nia laughed.

“What was her name?”

“Miriam.”

“Tell me about her.”

He told her about the front room where people came to learn to read. About the old chickens and the tomatoes. About the way his mother could make a stubborn man laugh without surrendering an inch of what mattered. He told her how she stood up to people who had no right to frighten her, and how she was frightened anyway. He told her that bravery was not the absence of fear, and that he had not understood that when he was young.

He did not tell her the whole story of the road. Not yet. There would be time, if she asked. A child's curiosity was not a demand for every terrible thing an adult had survived.

When he finished, Nia looked at the curtains.

“Your mama wanted everybody to see what was happening,” she said.

“Yes.”

“Can I still close them?”

“Of course.”

“Even if something bad happens outside?”

Erik considered the question carefully.

“You can close them when you need to feel safe,” he said. “And when you know someone outside needs help, you can decide how to help. You don't have to watch every terrible thing in order to care about people.”

Nia nodded. She seemed relieved.

“Are you going to stay?”

“For a while.”

“Do you have to go fight somebody?”

“Not tonight.”

She lay back on the bed, holding the photograph to her chest. Erik stayed until her breathing became steady. He could hear the harbor outside, a distant engine, footsteps in the hall. Nothing sounded like a threat that required him to move the world.

Before leaving, he looked at the curtains. He remembered a man in a suit saying that a child's fear could be measured and managed without ever asking the child what she wanted. He remembered a throne made from weapons and an old friend refusing to steal a mind for an easier victory. He remembered the name on his mother's death certificate and the people who had insisted they saw nothing.

He had once believed *never again* was something a man with enough power could guarantee.

He no longer believed that.

He believed in the people who opened doors, the people who told the truth, the people who stayed when it was easier to leave. He believed in enough power to protect a child and enough restraint to let that child decide when to close a curtain.

He pulled the curtains closed because Nia had asked him to.

Then he went outside, where there was work to do.

EOF

from PIL import Image, ImageDraw, ImageFont, ImageFilter
from pathlib import Path
import random, math
P=Path('/mnt/data/earth_gk_never_again'); W,H=1400,2100
random.seed(19)
im=Image.new('RGB',(W,H)); px=im.load()
for y in range(H):
    q=y/H
    for x in range(W):
        r=((x-W*.5)/W)**2+((y-H*.51)/H)**2
        glow=max(0,1-4*r)
        n=random.randrange(-2,3)
        px[x,y]=(max(0,int(11+18*glow+n)),max(0,int(16+12*glow+n)),max(0,int(24+12*glow+n)))
d=ImageDraw.Draw(im,'RGBA')

for inset, alpha in [(63,68),(69,20)]:
    d.rectangle([inset,inset,W-inset,H-inset],outline=(209,179,135,alpha),width=2)
# horizontal rule brackets
d.line([(142,214),(1258,214)],fill=(202,172,127,110),width=2)
d.line([(142,1645),(1258,1645)],fill=(202,172,127,110),width=2)
# field lines, asymmetric distorted loops
cx,cy=705,1180
for j in range(15):
    radius=245+j*24
    coords=[]
    for t in range(450):
        a=t*2*math.pi/449
        mod=1+.09*math.sin(a*3+j*.42)
        rx=radius*mod*1.37
        ry=radius*.53*(1+.06*math.cos(a*4))
        coords.append((cx+rx*math.cos(a)+28*math.sin(a*2),cy+ry*math.sin(a)))
    col=(139,160,173,max(12,95-j*6)) if j%3 else (210,166,118,max(12,85-j*4))
    d.line(coords,fill=col,width=2 if j%4 else 3,joint='curve')
# metal shards in orbital motion
for j in range(31):
    a=(j*2.39996+0.7)
    rr=175+17*(j%13)
    x=cx+rr*math.cos(a)*1.35; y=cy+rr*math.sin(a)*.55
    ln=16+9*(j%5); ang=a+math.pi/2
    d.line([(x-ln*math.cos(ang),y-ln*math.sin(ang)),(x+ln*math.cos(ang),y+ln*math.sin(ang))],fill=(195,202,208,95),width=3)
# red divided diamond -- emblem abstract rather than official logo
s=77
d.polygon([(cx,cy-s),(cx+s,cy),(cx,cy+s),(cx-s,cy)],fill=(128,35,46,155),outline=(232,119,103,155))
d.line([(cx,cy-s+8),(cx,cy+s-8)],fill=(243,197,150,195),width=3)
d.line([(cx-s+8,cy),(cx+s-8,cy)],fill=(243,197,150,195),width=3)

def centered(text,y,font,fill,spacing=0):
    bbox=d.textbbox((0,0),text,font=font,stroke_width=0)
    x=(W-(bbox[2]-bbox[0]))//2
    d.text((x,y),text,font=font,fill=fill)

[323] assistant (text, 2393 chars)
## Earth-GK: Never Again

*An alternate-universe Magneto and Professor Xavier novel, conceived for Denzel Washington and Colman Domingo.*

The story opens in the ruined Genoshan church from your trailer. Magneto is holding a frightened mutant girl. He's powerful enough to tear a Sentinel apart, but he cannot tell her whether her father is alive.

Then the story returns to Mississippi, 1959, to Miriam Lehnsherr—a Black Jewish mother teaching people to read and helping them register to vote in a house with no curtains.

She tells her son:

> “A curtain lets people pretend they didn't see.”

That becomes the dividing line between Erik and Charles. Both know what persecution looks like. Both are determined to protect mutant children. But they disagree profoundly about what power entitles a protector to do.

And Nathaniel Essex has every intention of exploiting that disagreement.

<row align="center" gap={3}>
  <icon name="book-open" size="2xl" color="secondary"/>

I also worked your **“Lynchsher”** idea into the historical narrative: the county clerk deliberately misspells Miriam Lehnsherr's surname on her death certificate. It's a small act of bureaucratic cruelty following an enormous one—and something Erik never forgets.

The book gives Storm, Rogue, Nightcrawler, Lorna Dane, and Mister Sinister consequential roles. Xavier isn't there merely to lecture Magneto about restraint. Both men have to make difficult choices, and neither gets to erase the consequences.

My favorite new line comes when Erik recognizes what he's becoming:

> *A throne was a chair made to prevent the occupant from hearing no.*

It's an unofficial Earth-GK novella, not a claim of actual Marvel casting.

And this time, the story goes somewhere your trailer couldn't: **what happens after Magneto makes the promise—and discovers what keeping it really requires.**
